The Coscuin ChroniclesR. A. Lafferty

Annals of the Green Revolution

A Coscuin Companion

An alphabetical guide to persons, places, and ideas across the four books

ABCD EFGH IJKL MNOP QRST UVWY Z§⁂
The Flame Is Green
Half a Sky
Sardinian Summer
First and Last Island
Multiple Works

A

Aasel Castle

Fortress on Bantry Bay, Ireland; a locus of demonic power. Originally Gabriel Castle, the name corrupted to Aasel—a fall from grace into possession by an "arch-devil." Ifreann Chortovitch and his forces hold it. The castle has a "weak connection" to the metaphysical underwater City Third Babylon, making it a terrestrial outpost for infernal powers.

Abadesa (Queen's Abadesa)

Political appointee in the new Spanish government; corruption and worldliness of the post-Carlist regime made flesh. A "new Queen's Abbess"—physically immodest, spiritually vacant, a "loose woman of no religion" holding her position through political favor and material wealth. Appears at Elena Prado's dinner, introducing the topic of Muerte de Boscaje. Participates in the sham wedding to entrap Dana, physically pinioning him before being killed by a pistol shot meant for him. Sacred institutions subverted for secular, decadent ends.

Abbess

Not a spiritual station but a corrupted political appointment under the new Spanish Queen's government. The abadesa at Elena Prado's dinner is a "new Queen's Abbess"—aligned with secular, anti-traditional power. Her irreligious, worldly demeanor: inversion of sacred roles, decay of institutional integrity. A recurring theme in the series' depiction of the modern world's spiritual crisis.

Aberdeen, Earl of [Historical]

Biographical: George Hamilton-Gordon, 4th Earl of Aberdeen (1784–1860). British statesman (Tory/Peelite), Prime Minister 1852–January 1855, forced to resign over his management of the Crimean War.

Connection: Mentioned as the Prime Minister whom the fictional Lord Kingsberry, a man of superior intellect, ought to replace. Existing, implicitly inadequate political leadership. His Crimean War role anchors the timeline.

Abomination

Normalization and societal acceptance of evil. Distinguished from ordinary sin, which may be "done but not condoned." The "abomination party" in Naples and ideologies like Ifreann's: evil becomes celebrated and foundational. Linked to the "diabolical mentality" and the "unjust man." Its triumph marks collective spiritual decay where fundamental moral distinctions are deliberately effaced.

Absolved

Spiritual state contingent upon genuine contrition, not mere ritual confession. When Dana attempts to confess killing a man, the Basse-Terre priest refuses absolution because Dana admits lacking remorse, still feeling the "willfulness" and excitement. Absolution cannot be granted until this internal state changes. Grace requires authentic consent and transformation of the will, not procedural observance.

Achilles

Classical archetype for the hero whose identity is fluid and vulnerabilities subject to supernatural re-inscription. Dana is identified as "Achilles hidden among women"—the myth of Achilles on Skyros disguised as Pyrrha. Complicates Dana's heroic identity, suggesting a concealed or androgynous element. The "Achilles' thumb" is a magical malediction transferred to the False Dana (O'Boyle), parodying the traditional heel and showing how mythic attributes are actively manipulated within the series's magical economy.

Action/Commitment

Central, often paradoxical, imperative for agents of the "Green Revolution." Not free choice but spiritual and historical necessity—a "calling" from higher authority (Count Cyril, God) that characters must fulfill despite incomplete understanding. Committed action rooted in fundamental, intuitive moral orientation, contrasted with the nihilistic destructiveness of the "Red Revolution" and the intellectualizing paralysis of uninvolved observers. Commitment as a test of faith and identity, forcing protagonists to navigate moral ambiguity where consequences are vast and spiritual failure constant.

Acton, Lord [Historical]

Biographical: John Emerich Edward Dalberg-Acton, 1st Baron Acton (1834–1902). English Catholic historian, politician, and writer. Liberal Catholic views, opposed papal infallibility. Famous for: "Power tends to corrupt, and absolute power corrupts absolutely."

Connection: Named by the spider-keeper Ashley as one of the symbolic spiders representing prominent figures ("There is Lord Acton in England"). Places the series' intellectual concerns within major 19th-century debates on liberalism, religion, and institutional power.

Adam

Archetype of unfallen humanity with prelapsarian intellectual and spiritual clarity. General Sabatelli describes Lord Joseph Peter Barbary as having "the direct and illuminated mind such as Adam had before the Fall"—innate innocence and wisdom lost to the modern world. Within the series' framework of historical and spiritual decline, Adam is a primordial ideal of integrated being, direct communion with truth that the complex, conflicted characters can only perceive in rare individuals.

Adam's apple

Metaphor for the intersection of speech, fear, and physical vulnerability. During a confrontation with Mariella Cima, Dana must choose words carefully to avoid bobbling his "Adam's apple... into the relentless knife." A single misstep—verbal or physical—can prove fatal. The precariousness of the protagonists' existence, where survival depends on perfect control over body and word.

Adventure

Mode of existence for the protagonists—spiritual and metaphysical quest, not mere sequence of events. The "death adventure" equated with the perilous sea voyage around the Horn, both defined by binary outcomes: "salvation for the salty elect, or destruction forever." For characters like Dana, adventure is the "Green Gamble" for which they were born, a process of confronting demonic forces, navigating radical uncertainty and illusion, and participating in the chaotic re-creation of reality itself.

Aegean Sea

Major arm of the Mediterranean Sea, located between the Greek and Anatolian peninsulas. In the mid-19th century, a region of strategic importance marking the maritime frontier of the Ottoman Empire. European powers tolerated Ottoman control because it prevented the Russian "Bear" from gaining access to the Aegean and threatening the Mediterranean.

Africa

During the mid-19th-century setting, much of Africa remained under indigenous control, though European colonial encroachment was intensifying. A source of primal magic, ancient traditions, and resilient peoples. Homeland of Damisa the Leopard and the Haussa, who possess deep spiritual knowledge, and origin of the formidable Chasseurs d'Afrique. A world of potent, non-European power and wisdom, a reservoir of "unnatural animals" and supernatural forces outside the rationalized framework of the West.

After death

Fluid and permeable boundary between life and death. The dead remain active and influential: capable of communication (Count Cyril, Catherine Dembinska), physical manifestation (Ifreann), and continued development (Kemper Gruenland's Testament). The afterlife, particularly Purgatory, is a "Second Kingdom" with its own society and language ("salt-water Italian"). Existence as a continuous state in which the "Company" of spiritual combatants acts across the veil of death.

Afterlife/Spirituality

Immanent, operative force; the afterlife is a continuous, interactive dimension of reality. The narrative universe is populated by a spectrum of spiritual beings—angels, devils, ghosts, elemental forces—who directly engage in human affairs. The protagonists regularly communicate with the dead, who act as guides, informants, and continued members of their "Company." A cosmos of "concentric bodies and bodies-public" where the spiritual and physical realms are inextricably and often violently intertwined, and the primary conflicts are metaphysical in nature.

Agency/Historical Change

History as a malleable narrative, actively and consciously contested by opposing metaphysical forces. Historical change driven not by impersonal trends but by the deliberate actions of a small number of agents—the "Company" of the Green Revolution and their demonic counterparts. These figures operate to "alter history before it happens, while it is happening, and even after it has happened." Agency as the capacity to intervene in the fabric of reality, to create or destroy historical "dualities." History radically subjectivized, presented as the outcome of a direct and ongoing spiritual war.

Agrarianism

Core value of the "conservative, monarchial, religious, agrarian, traditional" Carlist Revolution—a rooted, organic social order in opposition to abstract modern ideologies. Extends to the "Green Revolution," which uses the metaphor of the world as a garden to be cultivated against the "red blight" of its enemies. A connection to the land and a source of authentic, chthonic power, embodied by figures like Angelene Domdaniel who receives her wisdom "out of the Earth." Decentralized, localist worldview against the centralizing, urban-centric forces of the "Red" revolution.

Akkerman

Historic fortress city on the Dniester estuary of the Black Sea (now Bilhorod-Dnistrovskyi, Ukraine). During the Crimean War era of the mid-1850s, a strategic port within the Russian Empire. Identified as the shipyard where a new class of twelve small, revolutionary warships is under construction, one designated for Ifreann Chortovitch. Grounds the supernatural conflict in the concrete geopolitical realities of the Black Sea theater.

Alaric [Historical]

Biographical: Alaric I (c. 370–410), King of the Visigoths, famous for the 410 sack of Rome, a key event in the collapse of the Western Roman Empire.

Connection: The series presents a counter-historical myth in which Alaric, after sacking Rome, attempts to transport 300 of the finest Imperial stallions to Sicily on Irish ships. The "treacherous" Irish captains divert the ships to Ireland, thereby establishing the foundation of its superior horse-racing stock.

Albani, Monte (Priest-Music-Master)

Italian Salesian priest, musician, and follower of St. John Bosco. Appears at Joseph O'Boyle's inn in Skibbereen, where he secretly ministers to the community and teaches the boys. His presence incites the hostility of the local Anglo-Irish gentry, making him a fugitive. Dana arranges his escape from Ireland aboard the Piccola Spinta. The gentle, cultured, and resilient aspect of the "Green Revolution," a force for spiritual and artistic good operating under constant threat in a hostile world.

Albert, King Charles (of Sardinia) [Historical]

Biographical: Carlo Alberto of Savoy (1798–1849), King of Sardinia-Piedmont from 1831 to 1849. A complex and vacillating ruler, he granted a liberal constitution (the Statuto Albertino) in 1848 but was defeated by Austria in the First Italian War of Independence, abdicating in 1849.

Connection: Appears shortly before his historical death, speaking "straight King's truth" in the twilight of his life. Articulates a central theme: that human rulers are puppets of a higher, inscrutable "compulsion" and lack true agency. Laments his own historical role and presciently foresees a future of diminished monarchical power. A deeply pessimistic, metaphysical view of political power.

Alcoran

Archaic term for the Quran, the sacred text of Islam. Cited by Ifreann Chortovitch, who claims it supports his theory of subjective time in Hell. Ifreann's use of this slightly obscure term reflects his grandiose and syncretic diabolism, wherein he appropriates concepts from various world religions to construct his own perverse theology. A being of broad, if malevolent, erudition.

Alma

River in the Crimean Peninsula, site of the first major battle of the Crimean War on September 20, 1854. The Allied (British, French, Ottoman) victory over the Russians, though costly, opened the way for the siege of Sevastopol. Referenced as an early, somewhat confused engagement where the Allies had better intelligence than at later encounters, contrasting it with the more savage and elemental battle of Inkerman.

Almighty God

Source of goodness and metaphysical order, engaged in a perpetual and often indirect conflict with the Devil. Portrayed as an active but enigmatic force whose methods are frequently inscrutable to mortals. King Charles Albert characterizes Him as "unaccountable, is unreasonable," while other characters see His hand in miracles and fortunate coincidences. Acts through flawed human agents and allows for a world of radical freedom where evil is a potent force. The "Green Revolution" is implicitly His cause, but its victory is never guaranteed and its path is fraught with paradox and suffering.

Alsace

Historical region in northeastern France, bordering Germany. Characterized by a blend of French and Germanic cultures, part of France during the mid-19th-century setting. August Olt of Alsace named as one of the disparate intellectual forerunners of the "Green Revolution," underscoring the movement's decentralized and pan-European character.

Ambato

City in the central Andean highlands of Ecuador. In the 1850s, a significant commercial hub on the mountainous route between the coast and Quito. A place of intense intellectual and spiritual "explosion," where travelers are ambushed by profound "insights and revelations." A geographical locus for transformative, often disorienting, spiritual experience.

America

The United States of the mid-19th century: a nation of dynamic expansion, internal contradiction, and nascent global influence. A destination for Irish famine emigrants, a source of hardened gold-seekers like Otis Ranker, and a theater for the political machinations that culminate in the (counter-historical) presidency of Crimsi Hunter. The series also offers a mythic etymology, deriving the name from Irish, Greek, and Latin roots meaning "vision," "unlucky land," and "bitter land," thereby layering its historical reality with a deeper, more fateful identity. A key arena for the conflict between the Green and Red Revolutions.

Amharc (a vision)

Irish word for "a sight or a vision," presented by the Black Pope as one of the ancient, true names for the American continent. Part of the series's project of supplanting accepted history with a deeper, mythic narrative. Frames America not as a product of European discovery but as a place of innate prophetic and spiritual significance, linked to the visionary capacities of the ancient Irish.

An gluin (knee-high cousins)

Small folk who are blood kindred of Irish families, resembling the families in every way except for their diminutive stature—even the tallest will hardly stand knee-high to an average human. The an gluin cousins usually live in the low lofts of family homes, up under the thatch, or in spaces beneath houses reached by small trap-doors. Some live out in the cabbage patches. Their existence is a half-open secret, especially in the West of Ireland, though families are reluctant to admit having small cousins as it makes them seem "a little bit second class." The an gluin are counted among the Irish "indecencies"—peculiarities that explain why Irish cottages are built far apart and why visitors always call out "Halloo, Halloo!" when approaching a house.

Anarchists

Political faction identified in Montevideo by their black shirts. Their presence: the influx of radical European ideologies into the new world. Within the series' symbolic framework, agents of the "Red Revolution," contributing to the chaos and "babble" of the city. A modern political expression of a nihilistic, anti-order impulse in direct opposition to the creative, organic vision of the Green Revolution.

Anascaul

Village on the Dingle Peninsula in County Kerry, Ireland. Situated within the region of Dingle Bay, part of the "Back Doors of the World." In the mid-19th century, a remote, Gaelic-speaking community, representative of the deep-rooted, traditional Ireland from which Dana emerges.

Anatropic wound

Wound "of the spirit or soul" that disqualifies a Lion from command of Gate Castle. The term, likely a neologism from the Greek ana- (against) and tropos (turn), suggests a spiritual inversion or self-betrayal. Leadership in the series's central conflict is contingent upon spiritual integrity, not physical prowess. A fall from this state results not from external defeat but from internal moral or spiritual collapse.

Ancestors

Active spiritual presences who can interact with the living. Ifreann Chortovitch claims he can compel the skeleton of "Old Dana" to speak and reveal secrets. A worldview where the past is not inert but a dynamic force, and the dead remain participants in the affairs of their descendants. Ancestry is not merely biological lineage but a spiritual connection that transcends the barrier of death.

Angel

Functional rather than essential designation, meaning "one who is sent" (angelos). Both divine and demonic forces employ angels as messengers and agents. An Indian woman in Bolivia identifies Dana and his companions as angels because they are on a mission, regardless of their personal flaws. Conversely, Ercule Tirana refers to the meddling foreign operatives in Latin America as "angels of the Devil." A role of agency within the cosmic war, stripped of purely benevolent connotations and tied to the act of being dispatched by a higher power, for good or ill.

Angelus

Catholic devotion commemorating the Incarnation, marked by the ringing of a bell. Draws a sharp contrast between the two rival castles on St. Peter's Island. Thorn Castle's bells ring the Angelus at the traditional times of noon and dusk, alignment with sacred order and divine rhythm. In contrast, the bells of the demonic Gate Castle ring at "irregular and devilish hours." The Angelus as an auditory symbol of the legitimate, ordered spirituality of the "Green" cause.

Anierocracy

Neologism coined by Ifreann Chortovitch for the hierarchy of Hell, likely from the Greek anieros (unholy) as a pun on "hierarchy." Within this structure, he defines the relative ranks of himself, his father the Devil, and his uncle the Lord of the Flies. Characteristic satanic parody, creating a systematic, bureaucratic order for the forces of evil that mirrors and mocks the divine order.

Anselm

Likely alluding to St. Anselm of Canterbury, cited as a theological counterpoint to the theories of "Yosef Paradosis." While Paradosis argues that the "Seduction of the Saints" is the sole theme of high art, Anselm is said to contradict this by positing the existence of a "higher art" free from this fallen theme. Briefly places the series' aesthetic debates within the broader context of Christian scholastic philosophy, representing a view that transcends the purely terrestrial and fallen drama.

Antarctic

Earth's southernmost continent. In the mid-19th century, an almost entirely unknown landmass, a symbol of the ultimate terrestrial frontier. Referenced as the source of the powerful Humboldt Current, with Dana imagining its origins in a "shop under Antarctica itself." A geographical extreme, a source of immense, primal, and mysterious natural power that shapes the world's elemental forces.

Anti-Christ

Embodied in Ifreann Chortovitch. A parodic inversion of Christ, declaring himself the "true Son Incarnate" and a "caricature and blasphemy who exceeds the original in every way." He boasts of his own set of "thirteen devil wounds" and reenacts a blasphemous nativity. Traditional understanding of the Antichrist as a being who mimics sacred forms to advance a demonic agenda, representing not merely opposition but a fundamental, perverse imitation of the divine.

Anti-sin

Paradoxical transgression committed by a demonic being. When Ifreann Chortovitch acts to save Carolina Tirana from a collapsing roof, his action is a "horrifying anti-sin," for which he earns a "bitter a banishment." For a creature whose essence is evil, an act of genuine good is a violation of its own nature, a "fall" from its own infernal grace. Inversion of morality underscoring the series's fascination with paradox and the rigid, though inverted, logic that governs the demonic realm.

Antitheos

Greek term ("against/equal to God") used as an exclamation by the spider-keeper Ashley. Its use, along with the related Enantitheos ("opposite god"), suggests a dualistic, Gnostic worldview in which history is a struggle between opposing divine principles. Frames the series' central conflict in Manichaean terms, reflecting a theological landscape more complex and heretical than simple Christian orthodoxy.

Apocalypse

Not a single, future eschatological event, but a recurring and ongoing process of destruction and renewal. The narrative is punctuated by localized apocalypses, such as the sea battle off Basse-Terre where "the fountains of the deep were broken up." The world is in a constant state of "unraveling," and history itself is presented as an apocalyptic struggle between the "Green" and "Red" revolutions. The protagonists' actions as interventions in a perpetual cycle of cosmic death and rebirth.

Apostle

Title conferred upon the twelve boys living at Joseph O'Boyle's inn. Legally adopted by Charlotta O'Boyle in a document naming them "The Apostles" and "The Sons of Thunder," reconstituted as a new holy company. Their role: to be the next generation of agents for the "Green Revolution" in Ireland. Appropriation of this sacred title for a group of ragged boys signifies the series' theme of creating a new, localized, and folk-based sacred history in opposition to decaying formal institutions.

Apostles, The

Twelve original disciples of Christ. Invoked in an anecdote by the Cardinal-Archbishop of Naples, who jests that in Paradise, only the chief Apostles—Peter, Andrew, James, and John—receive the finest "Neapolitan Prisoner" cigars from God. Humorous aside using the Apostles to illustrate a celestial hierarchy, reflecting the series' tendency to blend the sacred with the profane and to treat even the most revered figures with an earthy familiarity.

Apparition castles

Mythical castles on Ireland's south coast said to possess a spectral, intermittent reality, sometimes physically present and at other times flying to Spain. A reality where the boundary between the material and the immaterial is fluid. This quality extends beyond architecture to characters and events throughout the series, which often exist in a state of "unreality" or as tangible "projections." A key image for the series' porous, magical worldview.

Ararat

Volcanic massif in modern Turkey, the biblical resting place of Noah's Ark. The ultimate geographical and mythical benchmark. A Carlist mountain is the highest in the world "except Ararat, and it is only one cubit less high." Situates the series' fictional events within a landscape of primary, world-historical myth, grounding the seemingly localized struggles of the Carlists in a context of biblical and elemental significance.

Arch-devil

High-ranking demon. Aasel Castle is said to have changed its name from that of an archangel to that of an "arch-devil." A structured infernal hierarchy, with arch-devils acting as major commanders in the cosmic war, responsible for significant strategic operations against the forces of good.

Archbishop

Represented by two contrasting figures: Monsignor Affre of Paris, a historical player among the "One Hundred Persons," and the fictional Cardinal-Archbishop of Naples, Philip Caracciolo, a worldly political operator. The latter embodies the sophisticated interplay of spiritual authority and secular power, granting permissions and navigating intrigue with urbane wit. Not merely a spiritual leader but a key institutional figure, a gatekeeper whose sanction is necessary for the series' complex plots.

Argentina (La Plata)

In the mid-19th century, the Argentine Confederation, also known as the United Provinces of the Río de la Plata, was dominated by the Federalist dictator Juan Manuel de Rosas. A period of civil war between Rosas's Federalists, who championed provincial autonomy, and the Unitarios, who advocated for a strong central government. Portrayed as the "loins and the entrails" of the South American continent—a place of raw, visceral power and the ideological heartland of a conservative, localist worldview locked in struggle with the centralizing forces of modernity.

Armagedon

Originally the name of Thieves' Town on Basse-Terre, invoking the biblical site of the final cosmic battle. The location serves as the gathering place for the "dark-side ecumenical gathering," a global conference of all demonic and revolutionary forces. Both a specific geographical location and a conceptual space where the series' apocalyptic struggle is focused and brought to a head, a microcosm of the worldwide war between the Green and Red Revolutions.

Armagedon Conference

Name for the "fearful meeting" of all diabolical forces at Thieves' Town (Armagedon) on Basse-Terre. Conceived as the "dark-side ecumenical" counterpoint to a Vatican Council, a global summit of "diabolists and opportunists" from every subversive faction, including "active members from Hell itself." The ultimate strategic mobilization of the "Red Revolution," the point at which its disparate elements coalesce for a final, decisive assault on the world order.

Arutinov, Arpad

Author of a fictional historical-philosophical work, The Back Door of History. His "writings" provide a theoretical lens for understanding the series' metaphysical view of history, particularly his dictum that a "Duality in history will break down in about five years." One of the series' internal, quasi-academic authorities, articulating the esoteric principles that govern its narrative universe.

Ashley

Decadent, "Spiderish" man of mixed European heritage who keeps a "spider room" where individual spiders represent and mystically parallel major historical figures (Pius IX, Marx, Cavour, etc.). He believes himself to be one of the world's hidden "sponsors." His entire enterprise is a form of passive, voyeuristic diabolism, observing and cataloging the world's evils without commitment or action. He and his monstrous household serve as a "devil's own way-station," a nexus of evil that Dana and Brume must disrupt. His eventual death off-stage marks the removal of a minor but significant node of corruption.

Atheist

Potent ideological and spiritual position, not a mere absence of belief. A defining characteristic of the modern, destructive forces of the "Red Revolution." The "totally atheistic and totally cruel premise" of the Red Napoleon underpins his mechanistic worldview. The series also explores nuanced forms, such as the friendly atheism of the governor Guerchin. Ultimately, atheism is framed as a spiritual orientation that, in its most virulent form, aligns with the demonic and enables a profound and calculated inhumanity.

Atheistic

Worldview actively hostile to the divine. The series attributes the "glee" of the "atheistic and Masonic English" during the Irish Famine to a belief that the Catholic Irish were "getting what they deserved for worshipping anything." Links atheism to a specific historical cruelty, framing it not as a neutral philosophical stance but as an ideology that can justify and even revel in the suffering of those it deems superstitious. A key intellectual component of the forces opposing the Green Revolution.

Atonement of humanity

Grandiose and perverse rationale adopted by Carolina Tirana for her descent into a demonic state. She frames her suffering and consorting with evil as a form of vicarious sacrifice, a "scapegoat" role necessary for the salvation of others. A deliberate and tragic parody of Christ's atonement. A key spiritual pathology: a romantic, self-aggrandizing embrace of damnation disguised as a noble, redemptive act.

Aunt Hattie

Seven-year-old Indian Elephant living as a member of the Oliphant family on Basse-Terre. A gift from the sea-faring Bruce Oliphant to his wife Celeste, her name domesticates her exotic nature. The harmonious integration of the strange and the mundane that characterizes the "paradise" of Basse-Terre. Her presence is a living piece of family heraldry, connecting the Oliphant name to its literal meaning and embodying the theme of myth made manifest.

Austria

Conservative, multi-ethnic Catholic power and a principal antagonist to the forces of nationalism and liberalism in Europe. One of the three powers occupying Poland and the main obstacle to Italian unification, controlling Lombardy and Venetia. A decaying but still formidable imperial structure, a key player in the complex geopolitics of the Crimean War and the Italian Risorgimento, and an embodiment of the old order against which "revolutions" are defined.

Authority

Legitimate authority is spiritual, mysterious, and delegated from a transcendent source like Count Cyril or God. Unconventional figures often exercise it, contrasting sharply with the hollow, coercive power of formal institutions like the Spanish government or the brute force of demonic agents like Ifreann. The central conflict is a crisis of authority, a struggle to identify and align with the correct, though often hidden, chain of command in a world where false authorities proliferate.

Avatar

Used by Malandrino Brume in a non-Hindu context to describe a potential state of modern man. Brume speculates that man, having lost his rational and spiritual integrity, might become a "stolid animal," a "hysterical ghost," or an "effete avatar." Here, "avatar" signifies a being with a tenuous connection to reality, a mere manifestation or superficial incarnation rather than a substantive person. Anxiety about the fragmentation of identity in the modern world, where inauthentic, hollow roles threaten authentic personhood.

Azazel

Demonic entity from the Book of Leviticus to whom a "scapegoat" bearing the sins of Israel was sent into the wilderness. The name is invoked in the title "Azazel's Lodge" for the chapter describing the destruction of O'Boyle's inn. Frames the event as a ritual of purgation, where the victims are cast as scapegoats for the sins of the community. The dark, primordial power to which society sacrifices its own in acts of violent and misdirected atonement.

Azazel's Lodge

Symbolic name for the site of the destruction of Joseph O'Boyle's inn. Not a formal lodge but a conceptual space where an act of communal violence is performed as a perverse ritual. By linking the attack to the scapegoat for Azazel, the title frames the event as a modern manifestation of an ancient, demonic rite of purgation, highlighting the archaic, ritualistic underpinnings of contemporary political violence.

B

Babylon

Worldly corruption, spiritual confusion, and decadent cosmopolitanism. Employed in three contexts: 1) Theologically, as the "whore of Babylon," the object of worship for which Irish Catholics were perceived to be justly punished by the Famine. 2) Metaphorically, for Montevideo, a city of "polyglottal stews, More babble-ish than Babylon," a modern center of intrigue and exile. 3) Mythically, as "Third Babylon the City in the Sea," a demonic, underwater realm from which Carolina Tirana is rescued and to which the ship The Untimely Encounter is said to sink.

Bahia Blanca

Port city in the province of Buenos Aires, Argentina. During the period (c. 1851), a remote southern outpost of the Argentine Confederation. A port of call where the Catherine Dembinska reprovisions after rounding Cape Horn, before proceeding to Montevideo.

Balaclava

Town and bay on the Crimean Peninsula, south of Sevastopol. During the Crimean War (1853–1856), the main British supply base. The location of the British encampment, the harbor for officers' yachts, and the site of the Battle of Balaclava on October 25, 1854. The battle: a series of confused but heroic engagements, culminating in the Charge of the Light Brigade.

Balaclava Plains

Battlefield adjacent to the town of Balaclava in Crimea. Setting for the October 25, 1854 battle, specifically the heroic stand of Campbell's 93rd Highlanders (the "Thin Red Line") against a Russian cavalry charge and the subsequent Charge of the Heavy Brigade.

Ballycotton

Coastal village in County Cork, Ireland. In the mid-19th century, a small fishing community. The location where Dana's ship captain, Crispin Flume, secretly lands the fugitive priest Monte Albani, allowing him to escape his pursuers.

Balzac, Honoré de [Historical]

Biographical: Honoré de Balzac (1799–1850), French novelist and playwright, a seminal figure of literary realism and author of the sequence La Comédie humaine.

Connection: Dana learns, with some surprise, at a bohemian gathering in Krakow that the "shambling chunky-faced Balzac" is considered "the greatest writer in the world." Situates the intellectual milieu within the mainstream of 19th-century European literary culture.

Banda Oriental (Uruguay)

Literally, the "East Bank" of the Uruguay River, the historical name for the territory that became modern Uruguay. In the mid-19th century, a chaotic theater of civil war between the Blancos (Whites) and Colorados (Reds) and a base for international revolutionaries and exiles opposing the Argentine dictator Juan Manuel de Rosas. A place of "polyglottal stews" and "babble," a focal point of ideological conflicts sweeping the region.

Banishment

State of metaphysical expulsion resulting from a profound violation of one's essential nature. Ifreann Chortovitch earns a "bitter a banishment" for committing a horrifying "anti-sin"—an act of good. For demonic entities, virtue is a transgression punishable by exile from their own infernal order. Inverts traditional morality and underscores the rigid, though perverse, logic of the demonic realm.

Bantry

Town in County Cork, Ireland, located at the head of Bantry Bay. In the mid-19th century, a key local port and commercial center for the surrounding rural area. A focal point within the larger "Bay Country" from which Dana originates.

Bantry Bay

Large, deep bay in County Cork, southwestern Ireland. One of the three "Back Doors of the World," a historically significant and semi-autonomous region with deep cultural and commercial ties to continental Europe, particularly Spain (also called "Spanish Bay"). Dana's homeland, characterized by its superior mariners, unique culture, and symbolic green and gold waters. Dana's point of origin and the source of his innate abilities.

Baptism

Christian sacrament of initiation. Appears both in orthodox form, as with Dana's son Brandon, and in heterodox applications underscoring the series's fluid spirituality. Elaine Kingsberry performs a summary baptism on Mary Margaret McGinty to qualify her for burial in a Catholic cemetery. Dana's ship, the Piccola Spinta, is "baptized" with cannon fire. The ritual signifies not only religious initiation but any act that confers a new, often dangerous, identity or purpose.

Barbary Apes

Not mere animals but a sapient, ancient people capable of complex thought and communication. Their representative, Lord Joseph Peter Barbary, is a key member of the "Company," acting as a source of creaturely wisdom and participating in major historical events, including aerial surveillance in the Crimean War. Non-human intelligence, a form of primordial innocence and perception that offers a critical perspective on human folly.

Barbary Joe (Lord Joseph Peter Barbary)

A Barbary Ape who is also a Lord of England, member of the Ancient Family of Macaque. He arrives at Dana's Seven Heads Castle with Lord and Lady Galopade and becomes fascinated by the sea-lore told by Hiodra the sea monster who dwells on the rocky shore three hundred feet below the castle. Barbary Joe is the only one who understands Hiodra completely, translating the monster's whistles and grunts for human listeners. He is startled by Hiodra's lack of ethics ("If it's on the shore, eat it") and in turn teaches the monster land-ethics and philosophy. Among the Lords of England, he is said to be closest to Lord Galopade and Lord Kingsberry. Emperor Napoleon III, King Victor Emanuele II of Italy, and the Pope all refer to him familiarly as "Barbary Joe." He attempts to help Carolina Tirana recover her missing right eye from the Devils.

Barret, Odilon

Based on the historical French politician Odilon Barrot (1791–1873). A key figure in the 1848 French Revolution, a self-styled "man of the people" who, when designated to form a new government, proves vacillating and reluctant. His actions, such as inciting crowds against a government he has been chosen to lead, epitomize the farcical and contradictory nature of the revolution.

Basque country

Region inhabited by the Basque people, straddling the Pyrenees on the border of France and Spain. In the mid-19th century, a distinct cultural and linguistic area known for its independent spirit. The setting for the Carlist Wars in Spain and the location of the border town of Hendaye, a crucial nexus for intrigues.

Basse-Terre

Capital of the French overseas department of Guadeloupe. Transfigured into a mythical space, a "Terrestrial Paradise" and the "Home" to which Dana is fated to come. An island of seven intermingled bloodlines (Irish, French, Spanish, English, Carib, East Indian, Negro), possessing a unique, syncretic culture and a powerful, immanent spirituality. The site of Greenfields and Porte d'Enfer, the respective estates of Count Cyril and Ifreann, making it a microcosm of the central metaphysical conflict.

Basse-Terre Island

Western island of the Guadeloupe archipelago, on which the town of Basse-Terre is located. A single, potent entity, a living landscape dominated by the volcano Soufriere and characterized by its "high aromatic hills," "tangled heights," and "purple chasms." See also Basse-Terre.

Bay Country

Region of southwestern Ireland encompassing Dingle, Kenmare, and Bantry bays. A distinct cultural zone, a liminal space known as the "Back Doors of the World," historically open to foreign influence and characterized by a spirit of independence and maritime skill. The formative landscape of Dana.

Bear Haven

Local name for Berehaven, a natural harbor on Bere Island in Bantry Bay, County Cork. In the mid-19th century, a significant deep-water anchorage. The location where the Norse ship Skaebne is anchored just off the Castletown landing.

Beatified

Act of declaring a deceased person "Blessed," the penultimate step toward canonization as a saint. The series describes a popular, folk process to have Joseph O'Boyle beatified as a "martyr for the Faith." The creation of a localized, independent hagiography by the "Company," operating parallel to, and sometimes in gentle defiance of, official ecclesiastical authority.

Bedeau, General

Based on the historical French general Marie-Alphonse Bedeau. A commander during the 1848 Revolution in Paris. Characterized as a "great talker," easily persuaded by the rationalist arguments of the businessman Fauvelle-Delabarre to abandon his military objectives, thus contributing to the farcical collapse of government authority. Also named Minister of War in the new provisional government but refuses the post.

Before Belial was, I am

Direct quotation from Ifreann Chortovitch. A blasphemous parody of Christ's declaration of divinity, "Before Abraham was, I am" (John 8:58). By substituting the demon Belial for Abraham, Ifreann asserts his own primordial and demonic pre-existence, framing his identity as a satanic counterpart to the divine.

Beginning

Point of primordial origin imbued with metaphysical significance. The series refers to the "serpent from the beginning," indicating the ancient root of evil. For Catherine Dembinska, "All the final answers were given in the beginning," framing history not as discovery but as recovering a foundational truth. Present conflicts are reenactments of an archetypal struggle originating at the dawn of time.

Belial

Name for a demon or the Devil in Judeo-Christian tradition. Ifreann invokes the name in his blasphemous declaration of pre-existence: "Before Belial was, I am." Positions Belial as an archetypal demonic figure and places Ifreann within that ancient, infernal lineage.

Bells

Bells of Thorn Castle and Gate Castle are sentient, prophetic instruments that participate directly in metaphysical drama. The "bronze bells" of Thorn Castle are aligned with divine order, ringing the Angelus and tolling a "primary dirge" for the "Fallen Lion" (Dana), reporting his sin to God. Gate Castle's bells ring at "devilish hours" and announce the fall of Ifreann. An auditory manifestation of the spiritual state of the world, voicing judgment and marking pivotal events in the cosmic struggle.

Benedictine Convent

Religious house located in St. Malo, Brittany. A temporary place of retreat for Carolina Tirana. However, not a space of perfect sanctuary; from here Carolina engages with the outside world, carving her prophetic statues and ultimately rejoining the central conflict. A traditional institution that is porous to the strange energies of the world.

Bentinck, General [Historical]

Biographical: Sir Henry Bentinck (1796–1878), a British general who commanded the Guards Brigade at the Battle of the Alma and the Battle of Inkerman during the Crimean War.

Connection: Appears as a participant in the discussion on a yacht after the Battle of Balaclava. Voices a grimly prescient view of modern warfare, predicting that future conflicts will become "very large and completely hellish" and that people will call for "rich old men" to fight them, forgetting that they always have.

Bermuda

British island territory in the North Atlantic. In the mid-19th century, already associated with maritime mysteries. The location of Ifreann's demonic ship, the Brami Piekielne or Gates of Hell, in the "Bermuda waters," using the location's reputation to enhance the vessel's supernatural and threatening character.

Betrayal

Pivotal and morally complex engine of historical change. Acts on multiple levels: political betrayal, such as Urquiza's calculated turning against his ally Rosas, which Dana orchestrates as a strategic necessity; and spiritual betrayal, as in Dana's violation of his vows as a Holy Lion. Not simple villainy but a paradoxical, often unavoidable, element in the metaphysical war, a tool that both divine and demonic forces can use to achieve their ends.

Bianco

Italian word for "white," used to denote the political faction of the Blancos (Whites) led by Juan Manuel de Rosas in the civil wars of the La Plata region. The conservative, Federalist, and localist ideology, standing in direct opposition to the centralizing, revolutionary Colorados (Reds).

Bird of Paradise

Rural neighborhood on Basse-Terre, named for the local Brainnan's Motmot. The idyllic community where the core families of the "Green Revolution" on the island (the Cosquins, Oliphants, Lancels, etc.) build their homes. A terrestrial paradise, a sanctuary of multicultural harmony and vitality in direct opposition to the corrupting influence of the nearby Thieves' Town.

Bishop

Role susceptible to the political and spiritual corruption of the age. The "new Queen's Bishop" in Spain is a sycophantic, irreligious man, a symbol of the decay of sacred institutions under a secularizing regime. In contrast, the historical Pope Pius IX, though flawed, is the authentic spiritual gravity and historical weight of the office. A figure whose authority and integrity are contested within the central conflict.

Bismarck, Otto von [Historical]

Biographical: Otto von Bismarck (1815–1898), the Prussian statesman who masterminded the unification of Germany and served as its first chancellor from 1871 to 1890.

Connection: Represented as one of the spiders in Ashley's spider-room, the rising power of Prussia. Later, his historical plan for German unification is referenced as having been appropriated by the revolutionary Ferdinand Lassalle, who intends to use Bismarck's strategy for the more sinister purpose of imposing the "Terror." Potent but morally ambiguous statecraft that demonic forces can co-opt.

Black Pope, The

Old priest and leader of the Carlist irregulars in Spain. A primary intellectual and spiritual guide for the "Green Revolution." His teachings, a blend of orthodox theology and esoteric counter-history, provide the metaphysical framework for the struggle against the "Red Blight." His title, a historical nickname for the Superior General of the Jesuits, is here applied to a figure representing a more ancient, militant, and localized form of spiritual authority, independent of formal Church structures.

Black Sea

Large inland sea between Europe and Asia. In the mid-19th century, the central theater of the Crimean War, dominated by the Russian naval base at Sevastopol. Access to the Mediterranean through the Turkish-controlled Straits was a key Russian objective. A major setting for the historical conflict, where Dana and Ifreann are to engage in a fated naval battle.

Black Thief Cove

Cove on Bantry Bay, Ireland, the departure point for Dana's journey into the wider world. Where the Norse ship Skaebne is anchored and where Dana parts from his cousin Aileen after their climactic "moon-madness." A liminal space, a gateway from the local and familiar to the global and adventurous.

Black-Maned Lion

Demonic counterpart to the holy "Golden-Maned Lion." Following Dana's fall from grace as the Lion of Thorn Castle, a "Black-Maned Lion" is simultaneously deposed at the demonic Gate Castle. A dualistic cosmology where actions in the realm of good have immediate and parallel consequences in the realm of evil, and where key spiritual roles have demonic antitypes.

Blanc, Louis [Historical]

Biographical: Louis Blanc (1811–1882), a French politician, historian, and socialist. A member of the provisional government after the 1848 Revolution and a proponent of "social workshops" (ateliers sociaux) to guarantee employment.

Connection: Named as one of the "One Hundred Persons" of Paris whose "dream of networks of cooperatives" made him a key intellectual force in the revolutionary ferment of 1848. The utopian socialist strain within the broader revolutionary movement.

Blaspheme

Sacrilege that perverts divine truth. The Black Pope identifies blasphemy as the core tactic of the "obstructing blight," the Red Revolution. Elena Prado is accused of blasphemy when she makes profane claims about Christ. Not merely verbal insult but a fundamental inversion of sacred order, a key weapon in the demonic assault on reality.

Blasphemous

Quality of being sacrilegious or in profound opposition to divine truth. Ifreann's claims to be a new incarnation, a "caricature" who "exceeds the original in every way," are defined as blasphemous. Characterizes the ideology of the Red Revolution, which seeks not merely to deny the sacred but to create a perverse and demonic imitation of it.

Blaye, Christian

Martyr of the Green Revolution, husband of Jane Blaye. Known primarily through his skull, preserved by his widow in an "angry shrine" in Hendaye and a powerful oracle. The skull communicates with Dana, offering "deep sanity," historical perspective, and strategic advice. Though murdered, he remains a potent spiritual guide, the enduring wisdom and incorruptible truth of the "Green" cause. Also identified by Malandrino Brume as an early formulator of the Green Revolution's tenets.

Blaye, Jane

Widow of Christian Blaye and keeper of his oracular skull. A central figure in the "Green Revolution," operating a "sanity-house" in Hendaye that serves as a refuge and information hub. A powerful, earthy woman with deep spiritual insight, she provides Dana with crucial aid and cryptic warnings. Mother of Sainte Erma; embodies a practical, resilient, and life-affirming spirituality in the face of demonic chaos.

Bless you

Used by Carolina Tirana. Within the heightened spiritual context of the narrative, this common benediction is presented as a literal conferral of grace, an active intervention in the spiritual well-being of another, rather than a mere social pleasantry.

Blessed

State of being in grace or under divine favor. Applied to the ultimate goal of the Green Revolution—a "blessed" world—and to its martyrs, such as Joseph O'Boyle. Not just goodness but a state of spiritual potency and right alignment with the cosmic order, the antithesis of the "cursed" state of their adversaries.

Blessed fire

Purifying, righteous conflict that aims at renewal rather than destruction. Contrasted with the profane fires of the Red Revolution. The ideal of a holy war, a violent but necessary action undertaken to cleanse the world of a greater evil and restore it to a state of "green" growth.

Blessed Joseph O'Boyle

Posthumous honorific given to Joseph O'Boyle after his martyrdom in Ireland. This informal beatification by the members of the "Company" is the creation of a private, localized hagiography. Elevates a flawed but redeemed hero to the status of a saint within their own spiritual economy, independent of and parallel to the official processes of the Church.

Blessed remains

Physical relics of a person revered as holy. The body of Captain Centennial Jones is referred to as "blessed remains" to be delivered to his family. Bestows a quasi-sacred status on those who serve the "Green" cause, framing their deaths as martyrdoms and their bodies as objects of veneration.

Blessed Sacrament

Consecrated Eucharist in Catholic theology. A tangible manifestation of divine power and protection. Carolina Tirana brings the Blessed Sacrament in a pyx to the house on Basse-Terre, and Father Manzoni administers it to Tom Cosquin just before he is killed by a demonic attack. Its presence is the ultimate spiritual defense in a moment of extreme metaphysical peril.

Blessed Virgin (Mary)

Mother of Christ. Appears as a powerful, militant figure in the series' symbolic landscape. The peasant statue of her in Jane Blaye's shrine depicts her crushing the serpent with "murderous glee," embodying a fierce and triumphant aspect of the divine feminine. Also invoked frequently in prayer by the protagonists, serving as a primary intercessor and source of spiritual strength.

Blessing

Confers divine grace and protection. Damisa the Leopard gives a "blessing" to the house of Dana and Angelene. Not merely a formulaic wish but an effective spiritual act that can alter the metaphysical state of a person or place.

Blind Woman, The

Prophetic figure in Hendaye who gives Dana the correct, cryptic instruction not to take the direct road to Pamplona. Her authentic warning is immediately contrasted with the deceptive message of a "false Blind Woman," an agent of the enemy. The two figures embody the theme of discernment, forcing the protagonist to distinguish between true and false spiritual guidance in a world saturated with illusion.

Blindness/Ignorance

Recurring motif signifying both physical disability and spiritual or intellectual deficiency. Jude Revanche's physical blinding by Dana is a consequence of his moral state. Ifreann inflicts temporary, metaphysical blindness on Dana as a form of psychic attack. Ignorance is treated with ambiguity: the Condor suggests it may be a "requirement for being a hero," while characters like Ifreann are crippled by their ignorance of natural and moral law. Probes the relationship between sight, insight, and the ability to navigate a deceptive world.

Blow-Hole

Nickname given to Jack Galopade by a yellow-haired Dutch street girl in Amsterdam, paired with Dana's nickname "Straw-Hair." The names refer to the two survivors' emergence from their strange ocean consciousness after the sea battle between the Catherine Dembinska and the Gates of Hell. "Straw-Hair and Blow-Hole . . . wake up! You are walking in the sleeps. It is the end of the sea monsters for you now and the return to the people." The nicknames evoke their "Ocean Change"—the touch of "strange fishiness" that Dana and Jack carry with them after swimming for three days away from the islands following the shipwreck.

Bohemia

Historical region of Central Europe, the heartland of the modern Czech Republic. Draws on its literary sense, as in Shakespeare's "sea-coast of Bohemia," to mock the idea of Bolivia possessing a coastline. Also a destination for Catherine Dembinska, who intends to carry the Green Scapular there, connecting her mission to the historical work of St. Cyril and the broader spiritual renewal of the Slavic peoples.

Boldness

Primary virtue for the protagonists, closely associated with Dana, whose name may be an Irish cognate for "The Bold." Defined as a proactive, courageous engagement with a hostile and paradoxical world. Not recklessness but a spiritual and physical confidence that allows characters to seize the initiative, confront demonic forces, and act decisively in moments of crisis. A necessary quality for any agent of the "Green Revolution."

Bolus Head

Headland on the Iveragh Peninsula in County Kerry, Ireland. Forms the northern entrance to Kenmare Bay, situating the "Back Doors of the World" in a specific, tangible landscape.

Bonaparte, Joseph [Historical]

Biographical: Joseph Bonaparte (1768–1844), elder brother of Napoleon I, whom Napoleon made King of Naples and later King of Spain.

Connection: His adherents are mentioned as a political faction still present in Campania in the 1850s. Populates the historical landscape with the lingering political legacies of the Napoleonic era, adding complexity to the Italian political scene.

Bonfire

Form of popular art and ritual action. In Naples, bonfires are lit by the people "without knowing why," suggesting a deep-seated, instinctual folk practice. In Paris, the people build bonfires in the Champs Élysées, the spontaneous eruption of revolutionary energy in 1848. Both primal community and political upheaval.

Bosco, John (Don Bosco) [Historical]

Biographical: Saint Giovanni Bosco (1815–1888), an Italian Catholic priest, educator, and founder of the Salesian Order. Known for his work with poor and abandoned youths, and for a joyful, practical spirituality combining piety with acrobatics, juggling, and popular entertainment.

Connection: A major spiritual model within the series. Joseph O'Boyle, after meeting him, dedicates his life to imitating his methods. The priest Monte Albani is a Salesian. An active, engaged, and joyful form of sainthood that is a cornerstone of the "Green Revolution's" ethos, contrasting with more staid or corrupt forms of religion.

Bosquet

Based on the historical French marshal Pierre Bosquet. An Allied leader at the Battle of Inkerman. Credited with turning the tide of the battle; he and his Algerian Zouaves fought with a "blood lust even deeper than that of the Europeans," highlighting the introduction of a new, more savage element into European warfare.

Boy needs something to read

Angelene Domdaniel's playfully profound explanation for having absorbed lewd French cartoons into her womb for her unborn son, Brandon. A startling fusion of the sacred (gestation) and the profane, a holistic worldview that embraces the full spectrum of human experience. Even the developing soul requires nourishment from the raw, unpurified material of life; a humorous critique of puritanical solemnity.

Brandenburg

Historical state of Germany surrounding Berlin. In the mid-19th century, a core territory of the Kingdom of Prussia. Brume and Coscuin traveling in "country Brandenburg," situating their clandestine activities within a key center of rising European power.

Brandon

Fifth child of Dana and Angelene Cosquin, named Dana St. Brandon Cosquin. Born laughing on the night of Tom's death. Bears birthmarks on his cheeks that are maps of the world—the left cheek has a map of the old world, the right cheek a map of the new world, with special maps of French Guadeloupe and Ireland underneath. Destined to be a "map-maker" and "far-traveler only on parchment," fated never to leave Basse-Terre physically. His instant friendship with the Lion embodies the magical realism of the island.

Bravery

Central virtue, but the series distinguishes between conventional and higher forms. The "high hilarity of blood and death" is one form, but Catherine Dembinska advocates for a more difficult bravery of prevention and non-violence. True bravery involves confronting metaphysical horror, moral paradox, and the "disgusting" truths of the world without succumbing to despair or cynicism. Less about fearlessness in combat and more about spiritual courage to uphold the "Green" cause.

Bray Head

Headland in County Kerry, Ireland, forming the southern point of the entrance to Dingle Bay. Listed in the opening geographical description of the "Bay Country," one of the "Back Doors of the World."

Brazil

Empire of Brazil under Pedro II is mentioned in the context of the global political turmoil of 1848, with the "Pernambuco uprising" cited as a parallel to the European revolutions. Also figures as a destination for the Red Napoleon's followers after their expulsion from Basse-Terre, underscoring the global reach of the series's conspiracies.

Brittany

Celtic region in northwestern France. A place of refuge for Carolina Tirana, who resides in a Benedictine convent there. The region's distinct cultural identity is emphasized through the character of Captain Jones and his Breton crew, who speak their own language and possess unique traditions.

Bronze bells

The bells of Thorn Castle are made of bronze. Their "roaring voices" and "booming" sound announce Dana's fall from grace, functioning as sentient instruments of divine judgment. The specification of bronze lends them an archaic and resonant power, emphasizing their role as ancient, quasi-divine arbiters of spiritual events.

Brotherhood of the Holy Lions

Secret, quasi-religious order of chivalry dedicated to the "Green Revolution." Membership, spiritually conferred, requires vows of extreme purity and integrity. Dana is made a "Castle Lion," the "Lion in Residence" at Thorn Castle, but is swiftly deposed after a spiritual fall. The highest echelon of the "Company," a castled centrality of the movement whose members are held to a superhuman standard.

Brudenell, James Thomas (Seventh Earl of Cardigan) [Historical]

Biographical: James Brudenell, 7th Earl of Cardigan (1797–1868), the British cavalry commander who led the Charge of the Light Brigade at Balaclava.

Connection: An anachronistic hero, consciously performing an act of "Counting Coup" in the medieval tradition. His "heroic and insane stroke" is the last gasp of an older form of warfare, an act of pure, aristocratic valor immediately misunderstood and condemned by the modern world of war correspondents and utilitarian morality.

Brudhail, Peacog (Peacock Proud)

Magnificent racehorse that participates in the "Race Itself" at Lynchy Farm. His name, Irish for "Peacock Proud," and his "friskiest" nature embody the pride and resurgent vitality of the Irish folk spirit.

Bruges

City in Belgium. A place visited by Brume and Coscuin where, unusually, they "shed no blood." Either a zone of temporary peace or a center for a more subtle form of conspiracy than the violent confrontations that mark their travels elsewhere.

Brume, Magdelena (Holy Panther)

Wife of Malandrino Brume. A figure of paradoxical power: a saint who is also a "guerilla warrior and sometimes an assassin." A woman of great beauty and passionate love for her "ugliest man in the world," she embodies a fierce, protective, and spiritually potent form of the feminine divine. As the "Holy Panther," a wild, untamed sanctity. A core member of the "Green Revolution" and a spiritual guardian of the "Company."

Brume, Malandrino

Central figure in the "Green Revolution," Dana's primary mentor and companion. A "noblest Roman" who has become a "rough mountain man," the "ugliest man in the world" but possessing immense strength, profound wisdom, and a direct, visceral intelligence. A master of both physical combat and metaphysical strategy, guiding Dana through the complexities of the European "shadowy land." The rugged, enduring, and deeply committed heart of the "Company's" struggle.

Buchez, Philippe [Historical]

Biographical: Philippe Buchez (1796–1865), a French historian, politician, and Christian socialist. Authored a 40-volume history of the French Revolution.

Connection: Named as one of the intellectual forerunners of the "Green Revolution," representing the strain of thought that seeks to reconcile Christian faith with social reform. Also mentioned as one of the "One Hundred Persons" of Paris, a key ideologue of the 1848 era.

Buenos Aires

Capital city of the Argentine Confederation. In the 1850s, the seat of power for the dictator Juan Manuel de Rosas. The center of a serene, self-confident, and locally-rooted culture, in stark contrast to the nervous, internationalized atmosphere of Montevideo across the La Plata estuary.

Bugeaud

Based on Marshal Thomas Robert Bugeaud. Appointed by King Louis Philippe to take supreme command of the armed forces during the 1848 Revolution with orders to "sweep Paris clean." His authority quickly evaporates when he is persuaded by rational arguments to halt his military action, the collapse of decisive force in the face of revolutionary rhetoric and confusion.

Bulnes, Manuel [Historical]

Biographical: Manuel Bulnes Prieto (1799–1866), President of Chile from 1841 to 1851. His tenure is known as a period of stability, institutional development, and peace.

Connection: His presidency is "the good years" of Chile, an era of workable, decentralized government. The end of his term and the election of his successor, Manuel Montt, is the catalyst for the revolutionary civil war, framed as an external disruption of a successful indigenous political order.

Burial

Final disposition of the dead, a recurring and significant ritual act. The proper burial of General Sabatelli's dismembered body in consecrated ground is a major undertaking for the protagonists. Mary Margaret McGinty's desire to be buried on Basse-Terre illustrates the importance of a sacred connection to place. A crucial act of spiritual and communal respect, the denial of which is a profound desecration.

Byron, Lord [Historical]

Biographical: George Gordon Byron (1788–1824), English Romantic poet.

Connection: The favored poet for translation by the literary exiles in Montevideo, including Esteban Echeverría. A key cultural icon for the 19th-century revolutionary and liberal intelligentsia in South America, a symbol of romantic rebellion.

C

Cabet, Etienne [Historical]

Biographical: Étienne Cabet (1788–1856), a French utopian socialist who founded the Icarian movement, which established egalitarian communes in the United States.

Connection: Listed among the "One Hundred Persons" of Paris, the key figures shaping the future in 1848. Identified as the inventor of "Cabetian Communism and the Icarian Commonwealth," the utopian, communalist strain of revolutionary thought.

Cabog, Tom (Clown)

Irish showman, the burgeoning popular culture of the "golden age of the music halls." Belongs to a new class of "poor Irishmen with their pockets full of money." Travels Ireland with a horse-drawn show-wagon, recruiting boys from Joseph O'Boyle's establishment for various acts. An agent of a vibrant, demotic, and mobile Irish culture, providing a practical network through which the protagonists move and interact outside formal political or military structures.

Caesar

Archetypal secular ruler, invoked by Malandrino Brume in dismissing his wife's monitory dream. When Magdelena wakes him with her warning, Brume growls, "Women have no business with these monitory dreams" and "Who are you, Caesar's wife or Pilate's, that you should suffer a dream?" Her answer—"I am the wife of a nobler Roman than either"—places Caesar (and by extension, the state) in a subordinate position to her own spiritual authority, the political realm as secondary to the metaphysical.

Cagliari

Capital city of the island of Sardinia. In the mid-19th century, the administrative center of the Kingdom of Sardinia. The destination for Kemper Gruenland after he leaves Hendaye, situating his clandestine mission in a key political center of the Italian Risorgimento. Also noted as the home of the "Father Club" of the Demos Clubs, a hub for revolutionary secret societies.

Cajetan [Historical]

Biographical: Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), an Italian philosopher, theologian, Cardinal, and Master of the Order of Preachers. A prominent Thomist, famous for his commentary on Aquinas's Summa Theologiae and for his encounter with Martin Luther.

Connection: Cited as having stated that "Evil is an illusion." This theological axiom frames Ifreann Chortovitch's power, suggesting that his destructive force is ultimately an unreality, a "dreadful dream" that lacks true substance, even as it has real and devastating effects.

Calais

Major port city in northern France on the Strait of Dover. In the mid-19th century, a primary point of transit between France and England. A stop for the "Company" after the Crimean War, the location of the "Parlor Bar of the Italian Inn" where False Jack Galopade reveals his experiences in Hell.

Camel

Used by Charlotta Paradisio as a metaphorical descriptor for the red-bearded General Sabatelli. The term, coupled with her statement that he might be found in a "manger" or that "Wise Men followed his star," creates a deliberately eccentric and blasphemous parallel between Sabatelli and Christ. A rough, unkempt savior figure, fusing the sacred with the grotesque and the absurd.

Campbell, General [Historical]

Biographical: Sir Colin Campbell, 1st Baron Clyde (1792–1863), a Scottish officer who commanded the Highland Brigade during the Crimean War. Famed for his command of the "Thin Red Line" at the Battle of Balaclava.

Connection: Accurately depicted in his historical role at Balaclava. Also appears as a participant in the post-battle discussion on the yacht, expressing the grim realization that traditional warfare has ended and voicing profound anxiety about future conflicts, which he foresees as proliferating into something immeasurably larger and more immoral.

Candy Dancer

"Friskiest" racehorse participating in the Race Itself at Lynchy Farm. The name embodies the joyful, almost surreal, energy of the revitalized Irish folk culture.

Candy Store (Mona Milean)

Racehorse in the Race Itself, described as being "striped red-and-white like candy." The fantastical description contributes to the mythic, larger-than-life quality of the Irish racing scene, a world where even the animals partake in magical reality.

Canonized

Formal process by which the Catholic Church declares a person a saint. Contrasted with the informal, folk-driven process of beatification for Joseph O'Boyle. Lady Valiente states that O'Boyle "may be canonized as a saint," framing it as a potential future outcome that would validate the "Company's" private veneration, while also suggesting that their own spiritual economy acts independently of official sanction.

Canrobert's Hill

Fortified position serving as the eastern anchor of the outer defenses of Balaclava during the Crimean War. Its capture by the Russians is the opening move in the Battle of Balaclava, a key strategic loss for the Allied forces.

Cape Horn

Southernmost headland of the Tierra del Fuego archipelago of southern Chile. Rounding the Horn was, in the age of sail, one of the most dangerous maritime passages in the world. The voyage of the Catherine Dembinska around the Horn is a metaphysical "death adventure," a journey through a chaotic space of elemental fury where the ship and its crew are tested to their limits and the laws of reality are suspended.

Capes-Terre

Town on the southern coast of Basse-Terre Island. In the mid-19th century, a small settlement. The destination of a local priest, whose hard ride there for Mass illustrates the physical demands of ministry on the rugged island.

Caprera

Small island off the northeastern coast of Sardinia. The historical home and final resting place of Giuseppe Garibaldi. The series notes his "temporary retirements" there after his campaigns, accurately reflecting his historical practice.

Capuchin

Mendicant order of Franciscan friars. The Capuchin house in Blois is where the "Select Company" attends Christmas Mass. An encounter with a traditional and orthodox form of Catholicism, providing a moment of spiritual grounding before the company engages in its own more eccentric celebration.

Carbonari Society

Network of secret revolutionary societies active in Italy in the early 19th century. A historical manifestation of "The Devil Unchained," a vehicle for the "Beast" but not the Beast itself. Malandrino Brume is an enemy of the Carbonari, while Jude Revanche is a "Carbonarist who'd burn the whole world down." The nihilistic, destructive impulse of the Red Revolution.

Carcassonne

Fortified city in southern France. In the mid-19th century, a provincial town known for its extensive medieval fortifications. The starting point of a road to Narbonne which, according to the ghost of Jude Revanche, is "overcrowded" with souls on their way to Purgatory.

Cardinal-Archbishop

Highest-ranking ecclesiastical office in a major archdiocese. The series features Philip Caracciolo, the fictional Cardinal-Archbishop of Naples, a sophisticated political and spiritual broker. Worldly, witty, and privy to the intricate workings of both secular and sacred power, granting permissions for Ercule Tirana's visit to the Prisoner of Naples. Institutional religion deeply enmeshed in, and not entirely pure from, the intrigues of the world.

Carinthia

Southern Austrian state. During the 1848 revolutionary period, part of the Austrian Empire. Brume and Coscuin are said to have been in Carinthia during their travels, indicating the pan-European scope of their clandestine activities for the Green Revolution.

Carlist

Adherent of Carlism, a traditionalist, legitimist political movement in Spain seeking to establish a separate line of the Bourbon dynasty on the throne. The Carlists are the primary agents of the "Green Revolution" in Spain, a conservative, agrarian, and deeply religious opposition to the centralizing, secularizing forces of the Madrid government. They thrive on defeat and operate as a mysterious, "invisible opposition."

Carlist Hills

Mountainous regions of the Basque country and Navarre in northern Spain, the historical heartland of the Carlist movement. Both a physical and a metaphysical landscape, a "fundamental rock" that is the source of a deep, archaic, and conservative spirituality. Here Dana receives his initial indoctrination into the "Black Revolution" and the "veriest touch of the Green."

Carlit

Mountain in the Pyrenees. The Black Pope uses it as an example of a name whose deep historical and etymological roots transcend its contemporary political association with Don Carlos. He links it to a similarly named mountain in the Caucasus and to words for "king," "dwarf," and "discourse," weaving it into a mystical history that posits Carlism as an ancient, primordial force rather than a modern political movement.

Carloforte

Main town on the island of San Pietro, off the southwestern coast of Sardinia. The narrative culminates in the gathering of the "Company" at Carloforte, which becomes the new operational center for the Green Revolution. The town's two opposing castles, Thorn Castle and Gate Castle, serve as a microcosm of the metaphysical conflict between the forces of Dana and Ifreann.

Carmelites

Catholic religious order. Their dark brown habit is noted as the color of a gown worn by a lady (the disguised Elena Prado) whom Dana encounters. The comparison establishes a baseline of religious orthodoxy against which the lady's ambiguous and ultimately snake-like nature is revealed.

Carob Tree

Referred to as the "Judas-Tree," the tree on which the boy Robin Ledwich hangs himself after betraying the secret of the priest Monte Albani. Its identification with both Carob and Judas links the boy's personal tragedy to a larger archetype of betrayal and despair, imbuing a simple landscape feature with profound and ominous significance.

Castel Gandolfo

Traditional summer residence of the Pope, located in the Alban Hills outside Rome. During the revolutionary turmoil in Rome, Pope Pius IX flees the Vatican and briefly stops at Castel Gandolfo, where he sees a "secret flag" warning him that the location is unsafe.

Castillo (in the valley)

Ancestral home and fortress of the Condesa Elena Prado, located in the Carlist Hills. A place of decadent luxury and moral ambiguity, serving as a base for the "new" Spanish nobility aligned with the Queen's government. The setting for Dana's first extended encounter with Elena and for the abortive wedding that is actually a "death-ambush," a locus of seductive and deadly power.

Castlemaine

Town in County Kerry, Ireland, situated on the River Maine at the head of Dingle Bay. Mentioned in the opening description of the "Bay Country" as one of the key towns in the region.

Castles than in Spayne

Quotation from Chaucer's Romaunt of the Rose, used as an epigraph for a chapter. Evokes the idea of building illusory or imaginary castles. Resonates with the series's preoccupation with apparitional architecture, metaphysical conflict, and the construction of reality through will and imagination.

Castletown

Town on Berehaven Harbour in Bantry Bay, County Cork. In the mid-19th century, a significant fishing port. Dana's home base at the beginning of the narrative and the location where his cousin Aileen Dinneen acts as lawyer and land agent.

Castletown Landing

Docks at Castletown in Bantry Bay. The site of Dana's first physical encounter with the "giant" Kemper Gruenland and later the location where the one-eyed Carolina Tirana reappears as a "hideous fishwife," a recurring point of arrival and departure.

Catholic

Catholic faith is the foundational spiritual framework for the protagonists of the Green Revolution. Portrayed not as a monolithic institution but as a living tradition encompassing orthodox belief (the Mass, the sacraments), folk piety (the creation of local saints), and esoteric orders (the Brotherhood of the Holy Lions). The narrative contrasts this vital, if sometimes heterodox, Catholicism with the corrupt, politically co-opted "new" Catholicism of figures like the Queen's Bishop in Spain.

Catholic Cemetery

Place of consecrated burial. Mary Margaret McGinty, a Protestant, desires to be buried in the Catholic Cemetery on Basse-Terre, which necessitates a summary baptism by Elaine Kingsberry. A communal space of sacred rest seen as desirable even by those outside its formal communion.

Catholic worship

In the historical context of the Penal Laws in Ireland, Catholics were compelled by law to attend Protestant worship and forbidden from attending their own, making the act of Catholic worship illegal and defiant. Underscores the theme of religious persecution and the resilience of a faith practiced under duress.

Caucasus Mountains

Mountain range between the Black Sea and the Caspian Sea. The Black Pope links the Pyrenean mountain Carlit to a mountain of the same name in the Caucasus, and the Spanish "Iberia" to the ancient Caucasian "Iberia," positing a mystical subterranean connection. This esoteric geography universalizes the Carlist struggle, framing it as part of an ancient, global conflict.

Causeway Heights

Topographical feature of the Balaclava battlefield. The Russian cavalry comes over Causeway Heights before being engaged by Scarlett's Heavy Brigade, situating the historical charge in a specific, if localized, geography.

Cavour, Count (Camillo Benso di Cavour) [Historical]

Minister of Sardinia and Piedmont who represents the kingdom at the Congress of Paris (February 25 to March 30, 1856). A secret member of the National Society working for Italian unification, in contrast to open members like Garibaldi. King Victor Emanuele II notes that "there are those who say that my minister Cavour has all the brains in his own head" and worries about finding a replacement for him. Dana and his companions travel to Turin to speak with both Count Cavour and King Victor Emanuele about the Italian situation.

Cavour, Count Camillo [Historical]

Biographical: Camillo Benso, Count of Cavour (1810–1861), a leading figure in the Italian Risorgimento and the first Prime Minister of a unified Italy.

Connection: Portrayed as a brilliant but deeply compromised Machiavellian statesman, the "puppet-master" behind King Victor Emmanuel II. One of the "Four Men" targeted in the "Great Substitution" plot. His physical attributes (the Scalenus Anticus muscle) are noted as signs of his triple nature as wrestler, sculptor, and ventriloquist. The complex, morally ambiguous nature of modern statecraft.

Centennial Flower

Designation for any imported plant that has grown on Basse-Terre for at least one hundred years, thereby earning "full citizen of this Paradise." The island's syncretic and absorptive culture, where foreign elements, through time and integration, become fully naturalized and part of the local identity.

Change/Progress

A deeply ambivalent view of progress. Acknowledges the material changes of the 19th century but frames them within a larger spiritual conflict. True progress is the advancement of the "Green Revolution," a spiritual and organic renewal. False progress is the mechanistic, soul-destroying program of the "Red Revolution," which cloaks itself in the language of modernity. Historical change is not a linear march forward but a cyclical and often violent struggle between these two opposing visions of the future.

Change/Progress in Warfare

A pivotal moment in the transformation of warfare. The shift from an older, aristocratic model of "tournament-type wars" and heroic individualism (epitomized by the Charge of the Light Brigade) to a new era of impersonal, mass slaughter facilitated by modern technology (minie rifles, photography) and ideological fervor propagated by a new class of war correspondents. A moral and aesthetic degradation, a loss of the "drama" and "neatness" that characterized older forms of conflict.

Chaos/Destiny

A deterministic framework where destiny, often revealed through prophecy or intuition, is a powerful force. However, this destiny unfolds within a world of radical chaos, illusion, and "creative confusion." The protagonists' task is to navigate this chaos while remaining aligned with their foreordained purpose. The tension between a seemingly fixed destiny and the chaotic, unpredictable nature of reality is a central dynamic of the plot.

Chaos/Instability

The mid-19th century is an era of profound instability, where old orders are collapsing and new forces are violently contending for control. This chaos is both political (the revolutions of 1848, the Crimean War, the Italian unification) and metaphysical (the unchaining of the Devil, the blurring of reality and illusion). The protagonists do not seek to impose a rigid order but to navigate the chaos, channeling it toward a "Green" outcome rather than a "Red" one. Instability is the fundamental condition of their world.

Chapel in Greenfields

Private chapel at the Greenfields Estate on Basse-Terre. The site of Tom Cosquin's funeral Mass, underscoring the estate's role as a self-contained social and spiritual center for the "Company" on the island.

Charity
