Primary social and spiritual need articulated by the beggars of Blois. They articulate a theology of charity in which the act of giving is a "Heaven-sent grace" necessary for the salvation of the wealthy, and the role of the beggar is to "assist our betters to this grace" by receiving. Reverses the conventional perspective, framing charity as a crucial transaction for the spiritual health of the giver.

Charlemagne [Historical]

Biographical: Charlemagne (c. 742–814), King of the Franks and first Holy Roman Emperor.

Connection: Invoked as a figure in folk astronomy and mythology. The star-group "Charles Wain" is said to be his wagon, a "vehicle of a higher King than Charlemagne." The historical emperor is thus used as a stepping-stone to a higher, more metaphysical kingship, linking the terrestrial history of Christendom to a celestial order.

Charles III (of Spain) [Historical]

Biographical: Carlos III (1716–1788), King of Spain, an "enlightened despot" known for his reforms.

Connection: Mentioned by the Carlist irregulars in their invocation of past kings, "Remember Charles III." Places him within the legitimist lineage that the Carlists champion, a model of strong, authentic kingship that they seek to restore.

Charles IV (of Spain) [Historical]

Biographical: Carlos IV (1748–1819), King of Spain, whose ineffective reign led to the Napoleonic invasion.

Connection: Invoked along with Charles III by the Carlists, establishing the historical continuity of the royal line they support.

Charles Wain (Osa Mayor/Big Bear)

Ursa Major, also known as the Big Dipper. The Black Pope cites its folk name, "Charles Wain" (Charlemagne's Wagon), as part of his mystical etymology of "Carlist." He interprets it as the "vehicle of the King of Heaven," linking the stars themselves to the sacred, monarchical principles of the Carlist cause.

Charon

Ferryman of Hades in Greek mythology. "Charon's boat" is invoked by Dana as the vessel for a "mysterious trip" undertaken by Count Vantour. Frames Vantour's journey in mythic and infernal terms, suggesting a clandestine mission connected to the underworld.

Chastel, Rosa

Niece of General Sabatelli and cousin of Charlotta Paradiso. A loyal member of the "Company," accompanying her uncle and later O'Boyle in their adventures. Plays a key role in the retrieval and reburial of Sabatelli's dismembered body and later becomes part of the circle of women in Ireland who support Dana's mission.

Chastity

State of spiritual and physical purity that is a central, though frequently violated, ideal. As a member of the Brotherhood of the Holy Lions, Dana takes a vow of chastity, which he breaks in the "Green Thorn" episode, leading to his fall. A source of immense spiritual power and a prerequisite for certain sacred roles, and its violation results in immediate and severe consequences.

Chaucer, Geoffrey [Historical]

Biographical: Geoffrey Chaucer (c. 1343–1400), English poet, author of The Canterbury Tales.

Connection: A couplet from his translation Romaunt of the Rose is used as an epigraph: "Thou shalt make castles than in Spayne, / And dreme of Joye, al but in vayne." Establishes themes of illusion, unattainable ideals, and the construction of imaginary worlds.

Chico River

River in southern Argentina. Its estuary, along with that of the Santa Cruz River, is mentioned as a possible place for Ifreann's damaged ship, the Brami, to land for repairs after its initial encounter with the Catherine.

Chile Coast

Long, narrow Pacific coastline of Chile. The "spine and ghost of the South American continent." A landscape of stark contrasts, from the arid northern desert where the Catherine lands at Tocopillo, to the forested southern archipelagoes where she navigates through ice and storm. The physical stage for the ideological battle over Chile's future.

Chimborazo

Massive, inactive stratovolcano in the Andes of Ecuador, the highest peak in the country. A "buey (steer) mountain, not a toro (bull) mountain" because it lacks volcanic fire. Part of a symbolic classification of the landscape, framing the natural world in terms of active and passive, masculine principles.

China

Mentioned as having been "discovered, early and often" from the bays of southwestern Ireland. This fantastical claim is part of the construction of the "Bay Country" as a mythic point of origin for global exploration, one of the "Back Doors of the World" connected to all corners of the earth through secret, non-historical pathways.

Chincha

The Chincha Islands, off the coast of Peru, famous in the 19th century for their massive deposits of guano, a valuable fertilizer. The phrase "carrying... guano to Chincha" is used as a proverbial expression for a pointless or redundant action, akin to "carrying coals to Newcastle."

Chortovitch, Ifreann (Son of the Devil, The Devil's Grandson, Purple Devil)

Dana's primary antagonist, a being of supernatural origin and power. Born in 1830 in a hayfield between Skawine and Krakow to an Irish governess (Katie Noonan) and the Devil. A "huge, coarse, handsome, and incredible person" who embodies the "Red Revolution." His name combines Irish Ifreann (Hell) with a Slavic patronymic suggesting "son of the devil." Educated at the world's great universities despite his accelerated, unnatural development—appearing twelve years old when born. He possesses the "thirteen devil wounds," can transform into monstrous forms, create powerful illusions, and commands demonic forces. His goal is not merely Dana's death but his "death and damnation." Capable of error—his "anti-sin" of saving Carolina earns him banishment from his own kind. Killed multiple times by Dana, he persists as ghost and influence, embodying the ineradicable nature of evil in the fallen world.

Chosen man of destiny

Describes the Italian revolutionary Giuseppe Garibaldi. His followers say that their offering to him "transcends any sin because he is a chosen man of destiny." A romantic and dangerous political messianism, where an individual is seen as an instrument of historical fate, exempt from ordinary moral laws. A key component of the "Red" revolutionary ethos.

Christ

Central figure of the Christian faith and the metaphysical anchor of the "Green Revolution." The "Center which is everything," the point of absolute reality in a world of illusion and "eccentricity." His Incarnation is the model for the renewal of the world. While figures like Elena Prado offer a blasphemous, carnal interpretation of him, for the protagonists he is the ultimate source of grace, truth, and the divine order they seek to defend.

Christ coming

The Second Coming of Christ, or the parousia. Kemper Gruenland's Testament reinterprets this eschatological event as an imminent, continuous process: "it is tomorrow, it is today yet if we hurry." This theological urgency frames the "Green Revolution" as a work of immediate and total preparation for a final cosmic resolution, demanding that all aspects of life be reconstituted in accordance with this impending arrival.

Christ Coming Through The Walls

"Rough peasant painting" in Jane Blaye's shrine. Depicts Christ entering a bolted room, divine power that transcends physical barriers and brings a paradoxical peace. Along with the skull of Christian Blaye and the statue of the Virgin, one of the three "strong objects" in the shrine, a source of spiritual insight for Dana.

Christendom

Historical and cultural reality of the Christian world. The series posits a "humming vitality of Christendom" threatened by the "disjointed and deformed intrusions" of the demonic. Christendom is not a static political entity but a living, organic, and often embattled spiritual civilization whose integrity is the central stake in the cosmic war.

Christian

State of being aligned with the grace and order of Christ. The concept is frequently contested. Elena Prado claims that "Christian men are not effete," only to be told by Angelene that "the times you are not effete are the times you also are not Christian." A complex moral and spiritual identity, often defined by struggle and paradox rather than simple piety.

Christian burial

Key concern for the characters, a proper and sanctified end. Jane Blaye laments that her murdered husband Christian's body received Christian burial but his head did not. The significance of ritual and the belief in a sacred order that extends to the disposition of the deceased.

Christian Hercules

Prophetic title given to the Ecuadorean leader Gabriel Moreno by his cousin Milagroso. A future role as a powerful, Christ-like savior of his nation. Combines classical and Christian archetypes, framing Moreno's destiny as a synthesis of heroic strength and religious mission.

Christian persons

Used by Lord Jack Galopade to express his exasperation with England, stating that "God had no right to require longer residence of Christian persons" there. Humorously defines Christian identity in opposition to a specific national culture, suggesting that to be truly Christian is to be somewhat alien to the mundane and irreligious world.

Christmas

Christian feast of the Nativity. The occasion for a gathering of the "Select Company" in Blois, where they celebrate by feasting with beggars, and later for a crucial confrontation with Ifreann at Seven Heads Castle. A point in sacred time when the forces of good and evil are brought into sharp, dramatic focus.

Christmas in July

Used by Count Cyril to describe the paradoxical and joyful nature of the "Company's" struggle. A suspension of normal temporal and seasonal logic, a state where hope and celebration ("Christmas") can erupt in the midst of trial and dislocation ("July"). The festive, miraculous, and counter-intuitive spirit of the Green Revolution.

Christocentricity

Theological principle that Christ is the absolute center of reality. The priest of Basse-Terre articulates it: "There are only two things: the Center which is everything, and the eccentricity (the off-center) which is almost nothing." The metaphysical foundation for the Green Revolution, defining its struggle as an effort to defend and restore this divine center against the chaotic and nihilistic forces of the periphery.

Christus

Latin name for Christ, used by Ifreann Chortovitch when he declares himself "the caricature of the Christus," a demonic imitation who "exceeds the original in every way." The use of the formal, Latin term emphasizes the archaic and liturgical nature of the reality Ifreann seeks to parody and supplant.

Church

Complex, both a divine institution and a human one susceptible to infiltration and corruption. The vessel of the "True Faith" and the source of the sacraments, but also a battleground. The "Green Revolution" acts largely outside its formal structures, yet in its defense. The narrative distinguishes between the eternal Church and its temporal manifestations, such as the compromised political bishops or the beleaguered Papal States.

Cima, Mariella (Mountain Wife)

Wife of Tancredi Cima, a Sardinian "mountain giantess" of immense strength, beauty, and primitive power. An expert rifle-woman, an unerring guide, and a fierce combatant who can "whip" almost any man. Embodies a wild, chthonic spirituality, possessing a deep intuition and a playful, often violent, approach to life. Fiercely loyal to the "Company" and serves as a vital, grounding force within the group.

Cima, Tancredi

Sardinian "mountainous man," husband of Mariella, and a core member of Dana's company. Tall, lean, and possessed of a "red-black anger," a "shy killer" who acts with deadly secrecy on the "steep roofs of Paris." Deeply committed to the Green Revolution but remains a complex and sometimes paradoxical figure. His premonitions are "almost always wrong," and his character is defined by a tension between fierce loyalty and a shy, brooding interiority.

Circolo Romano [Historical]

Revolutionary club or society in Rome. Mentioned by the spider-keeper Ashley as one of the "segmented spiders," a "generative happening" within the broader European revolutionary movement of the 1840s.

Clarity/Truth

Central epistemological and moral goal for the protagonists. Count Cyril preaches "Clarity in all things," yet his own nature remains shrouded in mystery. The narrative world is one of profound illusion and deception, and the characters are constantly engaged in a struggle to discern the "clear way," to distinguish true from false, and to perceive the underlying reality behind the masks. Truth is not a static fact but a state of being that must be actively achieved and defended against the obfuscations of the enemy.

Clausewitz, Carl von [Historical]

Biographical: Carl von Clausewitz (1780–1831), a Prussian general and military theorist, author of the seminal work On War.

Connection: Quoted as saying that "in such dangerous things as war, the error which proceeds from the spirit of benevolence is the worst." Frames the ruthless ideology of the Red Napoleon, providing a real-world philosophical justification for a new, amoral form of warfare.

Clio

Muse of History in Greek mythology. Not being a "decent lady" like her sisters and having "damnably unsanitary habits," a personification of the messy, contradictory, and often irrational nature of history itself. Also "cross-eyed," a metaphor for history's dual and conflicting narratives, and said to "swear absolutely" that the "Great Substitution" plot actually happened, lending a flawed, mythological authority to one of the series's central counter-historical events.

Clonakilty

Town in County Cork, Ireland. In the mid-19th century, a local market town. The site of a British barracks to which Dana goes for information regarding the disappearance of General Sabatelli.

Clonmel

Town in County Tipperary, Ireland. A place from which a "great hero" was forced to flee due to "too close love," drawing a parallel to Dana's own flight from his cousin Aileen in Bantry Bay.

Cloonee Hills

Range of hills on the Beara Peninsula in County Cork, Ireland. Part of the landscape of the "Bay Country" and the backdrop for Dana's departure from Ireland, the place from which he looks back to see the ship Skaebne.

Cobbett, William [Historical]

Biographical: William Cobbett (1763–1835), an English pamphleteer, journalist, and radical agrarian who advocated for political reform.

Connection: Named by Malandrino Brume, along with Richard Cobden, as one of the "outlandish and contradictory" English forerunners of the Green Revolution. Situates the movement's intellectual origins in the historical tradition of radical agrarianism and reform.

Cobden, Richard [Historical]

Biographical: Richard Cobden (1804–1865), an English manufacturer, Radical, and Liberal statesman, a leading figure in the Anti-Corn Law League.

Connection: Named alongside William Cobbett as an early, if contradictory, formulator of the Green Revolution's ideas, linking the movement to the historical currents of English Radicalism and free trade.

Cock-crow dawn

Allusion to the cock crowing after Peter's denial of Christ. Marks a moment of betrayal and spiritual failure. Dana is a "fallen lion" who "betrayed" his high commission "before the third cock-crow dawn," framing his personal moral lapse in the context of one of the Gospels' most resonant moments of human weakness.

Collective Identity

Identity as a divided and often shared phenomenon. Characters are frequently subsumed into larger collective identities, whether it be the familial bond of the "Company," the mystical unity of the Irish people, or the shared consciousness of a revolutionary cell. Individual identity is porous, with characters taking on the attributes of others (Dana inheriting Kemper's personality) or serving as doubles. Challenges the modern notion of the discrete, autonomous self, positing identity as a communal, and often contested, property.

Colorados

Italian/Spanish word for "Reds," denoting the liberal, centralizing political faction in the 19th-century civil wars of Uruguay. The primary opponents of the conservative, localist Blancos led by Rosas. The term aligns them with the broader, international "Red Revolution," the forces of modern, secular, and often violent upheaval.

Common Sense

Not a universal faculty but a "reasoned syndrome of the particular senses" that is specific to each species. The Devil and his hybrid offspring are said to suffer a "thinness" of Common Sense because their senses differ from those of humans. Transforms common sense from a simple pragmatic virtue into a complex, species-specific mode of perception, and its absence in demonic beings is a key to their inhumanity and their strategic vulnerabilities.

Commune

The act of receiving the Blessed Sacrament in the Eucharist. Angelene Domdaniel is going to communion daily with a "shattering humility," an act that grounds her wild, chthonic power in a framework of orthodox piety and underscores the paradoxical nature of her character.

Communion of souls

State of metaphysical intermingling where the boundaries between individual consciousnesses dissolve. Dana experiences this state in the "Ocean" dream, where "all the friends and enemies were already in town, whether in the flesh or out of it," and all "inhabited each other." A mystical unity that transcends physical presence and even personal enmity, a glimpse into the underlying spiritual substance that connects all beings.

Company of the Saints in Heaven

Celestial community of saints. The camaraderie and shared purpose of the series's terrestrial "Company" is compared to it, suggesting that the protagonists form a "church militant" on earth that is a mirror of the "church triumphant" in heaven.

Company Without A Name

Name for the "Company" used by Count Cyril. Their status as a secret, unacknowledged, and unofficial force for good in the world. Their anonymity is a source of strength, allowing them to operate as a "token opposition" to the "Red Lord of History" on battlefields that are "not found in the numerations of battles at all."

Compulsion

Irresistible, often irrational, force that dictates the actions of individuals and nations. King Charles Albert claims he is under a "compulsion that will not let me follow my bent," suggesting that historical actors are not free agents but are driven by larger, unseen powers. Challenges notions of free will and rational choice, portraying history as a drama orchestrated by metaphysical forces that compel their human puppets.

Comte, Auguste [Historical]

Biographical: Auguste Comte (1798–1857), a French philosopher and a founder of the discipline of sociology and the doctrine of Positivism.

Connection: Named as a key figure whose works, published by 1842, contributed to the rise of a new "mechanistic world" and a "totally atheistic and totally cruel premise." His philosophy of Positivism is framed as one of the intellectual cornerstones of the modern, secular, and destructive worldview that the Green Revolution opposes.

Concupiscence

Strong, often immoral, desire, particularly of a carnal nature. The music of the horn-piper in Hendaye is said to pipe the "concupiscence of the flesh." The raw, unbridled life-force that can be both creative and dangerous, a key element in the spiritual landscape that the protagonists must navigate and, in Dana's case as a Holy Lion, transcend.

Condor's Nest

Symbolic name for the lair of "the Condor" (Gabriel Moreno) located high in the crater of a "bull mountain" in the Ecuadorean Andes. A place of spiritual trial and revelation, where the sky is only "half a sky" and where the characters are forced to confront fundamental truths about their world and their mission. Both a physical refuge and a metaphysical vantage point.

Confessed

Act of confessing sins in the sacrament of Penance. Dana is "confessed of his sins at Prades" before entering Spain, and again to the Black Pope after his affair with Elena Prado. A crucial ritual of spiritual renewal for the protagonists, a necessary step in restoring the state of grace required for their holy war.

Congrega d'Inferno

Italian revolutionary secret society, the "Congregation of Hell." Named by Ashley as one of the "segmented spiders" or "generative happenings" of the 1840s, a specific historical manifestation of the broader, demonic "Red Revolution."

Congress of Animals

A hierarchy or parliament of animals in which various creatures hold rank and engage in political conflict. The Mongoose and the Weasel factions are high in the Congress, and there is bloody conflict between them. Most adult members seem to occupy positions of power. The Congress operates on principles of equity—"The Congress of Animals had worked in equity and had brought about a settlement." Tom Cosquin, not yet thirteen, confronts the Prince of Darkness's representative in single combat as a matter adjudicated by the Congress.

Congress of the Animals

Governing body of all animal species on Basse-Terre, which meets to adjudicate inter-species disputes. Holds a trial and passes judgment on the conflict between the Irish Weasels and the Mongoose King of Darkness. A non-human system of law and order, a primordial parliament that reflects and parodies human political structures.

Congress Poland [Historical]

Kingdom of Poland created by the Congress of Vienna in 1815, ruled in personal union by the Tsar of Russia. After 1848, the Cossacks occupied it in great numbers and all revolutionary plots there were betrayed, contrasting its state of total subjugation with the slightly more fluid situations in Prussian and Austrian Poland.

Connemara

Region in western County Galway, Ireland. Identified as the district where Irish Giants are most common, with "one family in every three" having a giant. Grounds a piece of fantastical folklore in a specific, real-world geography.

Connor Hills

Range of hills on the Dingle Peninsula in County Kerry, Ireland. Placed as the northern boundary of Dingle Bay, part of the defining landscape of the "Bay Country."

Conscience

Internal faculty of moral judgment. The characters frequently wrestle with their consciences, particularly regarding the necessity of violence. Charley Oceaan's conscience "groans pretty heavily" with the assassinations he has committed, even while he justifies them. A living, active force, the arena in which the moral struggles of the "Green Revolution" are most acutely felt.

Consecrated ground

Ground that has been blessed for sacred purposes, particularly for burial. The desire to bury General Sabatelli's remains in "consecrated ground" in the cellar of Skibbereen Castle is a primary motivation for his nieces. A space of spiritual safety and respect for the dead, a sanctuary from the profane world.

Conservatism

Not a modern political ideology but a primordial, restorative impulse. The Carlist Revolution is defined as "conservative, monarchial, religious, agrarian, traditional." This form of conservatism is revolutionary in its opposition to the "innovative, anti-religions" reigning government. Distinguishes between a rooted, organic traditionalism and a reactionary defense of a corrupt status quo.

Constantinople

Capital of the Ottoman Empire (modern Istanbul). In the 19th century, control of Constantinople and the Turkish Straits was a central issue in European geopolitics, known as the "Eastern Question." The series cites Napoleon's warning about the consequences of Russian control of the city. Also the destination given to the young Lion Mulville, transforming the geopolitical nexus into a site of personal, heroic destiny.

Consummated

Act that finalizes a marriage. Mentioned in the context of Dana and the "Company" picking out a wife for him in Montrouge, to be followed by a priest and then consummation. Used in its traditional sense but in a comically accelerated context, highlighting the protagonists' pragmatic and unconventional approach to life's rituals.

Continent

Landmass of Europe. The primary theater of operations for the "Company." Dana's travels across the Continent are a central element of his mission, and his final return to Ireland from the Continent marks a significant transition in his life and the narrative.

Contrition

Sorrow for sin, a necessary condition for absolution. Dana's lack of immediate contrition after a killing prevents a priest from absolving him. Elena Prado feigns a state of "chastity and contrition" after her conversion. Central to the series's theology of redemption, emphasizing the importance of an internal state of repentance over external acts.

Convent

Community of religious women. Carolina Tirana retreats to a Benedictine convent in St. Malo. Convents are not merely places of withdrawal from the world but can also be sites of continued spiritual and artistic activity, as shown by Carolina's statue-carving.

Corfu

Greek island in the Ionian Sea. In the mid-19th century, under British protection. Mentioned in Napoleon's geopolitical analysis as one of the key strategic locations that would be threatened by Russian control of the Dardanelles.

Cork

Major city and port in County Cork, Ireland. In the mid-19th century, a center of British administration and a hub of commerce. A recurring location in the Irish sections of the narrative, the place where people lie "dead and unburied" during the Famine and where General Sabatelli is presumed to have gone to rally support.

Corsica

French island in the Mediterranean Sea. Although French, "its people and its language which are Italian." Also the place where a Corsican named Giacomini is rumored to have fired the first shot that triggered the massacre at the Ministry for Foreign Affairs in Paris in 1848.

Cosmo, Charles (Red Napoleon, Red Melchial)

Primary antagonist of the latter half of the series, the embodiment of the "Red Revolution." A German of minor nobility, a charismatic, ruthless, and brilliant strategist who seeks to impose a new world order through terror and manipulation. Gathers an international cadre of revolutionaries ("The Influencers of the World") at Thieves' Town. His arc culminates in the (counter-historical) overthrow of governments in the Americas and Europe and the murder of the Pope, events that are later mostly "unraveled" and erased from history.

Cosmo, Mary Gretchen

Wife of Professor Max Cosmo and mother of Charles Cosmo. Her only recorded words—"I don't know, Max; I don't know"—when asked about the nature of her prodigious son, express a maternal bewilderment in the face of a phenomenon that defies normal understanding.

Cosmo, Professor Max

Father of Charles Cosmo, a professor at Heidelberg University. Initially fails to recognize his youngest son's genius, seeing him only in the shadow of his six older, more conventionally successful brothers. An established academic order unable to comprehend the new, monstrous form of prodigy that his son embodies.

Coscuin / Cosquin (orthography)

The spelling of the family name varies across the four books, and the variance is Lafferty's own: The Flame Is Green and Sardinian Summer use "Coscuin" throughout; Half a Sky uses both forms; First and Last Island prefers "Cosquin." Half a Sky makes the variance canonical in naming the House of Dana Cosquin: "that spelling has always been used on Basse-Terre; do not change it." Within First and Last Island the two spellings are themselves distinguished: the Basse-Terre family writes "Cosquin," while the surname of the Irish Dana remains "Coscuin"—though the text plays even with this, giving the infant born in February 1867 both forms ("his son… was Coscuin"; "his name is Dana St. Brandon Cosquin") within the same chapter. This Companion follows each book's usage when quoting and does not standardize.

Cosquin, Dana (Dana My Love, Straw-Hair, Count Dana)

Protagonist, an Irish adventurer from Bantry Bay. His name means "The Bold" or "The Dane." His arc is a heroic quest on behalf of the "Green Revolution." A man of immense physical prowess, intuitive wisdom, and deep spiritual commitments. He travels the world as an agent of the mysterious Count Cyril, combating the forces of the "Red Revolution" embodied by Ifreann Chortovitch. His identity is fluid: sea-man, soldier, "Count," Holy Lion, and lover. His journey is a series of spiritual trials, falls from grace, and redemptions, culminating in his role as the new Count Prasimos and leader of the Green cause.

Cosquin, Dominic

Second son of Dana and Angelene Cosquin. At age three, he already displays a precocious, if slightly skewed, understanding of the metaphysical order, speaking of the "Congress of the Animals" as a real and potent entity.

Cosquin, Tom

Eldest son of Dana and Angelene Cosquin. Handsome, rational, and protective of his younger brothers. Acts as an "advocate" for the Irish Weasel in the Congress of the Animals. Marked for a violent death, a fate he accepts with quiet courage. Killed in 1867, not yet thirteen, by the King of Darkness mongoose, inhabited by Prince Dorn, becoming a youthful martyr whose death galvanizes his father into a final confrontation with Count Vantour.

Count Cosquin

Title Dana assumes as part of his inheritance of Seven Heads Castle. Though initially a legal fiction on an old deed, it becomes a functional part of his identity. His elevation to a position of leadership and his entrance into the high-stakes game of European intrigue, making him a "personage" recognized even in strange cities.

Count Cyril Prasinos

Mysterious, unseen leader of the "Green Revolution" and Dana's ultimate employer. A "timeless and invisible instigator" who communicates through messengers, dreams, and oracles (like Christian Blaye's skull). A figure of immense wealth and power, a "Count of all green lands" who orchestrates the global struggle against the demonic forces of the Red Revolution. Though he dies during the narrative, his influence continues posthumously through his Testament and his designated successor, Dana. Hidden, transcendent authority guiding history toward a benevolent end.

Count Kirol

One of the speculative names for Dana's mysterious employer, fabricated by the "black fabricators" at Aileen Dinneen's house. The name, meaning "Eastern King," is a guess at the nature of the authority behind Dana's mission, an attempt to give a name to the unknown power.

Count of Record

Official title of the reigning Count Prasimos in the annals of Greenfields Estate. Angelene explains that even after his death, Count Cyril remains the "Count of Record" until a successor is chosen. In the event of a tie vote among the living governors, his ghost hand is empowered to cast the deciding ballot, authority that transcends death and maintains the continuity of the institution.

Count Ouzel Rotwappen

Elder half-brother and mentor of Ifreann Chortovitch. The "Count of extensive red meadows in hell and of a corresponding estate on earth." A high member of the "Serpent Lodge" and also identified as "the Lord of the Flies." A more aristocratic and strategic form of evil than the raw force of Ifreann, a hidden power broker within the demonic hierarchy.

Count Vantour

Rough, hard kinsman of Ifreann Chortovitch who acts as resident-in-command of the Porte d'Enfer Estate. Of the "Devils Brood," embodying a crude, physical form of evil. His confrontation with Dana over the death of Tom Cosquin reveals his connection to the demonic, and his own death is ambiguous, leaving open the possibility of a ghostly return. A local manifestation of the larger infernal power structure.

County Kerry

County in southwestern Ireland, part of the province of Munster. Encompasses the Dingle Peninsula and is a core part of the "Bay Country." The location of Dana's fabricated title, "Count of County Kerry."

Coursan

Town in southern France, near Narbonne. Mentioned by the ghost of Jude Revanche as being near a "Purgatory road," a waypoint in the metaphysical geography of the afterlife.

Courtnacsherry

Village on Courtnacsherry Bay in County Cork, Ireland. The site of one of the ten "vanished castles" of the South Coast, contributing to the region's atmosphere of lost history and mythic ruin.

Cowardice

A moral failing that is also a strategic tool of the enemy. Ifreann taunts Dana, calling him a "liverless Irish coward" to provoke him into a fatal encounter. Mariella charges Dana with cowardice for agreeing to a duel rather than acceding to derision. Explored through paradox: true courage sometimes requires appearing to be a coward, while conventional bravery can be a form of foolishness.

Coxley

Anglo-Irish Gentryman. His ten-year-old son, William (using the alias Robin Ledwich), acts as a spy in O'Boyle's establishment and is subsequently found hanged. Believing O'Boyle and his associates murdered his son, Coxley leads the mob that burns the Travelers Inn and participates in the summary court that condemns General Sabatelli. The vengeful, violent, and easily manipulated nature of the Anglo-Irish ascendancy.

Creation/Fate

A world in which powerful, often female, figures actively create reality. Scheherazade Jokkebrok claims to "make up" people and fates, animating her "fables" into existence. Angelene Domdaniel "weaves" a life for Dana. This creative power is a form of dictating fate. Challenges deterministic views of destiny, suggesting that the fabric of reality is a contested text, constantly being written and rewritten by opposing metaphysical authors.

Creator

Ultimate source of all being, God. The Red Napoleon's ambition is framed as a direct challenge to the Creator, seeking to supplant the divine order with a "chaos which is the primordial stuff" and become a "transcendent creator" himself.

Crevillente

Town in the province of Alicante, Spain. The series invents a folk etymology, claiming Crevillente Castle is the identical twin of the vanished Crookhaven Castle in Ireland, built by a Spanish son of an Irish mother. Transforms the real place into a symbol of the mystical connection between Ireland and Spain.

Crimea

Peninsula in the Black Sea, part of the Russian Empire in the mid-19th century. The primary setting for the Crimean War. A harsh, elemental landscape where ancient supernatural forces ("bear-people," the "iron prison" of the Devil) coexist with the modern conflict, a place where history and myth violently intersect.

Crimsi Hunter

Charismatic and demagogic politician from the American South. Becomes the (counter-historical) eighteenth President of the United States. His presidency is an instrument of Charles Cosmo's global plot: he disbands the U.S. military, allowing private armies to take control, and facilitates the hostile takeover of the southern states. The corruption of democratic politics by irrationalism and hidden agendas. His story is later dismissed by some characters as a "fictional" lampoon, contributing to the theme of historical duality.

Cristofori

Historical inventor of the piano, Bartolomeo Cristofori. The name is adopted as a surname by Patrick Daniel, one of O'Boyle's boys, who shows a talent for piano-making. O'Boyle's pianos are monogrammed "Cristofori-O'Boyle," linking his own creative work to its historical antecedent and elevating his craft through association with its master.

Croke, Andrew

One of the twelve boys ("The Apostles") at O'Boyle's Inn. Noted as the best stone sculptor among them, a skill learned from Carolina Tirana and Joseph O'Boyle.

Cromwell, Oliver [Historical]

Biographical: Oliver Cromwell (1599–1658), English military and political leader who led the Parliamentarian victory in the English Civil War and ruled as Lord Protector. His conquest of Ireland was marked by extreme brutality.

Connection: Cited at the beginning of the sequel as the source of the English belief that "Irishmen have tails." This historical slander is presented as a fact that helps explain the "strange secret shame that each Irishman has," humorously using a historical enemy's propaganda to "explain" a fictional aspect of Irish identity.

Crooked Man, The

Character from a nursery rhyme, invoked to describe the morally and physically twisted nature of Joseph O'Boyle after his time in the service of Ifreann. Frames O'Boyle as a figure from folklore, a grotesque but recognizable type whose deformities are both literal and spiritual.

Crookhaven

Village in County Cork, Ireland. The site of one of the "Spanish Coast Castles." The series creates a myth that this vanished castle flew to Spain, where it became Crevillente Castle, a story that encapsulates the deep, imaginative links between the two regions.

Croonin, Father

Old, "dried out" priest in the Bantry Bay area. Offers Dana cryptic and prescient warnings before his departure, seeing visions of his dishonorable death in Spain. Ancient, prophetic, and world-weary wisdom of the Irish clergy. After Dana's spiritual fall with Elena Prado, Father Croonin's knowledge of the event is instantaneous and trans-geographical, underscoring his role as a spiritual sentinel.

Crucified

Ifreann claims to have been crucified twenty-one times, a blasphemous parody of Christ's singular sacrifice. Part of his demonic mythology, an attempt to co-opt and pervert the central event of Christian salvation history for his own self-glorification.

Crucifix

Cross with a representation of Christ's body. Malandrino Brume carves a crucifix of "great power, passion and brutality," an object that reflects his own rough and powerful faith. A tangible symbol of the series's earthy, non-sentimental Christianity.

Cuchulainn

Great hero of the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology. Joseph O'Boyle is said to have been "Cuchulainn O'Boyle" in his youth, a nickname signifying his prowess as a warrior and his embodiment of the heroic Irish spirit. Places O'Boyle within the lineage of Ireland's most potent mythological figures.

Cudgel-Man

Large, unnamed henchman of Ifreann Chortovitch who confronts Dana on Basse-Terre. One of the four "flunkies" sent to ambush Dana. Though defeated, he later reappears as a friendly acquaintance, sharing a walk with Dana, the strange and often non-lethal nature of many of the conflicts. He is later identified as O'Boyle, revealing a continuity of character across different roles and identities.

Cult

Describes the group of men on Basse-Terre who take a "joint of the left thumb from each" of the hanged men. A secretive, ritualistic practice existing within the island's complex spiritual landscape, hinting at darker, syncretic beliefs operating beneath the surface of both folk Catholicism and revolutionary politics.

Curiosity

Driving force for many characters, but one that acts differently in the opposing factions. The "Company" is motivated by a holy curiosity, a desire to understand the mysteries of God and the world. Their enemies are driven by a perverse curiosity, a need to profane, dissect, and control. Embodied in the "itchy ears" of Mariella and the intellectual probing of characters like Dana, who constantly seek to unravel the "riddle" of their existence.

Curse

Verbal act that invokes supernatural harm. The Indian man on the "half-sky" mountain curses Dana. A young girl in France puts an "incantation" on Dana. Curses are not idle threats but effective speech acts that can have real, tangible consequences, part of the verbal and magical weaponry used by all sides.

Cyclops

One-eyed giant of Greek mythology. The series locates the "original Cyclop's Cave" in Sardinia and claims that his giant skull is preserved there. This act of geographical and mythological appropriation, like the stories of Alaric and Charlemagne, serves to ground the fantastic events in a re-imagined and universalized classical past, making Sardinia a nexus of ancient myths.

D

D'Allession [Historical]

Biographical: Likely a variant of Massimo d'Azeglio (1798–1866), a key figure in the Italian Risorgimento—writer, painter, and moderate liberal politician from Piedmont. Prime Minister of Sardinia-Piedmont from 1849 to 1852.

Connection: Mentioned as the namesake of a political faction in Naples alongside followers of King Amadeus of Savoy and Joseph Bonaparte. Places the series within the competing monarchical and political claims of mid-19th-century Italy.

Damiata [Historical]

Damietta, a port city in Lower Egypt on a distributary of the Nile. Historically significant during the Crusades; by the 19th century its commercial importance had declined. A titular see—an episcopal see without a functioning diocese, often in non-Christian territory. The title of Archbishop of Damiata was conferred in partibus infidelium. Vincent Pecci (future Pope Leo XIII) identified as the young Archbishop of Damiata, a historically accurate appointment from 1843, signifying his diplomatic role as papal nuncio to Belgium.

Damisa the Leopard

A mottled African man Dana and Charley Oceaan encounter in Paramaribo, who joins their company. His name means "leopard," referring to his spotted skin, which he attributes to a pre-natal encounter with spirits. From the Haussa people, he is a magician, an adept of "unnatural animals" and preternatural phenomena, possessing non-European wisdom that often surpasses his companions'. His abilities are practical and mystical: producing needed items from nowhere, intuitive understanding of spiritual conflict. An Indian woman in Bolivia identifies him as "the Leper," one of "three angels riding" (with Dana and Serafino Tirana) sent to "alter history"—his mottled skin a physical manifestation of his nature as one "mottled between God and the Devil." A loyal companion, protector, and guide through metaphysical dangers, representing primal knowledge outside European intellectual frameworks.

Damnation

Not merely a post-mortem state but an active, terrestrial force and potential outcome of moral and spiritual conflict. The explicit goal of demonic figures like Ifreann Chortovitch, who seeks not just death but "death and damnation" of his adversaries. Tied to the loss of one's soul through philosophical or carnal capitulation to the "Omega statement" (that there is no God), a choice that realigns the individual with a destructive, anti-creative principle. A state of being "unmade" or losing one's essential nature. Escaping damnation is a central struggle, often hinging on grace, external intervention (as with Magdelena Brume saving Dana), and the protagonist's flawed but persistent orientation toward the "Green," or creative, pole of existence.

Damnation/Horror

The intersection of spiritual ruin and visceral terror. Damnation is not abstract theological proposition but palpable horror manifest in events. The ultimate horror is not physical death but the "depraving and despoiling of the soul when it is not permitted to escape," as exemplified by the boy Ifreann resurrects only to murder again with greater finality. Located in moments of extreme spiritual and physical violation: Ifreann's necrophilic rape of Catherine, the "unnatural" state of Elena Prado, the psychological torment of Ifreann's illusions. True horror is metaphysical: being trapped in a reality defined by a demonic will, where the soul's integrity is the ultimate battleground.

Damned

Those who have irrevocably chosen the "Red" or "Omega" principle—the nihilistic, anti-creative force embodied by Ifreann and his cohorts. Not necessarily posthumous; characters like Elena Prado are depicted as damned while living, having embraced "torrid satanism." Characterized by ontological inversion: they may possess power, intellect, even a form of vitality, but directed entirely toward destruction and the unmaking of Creation. Distinguished from the sinning: the former may fall from grace but remain oriented toward redemption, while the damned have fundamentally rejected this orientation. Ifreann, Son of the Devil, is the archetype—existing in perpetual, active opposition to the divine order.

Danae

Invoked as the mythical identity assumed by Achilles when hiding among women. Occurs during the minstrelsy on Basse-Terre, where Dana is symbolically covered in skirts by a dozen girls. Connects Dana to a classical motif of gender fluidity and concealed identity within a heroic context. By becoming Danae, Achilles (and by extension, Dana) paradoxically affirms his essential heroic nature (Danai) through its temporary occultation. Deepens Dana's multifaceted identity, suggesting his heroism is complicated and enriched by encounters with feminine principles and hidden states of being.

Danai

One of the names for the Greeks in the Homeric epics, referenced with the myth of Danae. When Dana is characterized as Achilles-as-Danae, he is also "one of the Danai whose maleness was beyond doubt." A signifier for archetypal, heroic masculinity. Establishes a classical precedent for the hero's complex identity, suggesting his temporary assumption of a feminine persona (Danae) does not negate but exists alongside his fundamental heroic nature as one of the Danai.

Danger

A constant condition operating on physical, political, and metaphysical levels. Physically: combat, assassination attempts, perilous journeys. Politically: the life of the revolutionary, caught between oppressive state powers and treacherous internal factions. Metaphysically: the peril of losing one's soul, being trapped by illusion, falling into damnation. Distinguished from simple physical risk, which characters like Dana embrace with "high hilarity," and true "death-danger," a state of profound spiritual peril embodied by Elena Prado and Ifreann. Navigating danger is not merely about survival but maintaining moral and spiritual integrity in a world actively hostile to it.

Daniel come to judgement

Title of the "little epic" or "song-story" performed at the minstrelsy on Basse-Terre. A composite narrative woven from contributions in multiple languages and traditions, its shape managed by the weaver Angelene Domdaniel. The epic's central figure is a "double person"—simultaneously "craven clown and great battler," "skirt-minded man and mighty-minded magician," "girl-rider and stallion breaker." Identified with Dana, who is cast as the "Daniel" whose dual nature must be judged. A ritualized exploration of Dana's paradoxical heroic identity, framing his personal conflicts within a mythic, universal context.

Dante Alighieri [Historical]

Biographical: Dante Alighieri (c. 1265–1321), Florentine poet, philosopher, and political thinker. His masterpiece, the Divine Comedy, presents an encyclopedic vision of medieval theology through an allegorical journey through Hell, Purgatory, and Paradise.

Connection: Invoked by Dana during the grotesque demise of Ifreann, who gorges on a rotten hog while bleeding from mortal wounds. Dana remarks that Dante, "still in his own Purgatorio," would be envious of this "hellish scene." Places events in dialogue with the classical Western depiction of Hell, claiming their own unique, perhaps superior, intensity of horror.

Dardanelles

Narrow strait in northwestern Turkey connecting the Aegean Sea to the Sea of Marmara. Along with the Bosphorus, separates Europe from Asia; of immense strategic importance for millennia, controlling access to the Black Sea. In the mid-19th century, central to the "Eastern Question." Napoleon's strategic assessment is cited: Russian control of the Dardanelles would place Russia "at the gates of Toulon, of Naples, and Corfu." The Crimean War (1853–1856) was fought precisely to prevent this. The geopolitical fulcrum upon which the fate of Europe pivots.

Darwin, Charles [Historical]

Biographical: Charles Robert Darwin (1809–1882), English naturalist, geologist, and biologist. His 1859 book On the Origin of Species established the theory of evolution by natural selection.

Connection: Cited as one of the key figures whose work defined a new "mechanistic world." His Origin of the Species grouped with works of Comte, Marx, and Louis Blanc as one of a "series of absurdities." The failure of the world to "laugh at this series of absurdities" made the triumph of a "totally atheistic and totally cruel premise... inevitable." Framed not merely as scientific theory but as a component of a larger intellectual shift toward a materialist, deterministic worldview that paves the way for the "Red Napoleon."

Dauphine

Former province of southeastern France, roughly the modern departments of Isère, Drôme, and Hautes-Alpes. Characterized by the French Alps, historically a strategically important border region. Referenced along with Piedmont as the location of "impossible trees" depicted by the painter Fragonard, suggesting a grove of these surreal, fantastical trees survived there into the 19th century. Links the real-world location to a sense of the magical and unreal, where the landscapes of art and reality might physically converge.

Day of Massacres

Used by Giuseppe Garibaldi for the era of bloody, revolutionary violence he pursued before his apparent transformation into a proponent of bloodless warfare. He declares to his Red Shirts that the "Day of Massacres has gone by," an order throwing them into consternation as it seemingly removes their very purpose. A pivotal shift in the nature of the Italian Risorgimento, from brutal but straightforward conflict to a more subtle and possibly more insidious form of conquest, orchestrated by a hidden, controlling genius.

De Vigny, Alfred [Historical]

Biographical: Alfred de Vigny (1797–1863), French poet, playwright, and leading figure in the Romantic movement. His work is characterized by philosophical pessimism, exploring themes of solitude, stoicism, and the alienation of the artist.

Connection: His poem "The Bottle in the Sea" (La Bouteille à la mer) is quoted as an epigraph to the chapter on Basse-Terre. The poem's image of a doomed sailor hurling a bottle containing a message into the sea serves as the defining metaphor for the island itself—a "bottle in the sea," a vessel containing a message (and "good storm-tossed liquor") that has drifted through time and space, embodying a legacy of survival and a message for the future.

Deans

Part of the Catholic hierarchy targeted by the Penal Laws in Ireland. The "Act for banishing all Papists exercising any ecclesiastical jurisdiction" specifically lists "Deans" alongside archbishops, bishops, Jesuits, and other clergy to be expelled from the kingdom before May 1, 1698, under penalty of transportation or death. Illustrates the comprehensive and detailed nature of the English suppression of the Catholic Church's institutional structure in Ireland.

Death

Fluid, recurring, and often non-final state. Characters are frequently killed, but their influence—and sometimes their actual presence—persists. Figures like Ifreann, Catherine, Elena Prado, and Count Cyril interact with the living after their supposed deaths, blurring the line between life and afterlife. Distinguished from "death and damnation," the destruction of the soul. The "High Hilarity of Blood and Death," associated with Elena Prado, treats death not as solemn finality but as a source of ecstatic, amoral energy. Not an end but a transition, a state from which characters can return, communicate, or continue to exert their will—mortality itself a contingent and negotiable condition.

Death and damnation

The ultimate stake in the central conflict. The explicit goal of Ifreann Chortovitch: "Ah, I will have you, Dana, by this bait to your death and damnation. The one without the other would leave me unsatisfied." Physical destruction is insufficient for the forces of evil; the true victory lies in securing the soul's eternal ruin. Elevates the conflict beyond mere physical or political struggle into a metaphysical war. The conjunction implies that damnation is the true "sting" of death, the horror that gives it ultimate meaning. The protagonists' battle: not merely to survive but to avoid the spiritual corruption that leads to damnation.

Death-danger

A specific state of peril distinguished from ordinary physical risk. Magdelena Brume warns Dana that Elena Prado is "death-danger to you, Dana my love." A threat not merely to the body but to the soul. A danger emanating from a person or situation that is ontologically corrupt or aligned with damnation. To encounter death-danger is to face a choice that could lead to spiritual as well as physical destruction. Unlike the "High Hilarity of Blood and Death," which embraces physical violence with ecstatic glee, death-danger is a more insidious spiritual threat challenging the hero's fundamental moral integrity.

Death/Nature

The finality of death intertwined with the cyclical and often violent processes of the natural world. "Muerte de Boscaje" (Death of the Thicket/Grove), an alias for Elena Prado, directly links a human agent of death with a natural setting. The natural world is not a passive backdrop but an active participant: the volcano Soufriere erupts during a sea battle, chalk cliffs crumble underfoot, "rotten" elements (putrid hogs, rank vegetation) serve as bait for demonic forces. A universe where moral and natural laws are interconnected, death as much a part of the landscape's character as of human destiny.

Decadence

A spiritual and philosophical state rather than purely aesthetic. Embodied in the "fleshy philosophy" of Elena Prado, described not as scattered but as "totally integrated, filled-in and complete" upon its own perverse premise. Decadence as a coherent worldview, a logical system built on the "Omega" axiom that there is no God. Characterized by inverted love, "torrid satanism," and embrace of the purely carnal as ultimate reality. Critiqued not as a failure of morals but as a rival, and ultimately sterile, metaphysical system.

Decay/Decline

Motifs pervading the landscape, both physically and culturally. The "rot" in the Irish ground, the potato blight, is a physical manifestation of deeper spiritual and political decay under the "Slave Centuries." Mirrored in the moral decay of characters who fall from grace, the decline of nations into chaos, the intellectual decline represented by absurdities like materialism. However, decay is not always negative; the smell of rot can become a "bit of strength" in a healthy ecosystem, and characters like the "rotten man" Joseph O'Boyle can achieve redemption. A dynamic process, a necessary precursor to either regeneration or final dissolution.

Deception

Fundamental tool in the political and metaphysical struggles. Ranges from overt trickery of Elena Prado and the "black fabricators" to subtle, world-shaping illusions of Ifreann and strategic misdirections of Dana. The "crookie tongues of Ireland" are the wellspring of both lies and legends, blurring the distinction between falsehood and creative myth-making. A weapon used by both sides: the Devil's party uses it to create confusion and despair, Dana's party for strategic advantage and survival. In a fallen world, navigating and employing deception is an essential, if perilous, component of action.

Deception/Identity

The instability of personal identity in a world of illusion and strategic misdirection. Characters frequently assume false identities, are mistaken for others, or possess multiple, contradictory personas. Dana is mistaken for Count Cyril; Elena Prado is also Muerte de Boscaje; the Spanish Man who recruits Dana is later revealed to be a shape-shifting Irish boy. Not merely a plot device but a reflection of deeper metaphysical uncertainty. Questions the existence of a stable, core self, suggesting identity is performative, contingent, and subject to the manipulations of powerful, will-imposing forces, both divine and demonic.

Deception/Intrigue

A world where intricate webs of intrigue drive societal and political actions. Hendaye is a "nests within nests of spies," a microcosm of a Europe animated by secret societies (Carlists, Carbonari), competing intelligence networks, and clandestine political maneuvering. The "two revolutions," Green and Red, operate through deception, employing secret agents, codes, and strategic misdirection. Intrigue as the primary mode of political action, reflecting a historical moment where overt power structures are challenged and subverted by hidden forces. History itself as a product of conspiracy and covert struggle.

Degradation

Both physical and spiritual process, a central experience in the "veritable underworld" of Ifreann's hellish party. "Loud degradation that cannot be put into words," involving not just vulgar and heroic acts but also "monsters," "illusion," and "reality betrayed." Characterized by the collapse of moral and aesthetic order, participants engaging in sadism and perversion framed as "literal hellishness." A temporary immersion in a chaotic, anti-creative state, a trial from which protagonists emerge marked but not entirely consumed, highlighting the resilience required to confront pure, unmediated evil.

Dehumanization of War

Analyzed in the aftermath of the Charge of the Light Brigade at Balaclava. The "end of wars as we knew them," marking a shift from "old heroisms" to impersonal slaughter. Driven by new technologies (minie rifles), new media (photography and war correspondents who invent phrases like "Someone had blundered!"), and a new "repugnance and loathing against personal death in war." The generals predict this will lead not to the abolition of war but to "immeasurably larger," more "hellish" conflicts, and an "easy acceptance of that impersonal death." Critiques the modern turn in warfare, where the individual heroic act is rendered meaningless and death becomes a statistical, dehumanized reality.

Delilah

Mythic archetype invoked during the minstrelsy on Basse-Terre. A "gay Delilah" locks Dana between her knees and shears his hair. Re-enacts the Biblical story of Samson and Delilah, casting Dana as the hero whose strength is ritually removed by a seductive female figure. Places Dana's struggles within a larger mythological framework of heroic vulnerability. In the carnivalesque minstrelsy, both playful homage and symbolic representation of the dangers and entanglements the hero faces.

Dementia

A state of collective, hypnotic irrationality, a key weapon of Muerte de Boscaje (Elena Prado). When she harangues the young Carlists, she does not use logic but creates "dementia" through "hypnotic rhythm, the corporate blood-beat." A state of willed madness, a surrender of individual reason to a powerful, unifying, but destructive passion. A powerful force in revolutionary movements, capable of overriding reason and leading followers into self-destructive acts under the spell of a charismatic leader. A perversion of noble cadence, corruption of inspirational rhetoric into a tool of ruin.

Democracy of Heaven

Concept dismissed as absurd by a beggar at a Christmas feast in Blois. Arguing for the necessity of a true king to mirror the "Kingdom of Heaven," he asks rhetorically, "Who ever heard of the Democracy of Heaven? Who ever heard of a Citizen God?" A reductio ad absurdum supporting a monarchist and hierarchical worldview. Reflects the deeply conservative, anti-democratic strain within the "Green" ideology, which posits that earthly political structures must mirror a divinely ordained, hierarchical, monarchical cosmos.

Demon

Real, active entities distinct from but related to the Devil and his direct offspring like Ifreann. Masters of illusion with tangible, though often subtle, interactions with humans. The old woman Elena Prado has "consorted with devils." In Carloforte, the false Dana O'Boyle is attended by the "demonic and moronic crew" of Ifreann. A world populated by a hierarchy of evil beings, demons acting as agents, familiars, or lesser powers in the cosmic struggle. A pervasive, corrupting influence that must be actively fought.

Demonic

A quality of being or action aligned with the forces of Hell. Not merely evil but possessing a supernatural or preternatural character. The mongoose familiar of Prince Dorn is demonic; Elena Prado is demonic; the laughter and crew of Ifreann are demonic. An intrusion of an alien, hostile, and powerful order into the human world. Characterized by illusion, inexplicable cruelty, and deep-seated opposition to the natural and divine order of creation. A constant threat requiring spiritual as well as physical vigilance.

Dembinska, Catherine

Dana's first wife, a Polish noblewoman and a key figure in the Green Revolution. Described as having a "corona-like flaming personality," the "only complete adept at the dogma of the Green Revolution." She marries Dana in Krakow and is brutally murdered eleven days later by Ifreann Chortovitch. After her death, she continues as a potent spiritual presence, her ghost inhabiting the ship named for her and acting as a guide and protector to Dana and a "ghost mother" to Carolina Tirana. The highest ideal of the "Green" cause: a fusion of intellectual brilliance, passionate commitment, and spiritual grace.

Deputy elected by the Lord

From Shakespeare's Richard II, used as an epigraph for "The Man with the Spanish Eyes Revisited." Refers to a king's status as God's chosen representative on Earth. Suffused with irony: while figures like kings and Count Cyril operate as deputies of a higher power, their authority is complex, often obscure, and subject to failure and replacement. The "deputy" is a vessel of a power not his own, resonating with Dana's role as an agent or "angel" who acts on instructions he does not fully understand from a master he has never clearly seen.

Devil

Distinct personality and active force in the world, ultimate source of the "Red" revolution and the "Omega" worldview. Father of Ifreann Chortovitch by the human woman Katie Noonan, establishing a lineage of incarnate evil. Engages in direct metaphysical conflict with God, contending for the title of "Lord of History." According to Kemper's Testament, the Devil was "unchained" around the mid-18th century after having been bound for a millennium, initiating a new era of evil. Acts through a "Hidden Hand," manipulating human organizations and individuals as puppets. A being of immense power, cunning, and cosmic ambition, the prime mover of the central conflict.

Devil Has Fallen, A

The "clanging tune and banishment" rung by the bells of Gate Castle after Ifreann Chortovitch commits an "anti-sin" by attempting to save Carolina Tirana from the collapsing roof he had engineered. Not a damnation (as he is already damned) but a disgrace within the demonic hierarchy, a failure of his essential, destructive nature. Earns him a "bitter banishment" and marks a moment of profound weakness and confusion. Illustrates the rigid, inverted morality of the demonic world, where an act of compassion or protection is the ultimate transgression, a fall from the state of pure evil.

Devil ship

A vessel controlled by or manifesting a demonic presence. Applied to Ifreann's ship, the Brami Piekielne (The Gates of Hell), after it is seen to burn without being consumed. Also used by Carolina Tirana to refer to the Untimely Encounter, the illusory ship captained by Ifreann. Acts under its own perverse laws, capable of inspiring terror and participating in supernatural battles. The maritime extension of the demonic, a floating piece of Hell that intrudes into the world's waterways.

Devil wounds

Ifreann Chortovitch claims to bear the "thirteen devil wounds" as a demonic parallel to the stigmata of Christ. These include wounds from twenty-one different crucifixions, the stake through the heart, a circular neck wound from decapitation, and a "foot-long belly wound." He presents these wounds as "holy, in our great and revered way," a perverse inversion of Christian martyrdom. A rival, demonic martyrology, a systematic suffering that mimics and mocks the passion of Christ, creating counterfeit sanctity for the forces of evil.

Devil's foot-print

Name for the five-grave arrangement used for the burial of the dismembered General Sabatelli. A "broken-down cross," a cross-form with both arms broken downwards at a forty-five-degree angle. A sacrilegious parody of a sacred symbol, a deliberate act of demonic desecration. The arrangement of the graves as a physical imprint of the devil's presence and mockery on the landscape, a signature of the evil forces at work.

Devil's know

A distinct epistemology associated with devils. When Ercule Tirana asks, "But how much do the Devil's know?", it raises the question of the nature and limits of demonic intelligence. This knowledge is vast and detailed but also flawed, lacking intuition, wisdom, and understanding of grace. Devils may know facts and mechanics, but they do not comprehend the essential nature of goodness—their ultimate blind spot.

Devil's People

Humans aligned with or belonging to the Devil. Charlotta Paradisio asserts they are color-blind, some having taken to wearing red shirts without knowing the color is red. Allegiance to the Devil entails a sensory and spiritual deficiency, an inability to perceive the true qualities of the world. A faction in the cosmic struggle, the human adherents to the "Omega" principle, who live in a state of perceptual and moral darkness.

Devil's sow, The

"The Devil's sow had ten thousand sons" is an "old saying" mocked by Carolina Tirana in conversation with Ifreann. Likely refers to the fecundity of evil. Ifreann's response—that he is unique among his "brothers" because he possesses "two natures together" and is "the other Incarnation"—places him above the common demonic herd. A folkloric baseline of demonic proliferation against which Ifreann asserts his own unique and superior status.

Devil, The (Satan, Diabolos)

The ultimate antagonist in the cosmology, given the names Satan and Diabolos, grounding him in Judeo-Christian tradition. A conscious, intelligent, and articulate being who communicates with his son Ifreann through "arrogant and murderous dreams," preaching a gospel of destruction, terror, and sadism. His goal: the complete overthrow of the divine order ("the Thing itself") and establishment of his own "wasteland." Though of immense power, capable of being outwitted or resisted, ultimately subject to a metaphysical order he cannot wholly subvert. A blend of traditional demonic attributes and a more modern, ideological malevolence.

Devil-beast

A being in a state of demonic, bestial manifestation. Applied to the savage stallion on Basse-Terre, more than a mere horse, a creature of "screaming thumping thunder" that must be overcome by Dana in ritualistic battle. Implicitly applied to Ifreann during his physical transformations into a monstrous, gluttonous creature. A fusion of the demonic and the animalistic, a creature driven by primal, violent instincts animated by a supernatural, evil force.

Devil-flesh

The physical substance of beings of demonic lineage, like Ifreann Chortovitch. Dana accuses Ifreann's ghost of being able to "leave and reenter your bodies easily" because people of the "devil-flesh" have a different relationship to physical incarnation than humans. An ontology in which the body is merely a temporary vessel, a "hulk" that can be inhabited or abandoned by a pre-existing evil spirit. Distinguishes such beings from humans, whose bodies and souls are integrally united.

Devildom

The realm, society, or collective existence of devils. "All devildom" is aghast at Ifreann's anti-sin, suggesting an organized community with its own standards and reactions. A world with its own geography, including "Third Babylon The City In The Sea," and its own hierarchies and politics. Not merely a synonym for Hell but the entire structure, culture, and sphere of influence of demonic beings as they operate both in their own realm and within the human world.

Devilish hours

The irregular and dissonant times at which the bells of Gate Castle peal, contrasting with the orderly ringing of the Angelus at Thorn Castle. A chaotic, anti-liturgical sense of time associated with the demonic. The disruption of sacred and natural rhythms, a hallmark of the evil force residing in Gate Castle under the influence of Ifreann and his predecessors.

Devilry

The characteristic actions, plots, and general mischief of devils. Encompasses a range of activities from petty, malicious pranks to grand, destructive conspiracies. A cunning and perverse intelligence at work, a delight in causing chaos, confusion, and suffering. The citizens of Basse-Terre venture down to Thieves' Town to see "what devilry had been done," associating the place and its inhabitants with this specific mode of evil action.

Devout Life

Introduction to the Devout Life by Saint Francis de Sales (1567–1622). Francis is a "chilly aristocrat" but there was "some warmth in his writings, especially" in this book. A classic work of Christian spirituality intended to guide laypeople in cultivating piety and devotion while remaining engaged in the secular world. The reference contrasts the book's perceived warmth with the author's reputed coldness, highlighting the paradoxes inherent in spiritual figures and their legacies.

Diaboli

Latin plural for "devils." Used by the red-bearded General Sabatelli, who speaks of the need to prevent "illogical and irrational and unnatural intrusions from the Diaboli" in order to allow the world to "work themselves out in peace and happiness." The formal, Latinate term frames the conflict in classical, theological terms, identifying the enemy not as a human faction but as the collective forces of Hell itself.

Diabolic

The nature or quality of the Devil (Diabolus). A state of being not merely evil but fundamentally and essentially aligned with the supreme antagonist of God. Used to describe Ifreann's laughter, his dabblings, and the "diabolical assassin" who attacks Elaine Kingsberry. The essence of intelligent, willful, and cosmically significant evil, distinct from lesser forms of malevolence or simple human sin.

Diabolical assassin

A human agent whose acts of murder are inspired or directed by a demonic power, elevating them beyond the status of a common killer. The man who attacks Elaine Kingsberry with an axe is so described. Not merely a criminal but a participant in the metaphysical war, a tool of the diabolic forces seeking to eliminate key figures of the "Green" opposition.

Diabolism

The practice, worship, or worldview centered on the Devil. A characteristic of figures like Garibaldi and Ifreann. Not a simple inversion of Christian belief but a complex system with its own "logic," rituals, and "diabolical epigrams." The intellectual and spiritual framework of the "Red" revolution, a coherent and seductive ideology of destruction that serves as the primary antagonist to the creative and restorative principles of the "Green."

Diabolists

Practitioners of diabolism. Ifreann was popular with the diabolists at Oxford "for they knew that he was one of theirs." At the Armageddon Conference on Basse-Terre, "all the diabolists and opportunists" gather to plot the "proversion of the world." The conscious adherents and agents of the Devil's cause, the intellectual and political foot soldiers of the Red Revolution.

Diabolite (personified element)

One of two "corrosive elements," along with Lenferite, that Ifreann and his monstrous minions exude while in their underwater forms. Named after the Devil and Hell (l'enfer), identified as the hellish versions of Phosphorus and Sulfur. A substance of supernatural origin that burns pursuers "in the eyes and in all the orifices and membranes." A physical manifestation of demonic essence, a weaponized element making the very presence of Ifreann's forces toxic and corrosive.

di Bijou, Adrian

Pseudonymous author of On Transfixing Devils, a satirical and esoteric underground booklet. Quasi-scientific demonology detailing the metamorphic physiology of devils and the precise method for immobilizing them by transfixing their nine major limb joints. A source of arcane, textualized knowledge—scholarly, almost Linnaean approach to the supernatural. Classic example of the series' blending of the grotesque, the academic, and the satirical.

Diaz, Porfirio [Historical]

Biographical: José de la Cruz Porfirio Díaz Mori (1830–1915), Mexican general and politician who served seven terms as President of Mexico, totaling 31 years (1876–1880, 1884–1911). His authoritarian rule, the Porfiriato, was marked by stability, modernization, and economic growth, but also political repression and deep social inequality leading to the Mexican Revolution.

Connection: Appears as one of the international revolutionaries congregating in Thieves' Town on Basse-Terre. Ambitious and aligned with the "Red Napoleon," Charles Cosmo. After the purge in Thieves' Town, he returns to Mexico, where the series presents a fantastical version of his rise: he defeats Benito Juarez in a "special election" of only seven voters, after which Juarez dies in a "shooting accident." Characterized as a ruthless agent of international conspiracy, seizing power through anti-democratic and violent means.

Dingle

Town on the Dingle Peninsula in County Kerry, Ireland. Situated on the Atlantic coast, the primary town of the Irish-speaking Gaeltacht of the peninsula. In the mid-19th century, a fishing port and market town, relatively remote but connected through maritime life to the wider world. One of the principal towns on Dingle Bay, part of the "Back Doors of the World," a region of rugged independence and historical connections to continental Europe, bypassing English control.

Dingle Bay

Large bay in County Kerry, southwestern Ireland. One of the three "Back Doors of the World," extending from Dunmore Head to Bray Head. Geographically situated between the Connor Hills and the Iveragh Mountains, fed by the River Maine. Its waters "gray and silver." The bay and its towns (Dingle, Anascaul, Castlemaine) a historical nexus of "illicit commerce" with "outlaw lands," from Viking-era Scandinavia to Arab Iberia. A landscape of resistance to central authority and a point of origin for Dana, whose skills and worldview are shaped by this environment.

Dinneen, Aileen (MacCobble)

Dana's Irish cousin, a pivotal figure in his early life who embodies the vital, cunning, and myth-making spirit of Ireland. Their "queer passion" precipitates Dana's departure from Bantry Bay. Powerful, green-eyed, possessing a "talented tongue," she orchestrates local events with shrewd humor. In Dana's long absence, she marries John Andrew MacCobble ("False Jack" Galopade), acquires land through clever dealing, and becomes a local power broker. Dana's deepest connection to his origins and the enduring, earthy power of the Irish landscape and its people. A fixed point in his personal history, the standard against which other formidable women are measured.

Disciple

An adherent to a major ideological or metaphysical force, specifically the Devil's. The foreign visitor in Paris speaks of the "twelve Devil's Disciples"—Schopenhauer, Marx, Comte, et al.—assembled to advance a destructive, materialist worldview. Co-opts a religious term to describe apostles of a rival, anti-Christian faith. These disciples are intellectually formidable and poetically seductive, making their mission of undermining the "Green" order particularly dangerous.

Dishonorably

A key moral concern presented to Dana. The old priest Croinin sees a vision of Dana "dying dishonorably" in Spain and warns him to think twice. The manner of one's death has profound spiritual consequences. A dishonorable death is not merely violent or unfortunate but involves betrayal of core principles or a fall from grace. Later, Croinin accuses Dana of having "died dishonorably in Spain" and "done it thoughtlessly" after his affair with Elena Prado, linking the concept directly to spiritual and moral fall.

Dismas, Saint (The Good Thief)

The traditional name for the "Good Thief" crucified with Jesus Christ, who repented and was promised Paradise. Invoked with the exclamation "By the Good Thief Saint Dismas, that coffin was heavy!" as the company carries the gold-filled coffin in Amsterdam. An appeal to the patron of thieves and the dying, characteristic Lafferty-esque irony: the "good" characters are engaged in a semi-licit enterprise involving mysterious treasure, calling upon the most famous penitent sinner for aid. The blending of the sacred and the profane, where even holy figures are associated with roguish activities.

Disraeli, Benjamin [Historical]

Biographical: Benjamin Disraeli, 1st Earl of Beaconsfield (1804–1881), British statesman, novelist, and central figure in the Conservative Party. Served twice as Prime Minister (1868, 1874–1880). Known for his charismatic personality, literary career, and political rivalry with William Gladstone.

Connection: Cited as author of "That fatal drollery called a representative government," used as an epigraph for the chapter on the Red Napoleon. Later mentioned as the Prime Minister replaced in December 1868 by Lord Gabriel Galbritain, an agent of conspiratorial forces. Used as a historical marker, anchoring fictional political machinations in real-world 19th-century British politics, his cynical aphorism underscoring the critique of conventional political systems.

Dissonance/Chaos

The state of the world under the influence of the "Red" principle. Manifests audibly in the "clattering and dissonant peals" of the bells at Gate Castle, contrasting with the orderly chimes of Thorn Castle. Present in the "towering unmelody" and "crooked revel" of Ifreann's hell-party, and in the "shattering thunder and the swallowing earthquakes" he unleashes. Politically, the chaos of the 1848 revolutions, a "farce" that nonetheless shakes the world. Not random but the deliberate product of a destructive will, an "engineered" state of disharmony intended to overwhelm and break down the order of creation.

Divine

The nature and agency of God within the narrative. A real, active, though often inscrutable, force. The divine is the ultimate source of the "Green" revolution and the creative "Alpha" principle. Divine intervention is recurring but often subtle or ambiguous, as in the "miracle" of Dana's vindication by the green rock, immediately undermined by Mariella's playful confession of trickery. Characterized by a "merry and clownish" aspect, suggesting a God not bound by human expectations of sobriety and seriousness, whose purposes are advanced through paradoxical and often chaotic means.

Divine Communication

The difficulty and ambiguity of communication between human and divine realms. Pope Pius IX complains of his "great difficulty in receiving communication from Christ," while Dana notes a similar difficulty communicating with Count Cyril, his semi-divine authority figure. A parallel between sacred and secular hierarchies: in both, the will of the highest power is often obscure, leaving agents ("vicars" or "deputies") to act on their own flawed judgment. Absolute certainty is rarely granted; faith and action must proceed in a state of partial knowledge and profound uncertainty.

Divine intervention

Critical, though often ambiguous, force in the narrative. Credited with Dana's "victory" over Elena Prado, his conversion of her from a state of damnation, occurring in a "special and spectacular form." However, the nature of this intervention is consistently questioned. A universe where God's action is not always direct or obvious, but works through human agents, flawed miracles, and events that blend the sacred with the absurd. A force that restores order and grace, but whose methods are frequently paradoxical and resist simple interpretation.

Divine Judgment
