An ongoing cosmic process rather than a single eschatological event. The world exists in continuous judgment, a conflict between forces of creation and destruction. The "two revolutions" embody this struggle. While the Devil presumes to be the "Lord of History," a higher judgment is always at play, acting through individual choices and actions. Explored through the constant testing of protagonists, who must align with the divine, creative principle ("Alpha") or risk falling under the sway of the nihilistic, destructive one ("Omega"), thereby participating in their own judgment.

Divinity/Cosmology

A complex cosmology based on a fundamental dualism. The universe defined by the conflict between two "possible statements": "Alpha: there is a God" and "Omega: there is not a God." Not mere propositions but ontological principles upon which rival "total systems" are built. Alpha associated with the "Green Revolution," creation, growth, divine order. Omega linked to the "Red Revolution," decadence, chaos, the "torrid satanism" of the Devil. Divinity as the source of the Alpha reality, a creative force mirrored, parodied, and actively opposed by the anti-divinity of the Omega system, creating a universe defined by irreconcilable struggle.

Divinity/Humanity

The relationship between divinity and humanity: intimate, paradoxical, and fraught with conflict. Humans are the battleground for the cosmic struggle between divine and demonic forces. Humanity is "mottled between God and the Devil." The divine is not a distant, abstract principle but an active force that can indwell in individuals, granting grace and power. However, humanity also possesses the freedom to reject this grace and align with the demonic. Encapsulated in the figure of the "deputy elected by the Lord," suggesting human beings are agents of divine purpose, yet their flaws and failures can disrupt or betray that purpose.

Divinity/Providence

An active but often inscrutable force guiding events. Frequently questioned, as when Dana says "how can it be providential when there is no providence?" in the context of the nihilistic worldview he has temporarily adopted. Often manifests through seemingly random events, "lucky" coincidences, and timely intervention of mysterious helpers. Scheherazade Jokkebrok acts as a human (or semi-divine) agent of providence, "making up" fates that align with a larger, beneficial design. Even in a world seemingly dominated by chaos and evil, an underlying purposeful order exists, though its logic may be beyond human comprehension.

Divinity/Spirituality

A practical and often perilous engagement with the divine and demonic forces permeating the world. Not quiet contemplation but active struggle. True spirituality requires navigating a world of illusions, battling demons (internal and external), and making a conscious choice for the "Alpha" principle of creation. Involves bizarre rituals, prophetic dreams, out-of-body experiences, and direct communication with saints, ghosts, and angels. The Green Scapular of Saint Cyril serves as a "private sign" of this active, militant spirituality, a commitment to fight in the "green battle" for the soul of the world.

Djinn

Part of the exotic and magical fauna of the world. The Professor at Greenfields tells a story of a "talking camel" in Egypt who was actually a Djinn serving a penance for a "great sin." Places the Djinn of Islamic mythology within the syncretic supernatural landscape. Broadens the cosmological scope beyond a purely Christian framework, suggesting a world populated by a diverse array of powerful, non-human entities with their own moral codes and systems of justice.

Dniester River

Major river in Eastern Europe, rising in the Carpathian Mountains of Ukraine and flowing through Moldova to the Black Sea. In the mid-19th century, its lower reaches formed part of the border of the Russian Empire. Ifreann Chortovitch's new, revolutionary type of ship is being built at Akkerman, near the mouth of the Dniester on the Russian Black Sea shore. Places the origin of this new technological and military threat deep within Russian territory, highlighting the geopolitical stakes of the looming conflict.

Doctor

Used with double meaning, referring to both medical practitioners and powerful, often morally ambiguous, figures who manipulate the fabric of reality. The series mentions: "Doctors, not understanding it, once called it the Subconscious; later, understanding it even less, they called it the Unconscious." The most significant figure is Richard Vif on Basse-Terre, a "red-headed black" man with a Paris medical degree who is also a "witch doctor" capable of perceiving supernatural ailments. A fusion of scientific training and occult knowledge, a key figure in navigating the island's paranormal phenomena, embodying a synthesis of modern and archaic forms of power.

Dog Latin

One of the hybrid languages used for communication on Bantry Bay in past eras, along with scraps of Norman, Old Norse, and Arabic. A debased, mock, or macaronic form of Latin. Its inclusion illustrates the bay's long history as a cosmopolitan crossroads. Reflects the general linguistic playfulness and interest in hybrid, creolized forms of culture, suggesting communication and understanding can thrive through imperfect and non-standard linguistic means.

Dogma

The essential, formulated belief system of a movement. Catherine Dembinska is the "only complete adept at the dogma of the Green Revolution." The Green Revolution is not merely a set of actions or sentiments but possesses a core of defined principles. Elevates the conflict from mere political struggle to a war of competing dogmas, with the coherent, creative dogma of the Green Revolution pitted against the destructive, nihilistic dogma of the Red.

Domdaniel

Surname of Angelene, the "Bride of Dana," who explains it as a pun (calembour) on the French La Demeure de Daniel (the House of Daniel/Dana). Historically, Domdaniel is a legendary subterranean or submarine cavern, headquarters of evil magicians. The name links to both a holy dwelling and a place of pagan or chthonic power. Angelene, as Angelene Domdaniel, embodies this fusion of the sacred and the primal, making her home and her person the site of a new, paradoxical sanctity.

Domdaniel, Angelene (Cosquin)

Dana's second wife, embodiment of the primal, chthonic spirit of Basse-Terre. A "seven-colored female from the seven bloods," a powerful seer and weaver who receives the "Green Revolution" directly "out of the Earth." Fuses earthy sensuality with profound spiritual wisdom. Dana's anchor to his new home and its magic. Mother of Tom, Dominic, Cyril, Francesca, and Brandon. The living, fertile, and magical power of the land itself, a force both creative and dangerous.

Dominion

Used by the "Black Pope" of the Carlist Hills as a fundamental principle of the divine order. Man, "made in the Image," is "given Dominion." This requires that each person "rule over himself in justice, and that he rule over the world in justice." Dominion presented not as exploitation but as divinely mandated responsibility for stewardship and establishment of justice. The failure of individuals to exercise their proper Dominion—to rule themselves—is identified as the source of all conflict. The theological foundation for the Carlist/Green political vision of a decentralized world of self-governing individuals.

Don Carlos [Historical]

Biographical: Infante Carlos María Isidro de Borbón, Count of Molina (1788–1855), second surviving son of King Charles IV of Spain. A staunch reactionary, he was the first Carlist claimant to the Spanish throne, challenging the accession of his niece, Isabella II, upon the death of his brother Ferdinand VII in 1833. His claim, based on the Salic Law (which his brother had abrogated), initiated the First Carlist War (1833–1839).

Connection: The historical figurehead for the "Carlist Thing," the deeply conservative and traditionalist movement central to the first part of the series. He "had never been a great favorite with the Carlists; he was not near Carlist enough for them." Separates the historical individual from the more potent, mystical ideology that bears his name, suggesting Carlism is a force transcending its nominal leader, rooted in deeper, archetypal traditionalism.

Donegal

The northernmost county in Ireland, located in Ulster. Known for rugged coastline, mountainous terrain, and strong tradition of Irish language and culture. In the mid-19th century, remote and impoverished, heavily affected by the Great Famine. The home county of Joseph O'Boyle, whose character reflects the region's tough, independent, and sometimes wild spirit. The contrast between O'Boyle of northern Donegal and Dana of southern Cork establishes a geographical and temperamental polarity within Irish identity itself.

Donncha

Possible original form of the name Dana. The series dismisses the idea ("No, it isn't," Dana had insisted), but introducing the possibility serves to ground Dana's unusual name in a more traditional Gaelic context. Donncha (anglicized as Donagh or Dennis) is a common Irish name meaning "brown-haired warrior" or "strong fighter." The allusion, even rejected by the character, subtly reinforces the heroic and martial aspects of Dana's identity.

Dood-Christus-Kerk

"Secret church" in Amsterdam, located on Doodkist Street (Coffin Street). Site of a "simple and profound miracle" on the Feast of the Circumcision, where the "Word was made Flesh." This church, hidden for centuries in a private house, is the persistence of faith in a hostile environment. Its name links death (Dood) and Christ, and its location on Coffin Street reinforces the theme of life emerging from death. The location for the sacred reunion of the "Company," where their mission is reaffirmed through a direct experience of the Incarnation.

Doodkiststraat (Coffin Street)

Fictional street in Amsterdam, running back from the waterfront north of the IJ River. Its name, meaning Coffin Street, establishes it as a place associated with death. Location of Scheherazade Jokkebrok's mortuary and the Dood-Christus-Kerk. A symbolic nexus where the mundane and metaphysical intersect. A place of literal death (the mortuary) but also spiritual rebirth (the church) and creative genesis (Scheherazade's world-making). The point of entry and departure for Dana's and Jack Galopade's journey into the "dislocated ocean," a threshold between conventional reality and other, stranger worlds.

Dooms-Day

Mentioned with the bell-ringing at Thorn Castle, where the ghostly bells speak to the Fallen Lion in "Dooms-Day Irish." The judgment and fall of a Holy Lion is an event of eschatological significance, a personal apocalypse. Frames the hero's personal moral failure not as private sin but as cosmic event, a moment when final judgment is brought forward into the present and communicated in a primordial, terrifying language.

Dorn, Prince

Handsome, olive-skinned, thirteen-year-old boy of aristocratic mien, presented as the grandson of the Devil and the pseudo-son of Ifreann Chortovitch. The human master of the King of Darkness Mongoose and appears able to exchange identities with his animal familiar. An antagonist to Tom Cosquin, Dana's son. A new generation of evil: suave, intelligent, deeply versed in the manipulation of natural and supernatural worlds. Sophisticated, inherited evil contrasting with the more primal monstrosity of his "father" Ifreann. His final fate is not determined, but he is established as a long-term adversary.

Dorrego, Manuel [Historical]

Biographical: Manuel Dorrego (1787–1828), Argentine statesman and military man who served as governor of Buenos Aires. A leading Federalist, he opposed the centralist constitution of 1826. Overthrown and executed without trial in December 1828 by the Unitarian general Juan Lavalle.

Connection: A "good man" whom Juan Manuel de Rosas had backed. His murder by Lavalle is presented as the catalyst for Rosas's rise to power, as Rosas "raised a Gaucho army and moved on Buenos Aires" in response. Establishes the historical context of brutal conflict between Federalists and Unitarians in Argentina and gives initial justification for Rosas's authoritarian and often violent rule.

Dragon gold

One of the names for the magical, transient coins given to lepers by the dead in the Mummy Caves on Basse-Terre. The coin changes its nature in the hand, becoming sequentially "leper gold," "lizard gold," "dragon gold," and "goblin gold" before disappearing entirely. A type of enchanted or fairy treasure that is illusory and cannot be held. A form of wealth belonging to another, non-human order of reality that cannot be integrated into the human economy.

Dromere Castle

One of the towns on Kenmare Bay in southwestern Ireland, a neo-Gothic castle built in the 1830s overlooking the Kenmare River in County Kerry. A relatively new and prominent landmark at the time. Its inclusion grounds the mythic landscape of the "Back Doors of the World" with specific, contemporary historical details.

Drunken men

A motif appearing in moments of heroic or divine revelation, suggesting a link between intoxication and spiritual insight. The Russian General, witnessing the Charge of the Light Brigade, asks "Who were those drunken men?", unable to otherwise comprehend their insane courage. In Ifreann's house, the four protagonists engage in a "crooked revel" fueled by Holland gin, the context for their confrontation with the Devil. A form of intoxication, a departure from sober reason, is necessary to engage with the chaotic and supernatural realities beneath the surface of the world.

Duality/Conflict

The structuring principle of the series's universe. Manifest in the cosmic war between God and the Devil, mirrored on earth by the political struggle between Green and Red Revolutions. Plays out through binary oppositions: Dana vs. Ifreann, Catherine vs. Elena, Thorn Castle vs. Gate Castle, Guayaquil vs. Quito, sanity vs. dementia, creation vs. destruction. These conflicts are irreconcilable; the world is a battleground where opposing forces must clash. Every character and event is ultimately defined by its allegiance within this overarching, dualistic struggle.

Duality/Heroism

Dana is the "Daniel" of the song-story, a "double person" who is both "craven clown and great battler." He embodies the tension between his fated, heroic role and his flawed, all-too-human nature. His identity is split between Ireland and Basse-Terre, between his past as a simple boatman and his future as Count Prasimos. This duality is the source of his strength and his vulnerability. True heroism lies not in idealized perfection but in the dynamic, often contradictory, struggle to reconcile the opposing facets of one's own nature.

Duality/Mortality

A dualistic understanding of mortality, constantly blurring the line between life and death. Characters exist in multiple states simultaneously: Ifreann is both dead (killed by Dana) and alive (active in the world); Catherine is both dead and a palpable presence on her namesake ship; Count Cyril communicates more freely after his death. A fundamental paradox of existence, where "splintered history leaves some very untidy pieces." Challenges a linear conception of life and death, suggesting a reality where both states co-exist and interact, where true existence is not contingent on physical vitality alone.

Duality/Revolution

The central political conflict: a duality between two rival revolutions, Green and Red. The Green Revolution, associated with Count Cyril, Dana, and the "Alpha" principle, is creative, restorative, agrarian, and spiritually grounded. The Red Revolution, associated with Ifreann, the Lord of the Flies, Charles Cosmo, and the "Omega" principle, is destructive, nihilistic, materialist, and demonic. Not merely competing political programs but "two mutually exclusive trains of events" rooted in opposing cosmological axioms. The entire historical and political landscape structured as a battle between these two fundamental and irreconcilable revolutionary impulses.

Duchies of Parma, Modena, and Lucca [Historical]

Three small states in north-central Italy existing as separate political entities prior to Italian unification. In the mid-19th century, under Austrian influence. The series mentions "peaceful" revolutions of 1859, during the Second Italian War of Independence, when their rulers fled and the territories were absorbed by the expanding Kingdom of Sardinia-Piedmont. Their fate is a key step in the bloody and contentious process of Italian unification, manipulated by figures like Garibaldi and Cavour.

Dumas, Alexandre [Historical]

Biographical: Alexandre Dumas, père (1802–1870), prolific and immensely popular French writer, best known for historical adventure novels including The Three Musketeers and The Count of Monte Cristo.

Connection: Dana and Catherine attend a performance of Dumas's play Le Chevalier de Maison-Rouge on the eve of the 1848 revolution in Paris. The play, with its romanticized depiction of the French Revolution, serves as an ironic backdrop to the "real" and farcical revolution brewing outside. The audience's enthusiastic response to the song "Mourir pour la patrie" ("To die for the fatherland") highlights the gap between theatrical patriotism and the confused, chaotic reality of historical events unfolding in the streets.

Dunmanus Bay

Bay in County Cork, Ireland, between the Mizen Head and Sheep's Head peninsulas. One of the long, narrow inlets characterizing the coast of southwestern Ireland. In the mid-19th century, a remote area dominated by fishing and small-scale agriculture. Onogherm Castle is located near Mizen Head, "just around the corner from Bantry Bay on Dunmanus Bay," grounding the legendary and vanished castles of the "Spanish Coast" in specific, real-world geography of West Cork.

Dunmanway

Market town in County Cork, Ireland, about halfway between Bandon and Bantry. In the mid-19th century, a regional center for trade and administration. The "higher brass of the army of occupation" in Cork can overrule the "lower brass at Glengarriff and Dunmanway." Establishes the local British military hierarchy and highlights jurisdictional complexities that figures like General Sabatelli must navigate, grounding fictional events in the concrete administrative structure of the British presence in Ireland.

Dunmore Head

On the Dingle Peninsula in County Kerry, the westernmost point of mainland Ireland. The western extent of Dingle Bay, described as "once the most westward place in the world, still the most westward place in the Old World." A geographical and symbolic position as a threshold, the "Back Door" of Europe looking out onto the Atlantic. A point of origin, a place from which the protagonist and his worldview emerge, shaped by the sea and the historical independence of this remote coastline.

Dursey Head

Southwesternmost point of the Beara Peninsula in County Cork, Ireland. Established as the geographical boundary separating Kenmare Bay to its north from Bantry Bay to its south. A key landmark in the series's initial, detailed mapping of the "Back Doors of the World." Precise geographical fixing serves to ground the subsequent mythic and historical narrative in a tangible, real-world landscape.

E

Eamg (Irish Weasels)

Irish weasels brought by Dana to Basse-Terre, initially as snake-killers. Function as agents within the parallel, non-human political sphere of the "Congress of the Animals." Their conflict with the Mongooses and subsequent trial and judgment by the Congress mirror the larger conflicts and moral compromises of the human world. Fierce, clan-loyal, operating under a barbarian code that clashes with the more "civilized" order of their antagonists. Angelene Domdaniel's adoption of them signifies an integration of primal, chthonic force into her sphere of influence, a synthesis of natural and supernatural power.

Earth spirits

Identified by Father Manzoni as the "familiars" of the dead bodies in the Mummy Caves of Basse-Terre. Manifest as large, chirping "Goblin-Lizards" or "Latina-Lizards," each seemingly attached to a specific corpse. Part of the island's chthonic supernaturalism, existing in symbiotic relationship with the dead. Not malevolent but an integral part of the island's unique spiritual ecosystem, blurring the lines between the animal kingdom, the spiritual realm, and the state of death.

Earthly Paradise

Both a geographical location and a spiritual state. The island of Basse-Terre is identified with the Terrestrial Paradise, its coordinates matching a historical chronicle's description. A place of stunning beauty, bountiful nature, and "Pragmatic Perfection," yet not a world without conflict or evil. Presented paradoxically: a true Paradise must contain snakes; it is a place of "high hilarity" and "bloody hopes," not static bliss. A microcosm of the world, a sacred battleground where the forces of creation ("Green") and destruction ("Red") contend for dominance, where humanity's choice between these principles is enacted.

Easter Sunday

Date of the "Resurrection of France," the 1849 general election with universal suffrage. Framing of a pivotal political event in liturgical terms is a key example of the series's syncretic worldview, where secular history is interpreted through a sacred lens. The election, occurring on the feast of Christ's Resurrection, becomes a symbolic (if ultimately mediocre) attempt at national rebirth. The Brumes' arrival in Paris is tied to this event, marking their re-entry into the European political struggle at a moment of ostensible renewal.

Eastern Marches [Historical]

Borderlands of Central and Eastern Europe, specifically the contested territories of the former Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, including Austrian-ruled Galicia (with Krakow) and the lands bordering the Russian and Prussian Empires. In the mid-19th century, a region of intense political instability, revolutionary plotting, and imperial suppression. A theater for both "Green" and "Red" Revolutions, a place of "excellent shooting" where Dana, Catherine, and Ifreann are fated to clash. A frontier of ideological conflict, a volatile landscape where the future of Europe is violently contested.

Ecclesiastical jurisdiction

The authority of the Church to govern its members and administer its affairs. The historical "Act for banishing all Papists exercising any ecclesiastical jurisdiction" in Ireland is cited as a key instrument of English oppression. This act targeted not just the clergy's spiritual acts but their entire legal and administrative authority. The suppression of this jurisdiction was a deliberate strategy to dismantle the institutional structure of the Catholic Church in Ireland, rendering it a decapitated and outlawed entity—a prime example of the "strangulations" that defined the Irish condition.

Eden of Italy

Used by Pope Pius IX to express his patriotic love for the Papal States. An idealized vision of his temporal realm as a unique and blessed land, the "Italy of all Italy." This perception stands in direct conflict with his theological understanding that the Papal States are temporal and ultimately unimportant compared to his spiritual vocation. Encapsulates the deep, often contradictory, pull of nationalism and faith, portraying the Pope's personal struggle as a microcosm of the larger conflict between the sacred and the secular animating the unification of Italy.

Egypt

A semi-autonomous province of the Ottoman Empire, undergoing significant modernization and asserting de facto independence under rulers like Muhammad Ali and his successors. A place of ancient and sometimes esoteric knowledge and power. The Professor on Basse-Terre has a "workable smattering" of Egyptian Arabic. More significantly, home of Al Azhar University in Cairo, one of the ancient centers of learning where the young Ifreann Chortovitch studied, placing it on par with European universities in his monstrously accelerated education and linking his diabolism to ancient, non-European sources of power.

Elect

Individuals chosen for a specific, often difficult, mission within the cosmic struggle. Used in a secularized, operational sense. The "salty elect" are those who find "salvation" in the trial of rounding the Horn. More broadly, those who can perceive reality beyond deceptive surfaces, such as the "urbanity and plausibility" by which the Devil's Disciples "deceive all but the elect". Not necessarily sinless but defined by fundamental alignment with the "Green" or divine cause and ability to withstand the powerful illusions of their adversaries.

Elect of God

Used with deep irony, referring to Oliver Cromwell. Appears in the opening of "Seven Heads Upon the Sea," which cites Cromwell's assertion that Irishmen have tails. By labeling the Puritan dictator one of God's Elect, the series immediately satirizes the self-serving, supremacist theology used to justify the brutal colonization and oppression of Ireland. Highlights the perversion of religious ideas into instruments of political and cultural warfare.

Electors

Twelve individuals tasked with selecting the successor to Count Cyril Prasimos. This body acts under a unique, preternatural rule: they can only cast their votes after their own deaths, following a consultation with the last deceased Count. Their testimonies are gathered from beyond the grave and validated by the Residential Governors of Greenfields. A form of authority that transcends mortal life, ensuring continuity and legitimacy through a spiritual, rather than political, process. A key element of the mystical and hierarchical structure of the Green Revolution.

Eminence of the Lions

A spiritual and physical state of being, a "high one," from which one can fall through moral transgression. Dana, as a member of the Brotherhood of the Holy Lions, achieves this state but loses it through seduction by the "Green Thorn." A condition of heightened grace, power, and spiritual responsibility. The fall from this eminence is a momentous event, a "death" in the role of Castle Lion, announced by the booming of the Thorn Castle bells.

Emperor

Title for both secular rulers (Napoleon III) and supernatural or archetypal figures. On Basse-Terre, an Emperor is expected at the Centennial Supper, with Prince Dorn claiming a place at his right hand, suggesting quasi-divine status for the office. More significantly, a title assumed by leaders of rival revolutions: Napoleon III is the flawed, human emperor caught between forces, while the "Red Napoleon" Charles Cosmo represents a new, demonic emperorship. The title becomes a locus of conflict, representing competing claims to ultimate worldly authority.

Enantitheos

Greek term meaning "the god who is opposed" or "the adversary god," invoked by Ashley, the spider-master. Along with Antitheos, used to address the destructive force represented by the dead bodies of Ifreann's agents. Frames the conflict in classical, theological terms, identifying the enemy not merely as evil but as a rival divinity, a power in fundamental opposition to the creative God. Reinforces the series's central dualism, portraying the struggle as a clash between two competing cosmic principles.

Enchanted

A state of being under a powerful, often demonic, spell or influence. Carolina Tirana is in an "unclean enchantment" and under "demonic damnation" while in her one-eyed, fishwife state. A loss of free will and alteration of one's essential nature by a hostile supernatural power. The breaking of enchantment is a central heroic task, requiring direct spiritual confrontation and often counter-magic or divine intervention.

Enchanted Horses

Invoked in the "Skibbereen Castle Prayer" alongside the Puca as supernatural beings whose intercession is sought for Ireland. The magical, pre-Christian elements of the Irish landscape and imagination. Part of the deep, often hidden, spiritual reality of the land, a force that can be appealed to in times of distress. Their inclusion in a prayer reflects the series's syncretic spirituality, where Christian and pagan forces are marshaled together in the struggle for Ireland's soul.

Enemy

A recurring term for the ultimate antagonist, a being that transcends specific individuals. The "Black Pope" identifies the Enemy as the sower of the "red blight" from the Beginning. Lady Valiente refers to Ifreann as the "enemy of my race and my soul." A proper noun designating the singular, primordial source of evil, a force manifesting through various agents—Ifreann, Rosas, the Lord of the Flies—but which is itself a unified and constant metaphysical reality. The architect of the Red Revolution and the ultimate opponent in the cosmic war.

Engels, Friedrich [Historical]

Biographical: Friedrich Engels (1820–1895), German philosopher, social scientist, journalist, and businessman. Founded Marxist theory together with Karl Marx, co-authoring The Communist Manifesto in 1848.

Connection: Mentioned as co-author of the Communist Manifesto, grouped with works of Comte, Darwin, and Blanc as one of the "series of absurdities" defining a new "mechanistic world." Positioned as a key component of the intellectual shift toward the atheistic and materialistic "Red" principle, the primary antagonist of the spiritual, creative "Green" revolution.

England

The world's foremost industrial, financial, and imperial power. Portrayed as the primary oppressor of Ireland, architect of the Penal Laws and the economic "strangulations" leading to the Great Famine. Also a center of political intrigue, where figures like Elaine Kingsberry and Lord Galopade operate, and a destination or point of origin for many characters. Despite its power, spiritually impoverished, lacking the deep, paradoxical vitality of Ireland or the passionate complexities of continental Europe. A source of both oppressive order and, through figures like Elaine, unconventional agents of the "Green" cause.

English Channel

Arm of the Atlantic separating southern England from northern France and joining the North Sea to the Atlantic. In the mid-19th century, the busiest shipping lane in the world. Referred to by its alternate name, the Strait of Dover; serves as the location for the meeting in Calais where the Company discusses its next moves. A geographical and symbolic crossroads, a point of transit between England and the Continent, where the series's international cast of characters converge and disperse.

English Coast

Name given to the shores of Waterford and Wexford Counties in southeastern Ireland, east of the "Spanish Coast" of Cork. A region having "a different sort of Castles" from the Spanish Coast. Not historical but part of the series's mythic mapping of Ireland. A region historically and culturally dominated by the English presence, contrasting with the more independent and Continental-facing character of the "Spanish" south and west.

Enmity

A foundational and enduring relationship, often existing alongside or as a perverse form of intimacy. The rivalry between Cardigan and Lucan, the conflict between Dana and Ifreann, and the ideological war between Green and Red revolutions are all defined by deep-seated enmity. Not simple dislike but ontological opposition. Ifreann declares to Prince Dorn that "there is enmity between us forever," establishing it as a fated, inescapable condition. A primary engine of the plot, a force structuring relationships and dictating action in a world defined by fundamental conflict.

Entre Rios

Province in the Mesopotamian region of northeastern Argentina, between the Paraná River and the Uruguay River. In the mid-19th century, a key Federalist province dominated by its powerful caudillo, Justo José de Urquiza. A lush, pastoral land of Ombu trees and pampas, heartland of Gaucho culture. Site of the climactic confrontation between Urquiza and Rosas, where Dana goes to precipitate Urquiza's betrayal of Rosas, setting in motion the final act of the Rosas dictatorship.

Ercule (Hercules)

Italian form of Hercules, mythic archetype of heroic strength and divine parentage. Applied to Ercule Tirana and, more significantly, to Gabriel Moreno, dubbed the "Christian Hercules." Frames Moreno's political mission in both classical and Christian terms, casting him as a modern hero destined to perform labors for the salvation of his nation. The archetype elevates the political struggles of Ecuador into a realm of mythic significance.

Escarpe

Small, fictional city in coastal Ecuador, characterized by extreme intellectual and artistic vitality. A city of nine hundred famous poets out of a thousand men, existing in a state of creative fervor. A microcosm of the "liberal and radical" coastal culture of Ecuador, a "light-minded port" in stark contrast to the conservative highlands of Quito. A place of prodigal brilliance and easy connivance, a source of the "floating world of intellectuals" that gives Ecuador its unique character.

Espartero [Historical]

Biographical: Baldomero Espartero, Prince of Vergara (1793–1879), Spanish general and politician. A leading figure in the Progressive Party, he served as Regent of Spain from 1840 to 1843 during Isabella II's minority. His rule was marked by liberal reforms but also authoritarian methods leading to his overthrow.

Connection: Mentioned as one of the "strong men" and "Dictators" ruling Spain during Isabella II's reign, alongside Narváez. The series notes his regime killed "twelve times as many persons as the Inquisition," condemning the supposed liberalism of the 19th century as more brutal than the notorious religious intolerance of the past. The violent and unstable nature of Spanish politics, a backdrop for Carlist conflicts.

Estella

Town in the Navarre region of northern Spain. Historically the principal court and headquarters of the Carlist claimants during the Carlist Wars of the 19th century. The center of the "high Carlist Hills," the locus of indoctrination and discipline of Carlist forces. Given a mystical etymology, derived from Star, linking the town to a "Star here that came down on a Mountain." Both the real-world military and political capital of Carlism and a symbolic center of the movement's spiritual and cosmological worldview.

Eternal

The realm of divine and unchanging reality, contrasted with the Temporal world of human affairs. Pope Pius IX articulates this distinction, stating that while his patriotic emotions are tied to the Temporal Papal States, his true "vocation is with the Eternal." The ultimate horizon of meaning and purpose toward which the "Green" revolution is oriented. The "final answers" given in the beginning are eternal, and the protagonists' goal is to grow toward them. The timeless, spiritual order standing in judgment over the chaotic and transient events of history.

Eternal Conflict

The struggle between good and evil, creation and destruction, is not a temporary condition of history but a permanent feature of existence. "The battle will now be in more subtle fields," Dana is told, but the conflict itself is unending. Not static opposition but a dynamic, ongoing war requiring constant vigilance and engagement. A tragic but heroic view of history, in which there is no final victory within time, only perpetual struggle to defend the "Green" and creative principle against an equally persistent destructive force.

Eternity

Conceived not as endless duration of time but as a qualitatively different state of being, outside time's linear progression. The realm in which "all the final answers were given in the beginning." Characters like the transformed Dana ("thyself are already outside of time") and supernatural beings can access or exist within this state. O'Boyle's odds for Dana on the cliff-face are "on the very knife-edge of eternity," suggesting moments of extreme peril can serve as a threshold to this timeless realm. The ultimate reality against which the fleeting and illusory conflicts of the temporal world are measured.

Eupatoria

Port city on the west coast of the Crimean Peninsula. In September 1854, site of the unopposed landing of British and French forces at the outset of the Crimean campaign. Mentioned as the opening move of the conflict. Its strategic location allowed the Allies to establish a beachhead from which they could advance south towards their primary objective, the naval base of Sevastopol.

Europe

In profound turmoil, a battleground for the "two revolutions." A world of ancient nations, decaying empires, and nascent nationalist movements. The narrative map of Europe is a complex web of political intrigue, from the "back doors" of Ireland to the revolutionary centers of Paris, the contested territories of Italy and the Germanies, and the volatile "Eastern Marches." The primary stage for the series's historical drama, a civilization poised between a restorative "Green" future and a destructive "Red" one, its fate hanging on the actions of a small company of metaphysical agents.

Eusibius, Hugh

Lawyer of the Greenfields Estate and one of the eight Residential Governors. His name, suggesting both "piety" (eusebeia) and the church historian Eusebius of Caesarea, ironically positions him as a pillar of a mystical and ancient establishment. Present at the discovery of the dead/wax figure of Joseph Jolif and offers a prosaic, clock-time account of the event immediately contradicted by Angelene's deeper, more intuitive timeline. A representative of the formal, legalistic order of Greenfields, whose understanding of events is limited to the surface level of reality.

Euxine Chersonese (Crimea) [Historical]

Ancient Greek name for the Crimean Peninsula. "Euxine" derives from Greek for "hospitable," an ironic euphemism for the Black Sea, originally called "inhospitable." "Chersonese" means peninsula. The use of this classical name, along with the more modern "Crimea" and the folkloric "Lucky Peninsula," layers the setting of the Crimean War with historical and mythological resonance. Frames the contemporary conflict as the latest chapter in a long history of struggle in this strategically vital and symbolically charged region.

Euxine Ocean (Black Sea) [Historical]

Large inland sea between Europe and Asia. The name is a Greek euphemism ("hospitable sea"), and the series notes its alternate, more ominous name, the "Lucky Ocean." In the mid-19th century, a crucial theater of geopolitical conflict, the warm-water outlet sought by the Russian Empire and contested by the Ottoman Empire, Britain, and France. A site of both historical warfare (the Crimean War) and supernatural conflict, where Dana and his companions battle Ifreann and where its "tempests" play an active role in unfolding events.

Evangelica dicta

Per evangelica dicta deleantur nostra delicta ("By the words of the Gospel may our sins be blotted out") is a prayer from the Roman Catholic Mass, traditionally said by the priest before reading the Gospel. The priest of Basse-Terre concludes his sermon with this prayer. Grounds the priest's idiosyncratic and somewhat heterodox sermon—with its discussion of the "Center" and the "eccentric"—firmly within the orthodox liturgical tradition. A closing formula reaffirming the ultimate source of grace and forgiveness despite the strange and turbulent local manifestations of faith.

Eve

First woman in Genesis, invoked by Angelene in a debate with Dana about the singular nature of the world-woman "Helen." When Dana insists there has been "only one woman in the world, except Catherine," Angelene counters, "How about the first woman in the world, Eve?" Dana resolves the challenge by subsuming her into the Helen archetype: "Oh, her real name has always been Eveline, which is Eve-Helen." Playful etymological move reinforcing the series's theme of a single, protean feminine archetype manifesting in multiple historical and mythological figures.

Evil

A real, active, and intelligent force, not a mere privation of good. Embodied in the Devil and his son Ifreann, and in the "Red Revolution" they promote. Philosophically rooted in the "Omega statement"—the assertion that there is no God—from which a complete, logical, but destructive worldview can be built. Acts through deception, illusion, violence, and the creation of "dementia." Distinguished between the "great" evil of supernatural beings like Ifreann and the more mundane, but still dangerous, evil of human agents like the "Red Napoleon." The central conflict is the struggle against this pervasive and multifaceted force.

Evil mind worm

Creature that Carolina Tirana claims has replaced the "blue bird" in her head. It "killed and ate the blue bird" and now serves as her source of secret knowledge and her guide. The transition from a benevolent familiar (the blue bird of happiness) to a malevolent one: her fall from simple grace into a more complex, dangerous, and miserable engagement with the world. A corrupt, parasitic form of intuition giving her knowledge of evil at the cost of her own peace and joy, aligning her with the "scapegoat" destiny she embraces.

Evil spirits

A category of supernatural beings that actively interfere in the human world. Malandrino Brume acknowledges that he and his wife must sometimes "kill" in their work, implicitly against such spirits' agents. Angelene notes that the dead woman whose eye was transplanted into Carolina's head was in Hell, her eye providing a window into a world of "bleak and evil spirits." A constant, corrupting pressure on humanity, forces of malice and disorder that the protagonists must perpetually resist.

Evil stars

A malign astrological conjunction, a fated time when destructive forces are in ascendance. The ex-Emperor Napoleon III predicts that after the current "bunch" of revolutionaries is gone, "very soon there will be another conjunction of evil stars." Frames historical events within a deterministic, astrological model, suggesting periods of intense evil and conflict are not accidental but foreordained by cosmic alignments. History as a cyclical struggle, with recurrent periods of heightened danger under the influence of these malign conjunctions.

Exile

A central condition of human existence. The "Black Pope" teaches that the "Empire is in abeyance, we live all our lives in exile," framing the temporal world as a place of alienation from the true, heavenly Kingdom. This theological exile is mirrored in the political and social exile of numerous characters: the Irish from their homeland, the Italian literary figures (the "External Exile") in Montevideo, the revolutionaries in Thieves' Town, the fallen Lion Dana from his state of grace. Both a spiritual and a literal state, the defining condition of the protagonist who is a perpetual wanderer, seeking a home always just beyond reach.

Expiating promontory sin

The purpose of the "scapegoat feet on rocky roads" described in the epigraph to "Carolina Irredenta." Refers to the ritual of atonement for a sin linked to a specific high place or "promontory." Connects to the larger theme of the scapegoat, embodied by Carolina, who takes on the sins of others. The promontory suggests a place of heightened spiritual significance, a natural altar where sins are committed or revealed, from which a corresponding act of expiation must be launched.

Eyre, General [Historical]

Biographical: Sir William Eyre (1805–1859), British Army officer. Served with distinction in various colonial conflicts and commanded a brigade of the 3rd Division during the Crimean War. Present at the major battles of the campaign, including the Alma and Inkerman.

Connection: One of the British brigadiers at a gathering in a yacht at Balaclava after the battle. Engages in discussion about the changing nature of war, addressing Ercule Tirana with a question about the consequences of the "Green or Prasinian Revolution" if wars become "immensely larger." Later, when General Sabatelli recalls "I knocked this big oaf flat once," encourages him to try it again on Ifreann: "It's worth a try, General Sabatelli." A representative of the professional military establishment, grappling with the new and disturbing realities of modern warfare.

F

Fabricators

Agents of deception, those who craft false narratives, illusions, and lies to advance the cause of the "Red" revolution. "Black fabricators" are a category of damned persons who construct the ideological and perceptual framework that supports evil. Their work contrasts with the "myth-making" of the Green side, which, while also involving creative shaping of reality, is oriented toward truth rather than its destruction. A world where the very fabric of perceived reality is contested, woven by competing groups of fabricators.

Facundo [Historical]

Biographical: Juan Facundo Quiroga (1788–1835), Argentine caudillo and Federalist leader from La Rioja province. Known for his charisma, brutality, and colorful personality, he was a dominant figure in the Argentine civil wars until his assassination. He became the subject of Domingo Sarmiento's famous work Facundo: Civilization and Barbarism.

Connection: Mentioned as one of the "legendary Federalists" whose memory is invoked in the political struggles of La Plata. The archetypal provincial strongman, a figure of raw, localist power whose legacy haunts and inspires the later conflicts between Federalists and Unitarians.

Failure

A recurring and often necessary experience for the protagonists. Dana fails to prevent the destruction of O'Boyle's inn, fails morally through his entanglement with Elena Prado, fails to permanently defeat Ifreann. These failures are not final but stages in a larger struggle. The "Green Revolution" itself is characterized by setbacks and partial defeats. Distinguished from the absolute failure of damnation. Paradoxically, failure can be a path to deeper understanding and eventual, qualified success. "We failed well enough," the Company concludes, recognizing that in a fallen world, perfect victory is impossible.

Fair play

A concept mocked by Ifreann Chortovitch. When Dana demands a fair fight, Ifreann responds that "There is no fair play with us," asserting his demonic nature is beyond such human conventions. Evil operates without constraints of honor or reciprocity. This asymmetry is a central challenge for the protagonists: they are bound by moral laws that their adversaries freely violate. Their struggle is not merely to defeat evil but to do so without becoming evil themselves, maintaining "fair play" even when it is not offered to them.

Fairy gold

Enchanted treasure that appears valuable but vanishes or transforms when removed from its enchanted context. Applied to the coins distributed by the dead in the Mummy Caves of Basse-Terre, which shift through various forms ("leper gold," "lizard gold," "dragon gold," "goblin gold") before disappearing. A class of illusory wealth belonging to another order of reality, unable to be integrated into the human economy. The limitations on human interaction with the supernatural and the ultimate insubstantiality of material gains that lack spiritual grounding.

Faith

The fundamental orientation toward the "Alpha" principle—belief in God and commitment to the creative, restorative order. Not mere intellectual assent but an active, lived commitment tested through trial and conflict. Enables perception of deeper realities hidden to the faithless. The priests of Basse-Terre possess a "faith of a singular sort," adapted to their unique environment. Catherine Dembinska's faith is so potent that it becomes a "blinding flash" at her death. Not passive belief but the active spiritual foundation for heroic action and metaphysical power.

Fall

Both the primal event in Eden and a recurrent pattern in individual lives. Dana's seduction by Elena Prado is a personal fall, a "dishonored death" from which he must be rescued. Aasel Castle's name change from Gabriel reflects a fall from angelic to demonic. The "Company of Doers" can suffer "falls" from grace and role. Not irrevocable damnation but a descent from a state of grace requiring intervention (divine, human, or both) for recovery. The world itself is in a "fallen" state, constantly tending toward decay and requiring perpetual effort to restore.

Fallen Lion

A member of the Brotherhood of the Holy Lions who has lost his state of grace through moral transgression. Dana becomes a Fallen Lion after his seduction by the "Green Thorn" (Elena Prado). Announced by the booming of the Thorn Castle bells in "Dooms-Day Irish," a public, cosmic event. A state of disgrace and spiritual death, but not necessarily damnation. The Fallen Lion may be restored, though the process is arduous and the original state may not be fully recoverable. A condition of profound shame and loss, yet also a starting point for redemption.

False Dana

A counterfeit identity assumed by or imposed upon Joseph O'Boyle. O'Boyle, believing himself to be Dana Coscuin—his own full name is Joseph Dana O'Boyle—is used by Ifreann as a "bait" in a trap for the true Dana. Part of a broader pattern of identity confusion and doubling central to the series. The False Dana serves as a dark mirror, a cautionary example of what Dana might become if he succumbs to the forces arrayed against him. The instability of identity and the demonic capacity to create convincing simulacra.

False miracle

Deceptive supernatural event designed to mislead or entrap. The "miracle" of Dana's vindication by the glowing green rock on Basse-Terre is immediately undercut by Mariella Cima's playful confession that she engineered it. In a world saturated with genuine supernatural activity, the possibility of manufactured or false miracles adds another layer of uncertainty. Protagonists must discern true divine action from demonic or human-made imitations, requiring spiritual as well as intellectual acuity.

Familiar

A supernatural companion or servant, often in animal form, linked to a human master. The "King of Darkness Mongoose" is Prince Dorn's familiar; the "blue bird" and later the "evil mind worm" serve Carolina Tirana. The "earth spirits" (Goblin-Lizards) in the Mummy Caves are familiars of the dead. A being that mediates between its master and a broader supernatural reality, providing knowledge, power, or service. The relationship is intimate and symbiotic, but can also be dangerous and corrupting, particularly when the familiar is of demonic origin.

Famine

The Great Irish Famine (1845–1852) is a defining catastrophe shaping the series's worldview. A "strangulation" of the Irish people caused not by natural disaster alone but by the policies and attitudes of the English occupiers. The "atheistic and Masonic English" felt no pity because they believed the Catholic Irish were "getting what they deserved." A genocidal event, a deliberate or at least callously accepted destruction of a people. The backdrop against which Dana's early life and the Irish portions of the narrative unfold, a wound that defines the condition of Ireland and the moral stakes of the conflict.

Famine-fever

Typhus and other epidemic diseases that swept through famine-weakened Ireland. Among the afflictions listed, along with "Famine-dropsy, Famine-diarrhea, and Famine-dysentery," as the actual killers of the Irish during the Great Famine. These diseases are not separate from the famine but its direct consequence, manifestations of the "potato sickness" that follows the destruction of the food source. A clinical terminology masking a human catastrophe, a medicalization of mass death that obscures its political and moral causes.

Farce

Applied to the Revolution of 1848 in Paris. Dana and Catherine attend a Dumas play on the eve of the revolution, stepping out to witness the chaotic streets. Though there is genuine tragedy (the killed insurgents), the revolution's overall character is farcical: poorly organized, confused, and ultimately ineffective in achieving lasting change. A recurring assessment of liberal, secular revolutionary movements, seen as mere parodies of the deeper, spiritual revolutions (Green and Red) that truly shape history. Surface chaos concealing the more significant metaphysical conflicts beneath.

Fate

A powerful but contested force. Characters are described as having "fates" (Dana is "fated to be a fabulous and many-named hero"), yet the narrative also insists on freedom and the capacity to alter destiny. Scheherazade Jokkebrok "makes up" fates, suggesting fate is not fixed but actively constructed. The tension between predestination and free will is central. Protagonists accept their fated roles but also struggle to shape and resist them, creating a dynamic interplay between cosmic design and individual agency.

Father

Multiple valences: the earthly father, the spiritual father (various priests and guides), and the divine Father (God). Dana's baptismal name—Dana Patrick Cyril Coscuin—carries his fathers within it; his true "paternity" is complex, resting on the patronage of Count Cyril Prasinos: heroes are "snatched up to heroism by the hair of their heads—as yourself by the Count Cyril Prasinos." The Devil is literally Ifreann's father. Paternity establishes lineage, authority, and identity, operating on physical, spiritual, and demonic planes simultaneously. The search for and relationship with the Father is a central concern, driving much of the action and defining the protagonists' place in the cosmic order.

Father of Lies

Traditional title for the Devil, invoked in the context of the series's emphasis on deception. Ifreann, as son of the Devil, inherits this capacity for falsehood. The "Omega" worldview itself is presented as a fundamental lie about the nature of reality. The cosmic conflict is, in part, a war over truth itself, with the Father of Lies and his agents working to obscure, distort, and ultimately destroy the truth of God's existence and the goodness of Creation.

Feast of the Circumcision

January 1st in the traditional Catholic calendar, commemorating the circumcision of Jesus eight days after his birth. The date of the "simple and profound miracle" at the Dood-Christus-Kerk in Amsterdam, where the "Word was made Flesh" for the Company. A moment of Incarnation, linking the specific event to the foundational mystery of Christianity. Grounds the Company's mystical experience in the liturgical calendar, connecting their particular journey to the universal narrative of salvation.

Federalists [Historical]

Political faction in Argentina and Uruguay, championing provincial autonomy against the centralizing Unitarios. Associated with caudillos (provincial strongmen), Gaucho culture, and a decentralized, agrarian vision of society. Aligned with the "Green" principle of localism and traditional order. Their red color is ironic given the series's usual color symbolism, but their ideology places them on the "Green" side of the conflict. Juan Manuel de Rosas, though a dictator, is their supreme leader and is treated ambivalently—a necessary defender of traditional order against a more destructive centralizing force.

Ferdinand VII [Historical]

Biographical: Fernando VII (1784–1833), King of Spain. His reign was marked by the loss of most of Spain's American colonies, political instability, and conflict between absolutists and liberals. His death triggered the succession crisis and Carlist Wars.

Connection: The king whose death and controversial succession arrangements sparked the Carlist Wars. By issuing the Pragmatic Sanction allowing his daughter Isabella to inherit, he excluded his brother Don Carlos and violated traditional Salic Law. The Carlist "Thing" is thus a reaction to his actions, a legitimist movement rooted in his perceived betrayal of dynastic principle.

Fib

A small, often playful lie. Dana employs fibs strategically, as when he claims to Mariella Cima that the child's coffin brought from Amsterdam contained his own remains. Distinguished from the more profound and destructive lies of the "Father of Lies," fibs are a tool of survival and misdirection in a dangerous world. Not morally equivalent to the fundamental deceptions of the enemy, but still a form of untruth that the protagonists must navigate carefully.

Fidelis servus

"Faithful servant," a Latin phrase encapsulating the ideal of loyal service to a higher authority. A key virtue for members of the "Company," who serve Count Cyril and, ultimately, God. Faithful service requires obedience even when the commands are obscure or the master's purposes unclear. Contrasted with the demonic parody of service, where minions like Ifreann's crew serve out of fear and compulsion rather than fidelity. The protagonists' struggle is to remain fidelis servus in a world of confusion and temptation.

Final answers

The ultimate truths of existence, given "in the beginning" and toward which humanity must "grow." The "Alpha" principle—that there is a God—is the foundation. These answers are not discovered through inquiry but received through revelation and tradition. The protagonists' task is not to find new answers but to align themselves with, and defend, the answers already given. A deeply conservative epistemology, valuing received truth over novel discovery, tradition over innovation.

Fire

A multivalent symbol. Destructive fire consumes O'Boyle's inn and threatens the Company at various points. Purifying fire is linked to Hell and judgment. Transformative fire is associated with volcanic eruptions (Soufriere) that intervene in battles. The fires of Ifreann's ship burn without consuming, a demonic perversion of natural law. The "burning" Brami Piekielne (Gates of Hell) is a floating embodiment of hellfire. Fire as a primal element, capable of being wielded by both divine and demonic forces, always signifying moments of intense spiritual and physical transformation.

First Bourbon

Henry IV of France, invoked by Dana as the exemplary king who provided the standard for good monarchy: "a chicken in every pot" on Sundays, and that the kingdom should follow his law, which is a just law. The standard of legitimate, beneficent kingship against which later monarchs and political systems are measured. Henry IV's practical, popular, and just rule is the ideal that Carlism and the "Green Revolution" seek to restore, contrasted with the tyranny of modern dictators and the chaos of revolutionary republics.

Fish

A recurring motif with multiple associations. Dana is characterized as a fish (a "mottled, powerful" one by an Indian woman in Bolivia). The Haussa like Damisa are linked to fish through their spotted skin and aquatic knowledge. Ifreann and his minions can transform into monstrous underwater forms. Fish are creatures of the "Back Doors," the hidden waterways and secret passages of the world. A symbol of the hidden, the primal, the adaptable—beings that navigate the depths and can survive in environments hostile to surface-dwellers.

Fishwife

The degraded, one-eyed form of Carolina Tirana encountered by Dana on Basse-Terre. A coarse, common woman, the antithesis of her former beauty and grace. This transformation is the result of her "unclean enchantment" and the demonic eye transplanted from a damned soul. A state of profound debasement, a parody of femininity and a living emblem of the corruption wrought by consorting with evil. Her restoration to her "two-eyed" self is a central goal of Dana's efforts.

Flag

A symbol of allegiance and identity. Dana famously plants various flags at key moments, claiming territories for himself and his causes. The Carlist flag is hoisted over the Skibbereen Castle. Garibaldi's Red Shirts have their own banners. The "Green Revolution" lacks a formal flag but is represented by the Green Scapular worn by its members. Flags are contested symbols; the act of planting a flag is a declaration of sovereignty and intent, a physical assertion of ideological and territorial claims in the ongoing conflict.

Flame

Linked to both passion and destruction. The "torrid" Elena Prado is associated with flame and heat. The burning Brami Piekielne is a ship of supernatural flame. The volcano Soufriere erupts in flame during the sea battle. Catherine's death is accompanied by a "blinding flash" of faith. Flame signifies moments of intense spiritual and physical transformation, a liminal element marking the boundary between states of being. Can be divine (the flame of faith) or demonic (the fires of Hell), depending on its source and purpose.

Fleas

A comical but pointed detail. The "Apostles" at O'Boyle's inn are described as having "a hundred fleas and lice on each of them." The material poverty and roughness of the boys who are nonetheless chosen for a sacred role. The series consistently elevates the lowly and grotesque, finding sanctity in unlikely places. Fleas and lice are markers of the protagonists' earthly, suffering condition, not obstacles to but paradoxical signs of their election.

Flesh

The material body, site of both spiritual struggle and incarnate presence. The "Word was made Flesh" at the Amsterdam miracle. Elena Prado's "fleshy philosophy" builds a complete worldview on carnal premises. Dana's "devil-flesh" is distinct from ordinary human flesh. The flesh is not inherently evil but a battleground: it can be the vessel of divine incarnation or the instrument of demonic seduction. The protagonists must navigate the demands of the flesh—hunger, desire, mortality—while maintaining spiritual integrity.

Fleshy philosophy

The worldview of Elena Prado, a complete and "totally integrated" system built on the "Omega" premise that there is no God. Characterized by the embrace of the carnal as ultimate reality, "torrid satanism," and the pursuit of sensory pleasure as the highest good. Not incoherent but internally logical, a seductive rival to the "Alpha" worldview. Its danger lies in its completeness; one who accepts its initial premise is led inexorably to its destructive conclusions. A sophisticated, philosophical evil, more insidious than mere brute malice.

Flight into Egypt

The biblical narrative of the Holy Family's escape from Herod's massacre (Matthew 2:13–23). Invoked in the context of the "Black Nativity" of Ifreann, which parodies this and other elements of the Christmas story. Also a model for the protagonists' own flights and exiles, their journeys through hostile territories in pursuit of a higher purpose. A typological link between sacred history and the characters' experiences, framing their struggles within a pattern established by the original holy refugees.

Floating Mortuary

Scheherazade Jokkebrok's establishment on Coffin Street in Amsterdam, a building of "wavering reality" at the heart of the city's metaphysical underworld. A place where the dead are prepared, but also a "thin-spot" where the veil between realities is permeable. The starting point for Dana and Jack's journey into the "dislocated ocean" and alternative realities. A threshold space, a gateway from the mundane world into the stranger, magical dimensions that underlie it. Scheherazade herself is its presiding spirit, a maker of fates and spinner of alternative histories.

Flores, General [Historical]

Biographical: Juan José Flores (1800–1864), Venezuelan-born general and first President of Ecuador. Dominated Ecuadorian politics during its early decades, serving multiple presidential terms and attempting to return to power repeatedly.

Connection: A key figure in the Ecuadorian political landscape. The series presents him as a recurring threat, someone whose ambitions must be countered. His conflict with Gabriel Garcia Moreno and other factions represents the ongoing struggle for Ecuador's soul between traditional, Catholic conservatism and secular, liberal forces.

Folk religion

A pervasive element on Basse-Terre and in the Irish sections of the series. Blends Catholic orthodoxy with local superstitions, African traditions, indigenous beliefs, and practical magic. The priests of Basse-Terre incorporate it; the Irish invoke Enchanted Horses and the Puca alongside Christian saints. Not a corruption of true religion but an adaptation, a local incarnation of universal spiritual truths. The series treats folk religion with deep sympathy, viewing it as a legitimate and powerful form of spiritual engagement, often more vital than the abstract formulations of official theology.

Fool

A complex figure, both comic and profound. Dana is cast as "craven clown" in the minstrelsy, his foolishness a disguise for or complement to his heroism. The "holy fool" tradition is invoked: those who appear foolish by worldly standards may possess deeper wisdom. The Basse-Terre "revel" and its "crooked" quality suggests a carnivalesque inversion where fools become wise and the wise are revealed as fools. An embrace of paradox, a recognition that true heroism and sanctity may look like folly to the uninitiated.

Forbidden ground

Territory that is spiritually or physically off-limits. The "Green Thorn" and its seductions are forbidden ground for the Holy Lion. Certain places (Thieves' Town, Gate Castle) are forbidden to those aligned with the "Green" cause. Transgression of forbidden ground leads to fall, corruption, or death. A moral geography in which space itself is charged with spiritual significance, where crossing certain boundaries has immediate and dire consequences.

Foreign influences

A constant theme in the political conflicts of both Europe and Latin America. European ideologies (liberalism, socialism, anarchism) are transplanted to the New World. The "angels of the Devil" are foreign operatives meddling in local affairs. The series views such influences with suspicion, preferring rooted, indigenous traditions to imported abstractions. The "Green Revolution" is itself international, but it works to support local, traditional structures rather than impose a foreign template. The conflict is partly between an authentic, localized order and a homogenizing, external ideology.

Forest

A wild, primal space of danger and transformation. The "Muerte de Boscaje" (Death of the Thicket/Forest) is one of Elena Prado's names, linking her to this environment. Forests are sites of ambush, illusion, and supernatural encounter. The "Grove" or "Thicket" associated with death is a specific, sinister variant. Contrasts with the cultivated garden imagery of the "Green Revolution"; forests are untamed nature, places where the ordered world gives way to chaos and primal forces.

Forgiveness

A powerful but demanding spiritual act. Dana attempts to confess his killing but cannot receive absolution because he lacks genuine remorse. True forgiveness requires authentic contrition. Yet forgiveness is also offered to the most degraded: Elena Prado is converted, Carolina Tirana is restored, O'Boyle is redeemed. Not cheap grace but the result of genuine transformation. Linked to the resurrection and renewal central to the "Green" cause; forgiveness is a form of spiritual regeneration, a defeat of the death and damnation sought by the enemy.

Fort of the Lilies (Fuertes de las Azucenas)

Fortress on the western coast of Basse-Terre, opposite Gate Castle. One of the "eight Spanish and French Forts" remaining from colonial times. Its name associates it with lilies, a symbol of purity and Bourbon royalty. Part of the island's defensive and symbolic geography, a strong point in the ongoing struggle between the forces centered on Thorn Castle and those emanating from Gate Castle.

Fortune

A fickle, often personified force in the characters' lives. Dana is a "man of many fortunes," experiencing dramatic reversals and windfalls. Fortune can be "made up" by figures like Scheherazade, suggesting it is not random but subject to supernatural manipulation. Distinguished from Providence, which is divinely ordered. Fortune is the realm of chance, luck, and contingency—a space where human agency and supernatural intervention interact unpredictably. Navigating fortune is a key skill for the protagonists.

Fragonard [Historical]

Biographical: Jean-Honoré Fragonard (1732–1806), French Rococo painter known for his exuberant, playful, and hedonistic works, often depicting scenes of love, nature, and aristocratic leisure.

Connection: Referenced for his paintings of "impossible trees" in the Dauphine and Piedmont regions. The series suggests that these fantastical trees were real, a grove of which survived into the 19th century. Art as a window into hidden realities, the paintings not mere imaginative fancies but records of an enchanted world that persists beneath or alongside the mundane.

France

A major power in political and cultural turmoil throughout the 19th century. Site of the Revolution of 1848 (a "farce"), the Second Republic (the "Resurrection of France"), and the Second Empire under Napoleon III. A cockpit of revolutionary ferment, from which ideas and agents spread across Europe and the world. Paris is a recurring setting, a center of intrigue, art, and ideological conflict. France is also a source of military power (the Chasseurs d'Afrique in the Crimea) and colonial ambition (Basse-Terre is a French possession). A nation embodying the contradictions of the modern era: revolutionary and reactionary, enlightened and brutal, creative and destructive.

Francis de Sales, Saint [Historical]

Biographical: Francis de Sales (1567–1622), Bishop of Geneva and Doctor of the Church. A key figure in the Counter-Reformation, known for his gentle, humanistic approach to spirituality. Author of Introduction to the Devout Life.

Connection: Described as a "chilly aristocrat," though his writings, especially Introduction to the Devout Life, possess "some warmth." A complex figure blending personal aloofness with literary warmth, offering a model of sanctity distinct from the fiery passion of other saints. His work provides spiritual guidance for laypeople—a key concern given the series's focus on non-clerical agents of the Green Revolution.

Freedom

A contested value, understood differently by the rival revolutions. The "Green" freedom is rooted in obedience to legitimate authority (God, the true king, Count Cyril) and the exercise of one's proper "Dominion" over self and world. The "Red" freedom is libertine, a rejection of all constraints, leading ultimately to chaos and enslavement to base passions. The Penal Laws and English colonialism are denials of freedom; so too is the demonic domination of Ifreann. True freedom is found in alignment with the creative order, not in its rejection.

French language

One of the primary languages of the narrative, spoken by educated characters across Europe and the Americas. The language of diplomacy, culture, and much of the "floating world of intellectuals." Basse-Terre's Creole is French-based. Many key texts, conversations, and interjections are in French. A marker of cosmopolitanism and education, distinguishing the international elite from more localized, vernacular communities. The series incorporates French naturally, reflecting the linguistic reality of the 19th-century educated class.

Friar

Members of mendicant religious orders (Franciscans, Dominicans, etc.) who play various roles in the narrative. "Friars minor" (Franciscans) are among the targets of the Penal Laws in Ireland. Friars appear as teachers, confessors, and agents of the Church in both Europe and the Americas. Part of the institutional structure of the Catholic Church, they provide spiritual services and serve as nodes in the network of the "Green" cause. Distinguished from secular priests and monks, friars are itinerant and closer to the people, often operating at the margins of official power.

Friend

A crucial relationship in a world of shifting allegiances and constant danger. Dana's friends—Catherine, O'Boyle, the Brumes, the Tiranas—form the "Company" that is his primary support and context for action. Friendship is tested by the pressures of the conflict: O'Boyle's transformation into the "False Dana" strains his bond with the true Dana; Catherine's death leaves Dana bereft of his closest companion. True friendship, like faith, is a commitment that persists through trial and provides a foundation for heroic action. Demonic forces cannot form genuine friendships, only alliances of convenience and fear.

Frog

A creature appearing in the supernatural fauna of Basse-Terre. The "Goblin-Frog" Dorobbo is one of the principals at the Congress of the Animals, representing the "old families" of amphibians. Frogs, like other non-human creatures, are participants in a parallel political and spiritual order, with their own interests and allegiances in the cosmic conflict. Their presence reinforces the series's vision of a world where all creation is animate and engaged in the struggle.

Funeral

A ritual marking the transition between life and death, but in this series, often a site of supernatural activity and narrative significance. The funeral of Ifreann's victims, the disposal of General Sabatelli's dismembered body in the "Devil's Footprint," and the mortuary practices of Scheherazade Jokkebrok all show death rituals as moments of power and peril. The funeral is not an end but a threshold, a rite that may or may not properly guide the soul to its destination, subject to manipulation by both divine and demonic forces.

Fury

Intense, often divine or demonic, wrath. Dana is capable of great fury in battle, a "battle happiness" that sometimes crosses into something darker. Elena Prado's passions are furious in their intensity. The forces of nature—storms, volcanoes—can manifest fury as instruments of cosmic conflict. A double-edged quality: fury can be righteous (the wrath of God against evil) or destructive (the demonic rage of Ifreann). Protagonists must channel their fury appropriately, directing it against genuine enemies without being consumed by it.

G

Gabriel Castle

Original name of Aasel Castle on Bantry Bay before its corruption. Named for the Archangel Gabriel, its transformation to "Aasel" (an arch-devil) marks a spiritual fall mirrored in its physical and metaphysical nature. The castle's name change is a microcosm of the larger pattern of corruption afflicting Ireland and the world—sacred things perverted to demonic purposes, angelic protection replaced by infernal occupation.

Galbritain, Lord Gabriel

Agent of the conspiratorial forces who becomes British Prime Minister in December 1868, replacing Disraeli. His name combines "Gabriel" (angelic) with "Britain," suggesting a false or parodic guardian of the nation. Part of the network manipulated by the "Hidden Hand" of the Devil, his ascent to power marks a victory for the forces of the Red Revolution in British politics. A puppet of larger, darker forces, his rule advances the mechanistic, atheistic agenda threatening the world.

Galicia [Historical]

Historical region in Central Europe, part of the Austrian Empire during the 19th century. Encompassed much of what is now southeastern Poland and western Ukraine, with major cities including Krakow and Lemberg (Lviv). A multi-ethnic region of Poles, Ukrainians, Jews, and Germans, it was a hotbed of nationalist sentiment and revolutionary activity. Part of the "Eastern Marches" where Dana, Catherine, and Ifreann are fated to clash, a volatile borderland central to the geopolitical struggles of the era.

Galopade, False Jack

Name for John Andrew MacCobble after his marriage to Aileen Dinneen. Distinguished from "True Jack" Galopade, the original bearer of the name and Dana's fellow survivor of the Catherine Dembinska. A "half-sized horse dealer" and "land-dealer," he acquires property and position through Aileen's clever maneuvering. His epithet "False" suggests his secondary status relative to the "true" figures in Dana's life, a stand-in or substitute rather than an original. Represents the pragmatic, earthbound side of Irish life, prospering through cunning and commerce while the great metaphysical struggles rage elsewhere.

Galopade, Jack (True Jack, Lord Jack Galopade)

The real Jack Galopade, as distinguished from "False Jack" (John Andrew MacCobble who took his identity). True Jack survives the sea battle and swims with Dana for three days, acquiring an "Ocean Change" that leaves both men with "a touch of the strange fishiness" for life. He marries Elaine Kingsberry, daughter of Lord Kingsberry "who has possibly the finest mind in England." Lord Jack Galopade is appointed Governor of the British Island of St. Lucia, from which position he assists Dana's network. The couple visits Dana at Seven Heads Castle, often traveling with Lord Joseph Peter Barbary (Barbary Joe). True Jack somehow knew about his doppelganger False Jack for years, "and neither would ever regret it."

Galopade, Lord

English nobleman, member of the Company, and husband of Elaine Kingsberry. A figure of the British establishment who has aligned himself with the "Green" cause, demonstrating that the revolution transcends national boundaries. His title and position provide cover and resources for the Company's activities. The marriage links Galopade to the English aristocracy through the Kingsberry line—her father "has possibly the finest mind in England"—creating a hybrid node within the Green network.

Gamble

A central metaphor for the protagonists' engagement with their fated roles. The "Green Gamble" is the risky commitment to the restorative revolution, requiring action without certainty of outcome. Dana is "born for the adventure, for the Green Gamble," his very existence oriented toward a cosmic wager. Distinguished from mere chance, the gamble involves skill, courage, and faith—staking everything on a venture whose outcome depends on factors beyond one's control. Life itself as a gamble between salvation and damnation, creation and destruction.

Garcia Moreno, Gabriel [Historical]

Biographical: Gabriel García Moreno (1821–1875), Ecuadorian statesman and two-time president (1861–1865, 1869–1875). A devout Catholic and conservative, he sought to establish a theocratic state, dedicating Ecuador to the Sacred Heart of Jesus. Assassinated by liberal opponents.

Connection: The "Christian Hercules" of Ecuador, a key figure in the "Green Revolution" in South America. His vision of a Catholic, traditional Ecuador directly opposes the liberal, secular forces of the coast. Dana and his companions work to support Moreno's cause, seeing in him a champion of the creative, divine order against the destructive Red Revolution. His eventual assassination (historical) is foreshadowed as the martyrdom of a saint.

Garden

The central metaphor of the "Green Revolution." The world is a garden to be cultivated, protected from the "red blight" of destruction. Contrasts with the wild forest or thicket associated with death and chaos. The garden requires tending—active human participation in the divine plan of creation. Basse-Terre, despite its dangers, is a kind of earthly paradise, a garden island. The "Black Pope" speaks of the Enemy sowing blight in the garden "from the Beginning," framing the cosmic conflict as an ongoing struggle over cultivation versus corruption.

Garcia, Calixto [Historical]

Biographical: Calixto García Íñiguez (1839–1898), Cuban revolutionary general who fought in all three Cuban wars of independence against Spain.

Connection: Arrives in Thieves' Town on Basse-Terre, having abandoned his earlier belief in an "unbloody movement" for a more radical "bloody revolt of everybody against everybody else." The island as a nexus for international revolutionaries, gathering figures from disparate global conflicts.

Garibaldi, Giuseppe [Historical]
