Biographical: Giuseppe Garibaldi (1807–1882), Italian general, patriot, and central figure in the Risorgimento. His "Expedition of the Thousand" (1860) conquered Sicily and Naples, contributing to Italian unification. A republican and revolutionary, he became an international symbol of nationalist struggle.

Connection: A complex, troubling figure in the series. Initially a man of blood and "massacres," he undergoes a transformation, declaring the "Day of Massacres has gone by." This change bewilders his Red Shirts and suggests he has become a puppet of deeper forces, possibly the "Red Napoleon" Charles Cosmo. His diabolism and "Garibaldine epigramism" mark him as aligned with the destructive principle, though his exact role remains ambiguous. A symbol of how even genuine nationalist passion can be co-opted by darker powers.

Gate Castle

Fortress on the western coast of Basse-Terre (St. Peter's Island), opposite and rival to Thorn Castle. The seat of demonic power on the island, formerly held by Donatien Marie Doré, then by Doré's puppet, and contested throughout the narrative. Its bells ring at "irregular and devilish hours," contrasting with Thorn Castle's orderly Angelus. The "Gate" in its name suggests a threshold or portal—to Hell, to chaos, to the destructive principle. The physical locus of the Red Revolution's presence on the island, a fortress that must be contained or conquered for the Green cause to prevail.

Gates of Hell (Brami Piekielne)

Ifreann Chortovitch's ship, named in Polish. A vessel that burns without being consumed, crewed by demonic and monstrous beings. The floating embodiment of Hell's power, it intervenes in the sea battles off Basse-Terre and in the Black Sea. Its name invokes the biblical "gates of Hell" that shall not prevail against the Church, here literalized as a warship of the Enemy. A mobile piece of the infernal realm, projecting demonic power across the world's waterways.

Gaucho

The iconic horsemen of the Argentine and Uruguayan pampas. In the mid-19th century, the backbone of Federalist armies and the embodiment of a rugged, independent, pastoral way of life. Juan Manuel de Rosas rose to power leading a Gaucho army. The Gauchos represent the localist, traditional order valued by the "Green" principle—rooted in the land, resistant to centralizing, urban, liberal ideologies. Their culture, skills, and loyalty are resources for the protagonists in their South American operations.

Generals

A recurring class of characters, representing military power and its moral complexities. Historical generals (Eyre, Bosquet, Canrobert) appear alongside fictional ones (Sabatelli). They are often caught between the demands of their profession and the deeper spiritual conflicts underlying the wars they fight. The series portrays generals as men of considerable intelligence and honor, yet frequently bewildered by the new, dehumanized nature of modern warfare and the hidden metaphysical forces shaping events beyond their understanding.

Genius

A term for exceptional, often dangerous, intellect. Ifreann Chortovitch is a "genius among devils," his brilliance directed toward destruction. The "Red Napoleon" Charles Cosmo is described as a genius manipulating world events from the shadows. Genius is not inherently good; it can serve either the Alpha or Omega principle. The series is wary of genius unmoored from moral and spiritual foundations, seeing it as a potential instrument of great evil. True wisdom, embodied by figures like Catherine Dembinska, is distinguished from mere intellectual power.

Geography

Not merely physical terrain but a moral and spiritual landscape. The "Back Doors of the World" (the Irish bays), the "Eastern Marches," the "Earthly Paradise" of Basse-Terre—all are places where geography and metaphysics intersect. The series maps a world where certain locations are nodes of power, thresholds between realities, or strategic points in the cosmic conflict. Travel through this geography is always fraught with significance; moving from place to place is moving between spiritual states and zones of influence.

Germany/Germanies

In the mid-19th century, not a unified nation but a collection of states dominated by Austria and Prussia. A source of philosophers (Schopenhauer), revolutionaries, and military power. The "Germanies" are part of the European chessboard, their eventual unification (under Prussian leadership) a major event in the reshaping of the continent. German thinkers are among the "Devil's Disciples" promoting the mechanistic, atheistic worldview. A region of immense potential, its future direction a matter of concern for the forces of both Green and Red revolutions.

Ghost

A common presence in the narrative, the spirits of the dead who remain active in the world. Count Cyril, Catherine Dembinska, Kemper Gruenland, and others communicate from beyond death. Ghosts are not mere remnants but continuing participants in the cosmic struggle, capable of providing guidance, information, and even physical intervention. The boundary between life and death is permeable; ghosts are proof that existence extends beyond mortality and that the dead retain their allegiances and purposes.

Gibraltar

British territory at the southern tip of the Iberian Peninsula, controlling the strait between the Atlantic and Mediterranean. Home to the famous Barbary Apes (actually macaques), one of whom, Lord Joseph Peter Barbary ("Barbary Joe"), is a talking, intelligent creature and companion of Lord Jack Galopade. Gibraltar's apes are revealed to speak a form of colloquial Spanish, a discovery made by the pioneering linguist Archer Leyland and suppressed by the academic establishment. A liminal place, a point of contact between Europe and Africa, civilization and the wild, where the impossible becomes possible.

Gift

Supernatural abilities or objects granted to characters, often with strings attached. Dana's gifts include his heroic destiny, his connection to Count Cyril, and his capacity to perceive hidden realities. Gifts can also be curses in disguise; the "Achilles' thumb" is a malediction transferred from Dana to his enemy. The giving and receiving of gifts is a key form of supernatural transaction, establishing relationships and obligations that transcend ordinary exchange. True gifts come from the divine order; demonic "gifts" are traps and corruptions.

Gladstone, William [Historical]

Biographical: William Ewart Gladstone (1809–1898), British statesman who served four terms as Prime Minister. A Liberal, he championed Irish Home Rule, educational reform, and expansion of the franchise. His rivalry with Disraeli dominated Victorian politics.

Connection: Mentioned in the context of British political machinations. His policies toward Ireland, while more sympathetic than many of his contemporaries, are still part of the system of English domination that the series critiques. A figure of the establishment whose reforms, however well-intentioned, cannot address the deeper spiritual and political crises afflicting Ireland and Europe.

Glengarriff

Village in County Cork, Ireland, situated at the head of Glengarriff Harbour on Bantry Bay. In the mid-19th century, a small settlement in a region of striking natural beauty. Part of the local British military administration, with "lower brass" stationed there. A specific point on the map of the "Back Doors of the World," grounding the narrative in the concrete geography of West Cork while participating in the larger mythic landscape of the series.

Glory

A state of divine radiance and honor, the ultimate goal of the "Green" cause. The protagonists fight for the "glory of God" and the restoration of a world reflecting divine order. Glory is also a personal quality, the heroic reputation that figures like Dana accumulate through their deeds. Distinguished from mere worldly fame, true glory is spiritual, an alignment with and participation in the divine light. The "High Hilarity of Blood and Death" pursued by Elena Prado is a counterfeit glory, an intoxicating but ultimately destructive parody.

Gnostic

A term applicable to certain elements of the series's cosmology, particularly its dualism and emphasis on hidden knowledge. The struggle between Alpha and Omega, God and Devil, has Gnostic overtones, suggesting a world created or corrupted by an inferior or evil power. The "secret" nature of the Green Revolution, the importance of initiation and hidden wisdom, and the contempt for the material world expressed by some characters all echo Gnostic themes. However, the series ultimately affirms the goodness of Creation and the Incarnation, distinguishing it from thoroughgoing Gnosticism.

Goblin

A class of supernatural creature appearing on Basse-Terre. "Goblin-Lizards" or "Latina-Lizards" are the earth spirits familiars of the Mummy Cave dead. "Goblin-Frogs" like Dorobbo participate in the Congress of the Animals. "Goblin gold" is one of the transient forms taken by the coins of the dead. Goblins occupy the boundary between the animal, mineral, and spiritual worlds, creatures of the chthonic depths embodying the island's rich, strange, supernatural ecology.

God

The ultimate source of the "Alpha" principle, the creative and restorative order underlying reality. An active but often inscrutable force, working through flawed human agents, providential coincidences, and moments of miraculous intervention. The series affirms God's existence as the foundational truth ("Alpha: there is a God") against which the nihilistic "Omega" worldview is measured. God's purposes are advanced through the "Green Revolution," though His methods may appear paradoxical, "merry and clownish," or even cruel to limited human understanding. The struggle is ultimately for or against God, and all other conflicts derive from this central, cosmic one.

Gold

A recurring symbol of wealth, temptation, and transformation. The child's coffin from Amsterdam—"quite the heaviest in the room"—is filled with gold, treasure of ambiguous origin and purpose. The coins in the Mummy Caves shift through forms of enchanted gold before vanishing. Gold seekers like Otis Ranker represent the materialistic drive that the "Green" worldview critiques. Gold can be a resource for the protagonists' mission or a snare drawing them toward corruption. Its true value depends on its use—in service of the divine order or in pursuit of worldly gain.

Good

A real, active force embodied in the "Alpha" principle and the "Green Revolution." Not a mere absence of evil but a positive, creative power working through individuals, institutions, and events. Good is demanding, requiring commitment, sacrifice, and often suffering from its adherents. The "Company of Doers" are agents of good, though flawed and capable of failure. Good's ultimate triumph is assumed but not guaranteed within historical time; the struggle is perpetual, and good must be actively defended and advanced against the constant pressure of evil.

Good Thief

Saint Dismas, the penitent thief crucified with Christ who was promised Paradise. Invoked as patron and model for the protagonists' own morally ambiguous activities. His example demonstrates that redemption is possible even at the last moment, even for those whose lives have been defined by transgression. The "good" in Good Thief is crucial—he is not merely a thief who got lucky but one who, in his final moments, recognized truth and aligned himself with it. A figure of hope for the fallen, including Dana after his own "falls."

Gospel

The Christian scriptures and the message they contain, invoked at the conclusion of sermons and in theological discussions. The priest of Basse-Terre ends his sermon with the prayer that "by the words of the Gospel may our sins be blotted out." The Gospel is the authoritative source of the "final answers" given in the beginning, the foundation of the "Alpha" worldview. Its truths are not discovered but received, transmitted through tradition and liturgy, providing the stable reference point against which the chaos of history is measured.

Government

A problematic institution in the series's political vision. "Representative government" is dismissed by Disraeli (quoted in an epigraph) as a "fatal drollery." The governments of England, Spain, France, and the Latin American republics are portrayed as corrupt, oppressive, or ineffectual, often manipulated by the "Hidden Hand" of demonic forces. Legitimate government, in the series's view, is monarchical, rooted in divine sanction and traditional authority, not the product of revolutionary or democratic innovation. The "Green Revolution" seeks not to abolish government but to restore it to its proper, sacred form.

Governor

An administrative role with varying valences. The eight "Residential Governors" of Greenfields are custodians of a mystical estate and witnesses to supernatural events. Colonial governors (like Guerchin of Basse-Terre) wield political power but are constrained by the island's unique spiritual ecology. The Governor-God of the series's theology rules the cosmos, with earthly governors as His deputies. Proper governance requires alignment with divine order; governors who fail this test—through corruption, atheism, or demonic influence—are illegitimate, regardless of their formal authority.

Grace

Divine favor enabling spiritual transformation and heroic action. Grace is necessary for salvation, for resistance to demonic seduction, and for the extraordinary feats required of the protagonists. It can be received through sacraments, through direct divine intervention, or through the mediation of holy figures like Catherine Dembinska. Grace is not earned but given, though it requires a receptive disposition—genuine contrition, faith, and openness to transformation. The loss of grace (as in Dana's "fall") is catastrophic but not necessarily permanent; restoration is possible through repentance and renewed reception of divine favor.

Grass

A recurring image associated with the "Green" principle. The world is a garden where grass (and all growing things) must be cultivated against the "red blight." Grass represents the humble, persistent, life-giving force of creation—easily trampled but always returning. The "green" of grass, fields, and growth is the color of the restorative revolution, contrasting with the red of blood, destruction, and the demonic. An elemental symbol of the natural order aligned with God's creative purpose.

Grave

Not merely a place of interment but a threshold and a symbol. The "Devil's foot-print" is an arrangement of five graves forming a broken cross. The Mummy Caves contain graves from which the dead interact with the living. Scheherazade's mortuary is a place where graves are prepared but also where alternative realities are accessed. The grave is the boundary between life and death, but in this series, that boundary is permeable. What lies in the grave may rise, speak, or exert influence; the grave is a point of contact, not of final separation.

Great Famine

The Irish Famine of 1845–1852, caused by potato blight and exacerbated by English colonial policy. A defining catastrophe for Ireland and for the series's worldview. Approximately one million died; another million emigrated. Presented not as natural disaster but as deliberate or at least callously permitted genocide, the culmination of centuries of "strangulations." The Famine is the historical wound from which the Irish portions of the narrative emerge, shaping Dana's character and the conditions of his homeland. Its horrors are the immediate context for the desperate resistance and exile that define the Irish experience.

Greece

In the mid-19th century, a recently independent kingdom carved from the Ottoman Empire. A nation of ancient glory and modern struggle, its independence supported by the Romantic movement and Great Power intervention. Greece is referenced through its classical heritage—the Danai, the Achilles myth, Greek terminology—more than its contemporary politics. The Aegean Sea and the broader "Eastern Question" involve Greek interests. A place where ancient myth and modern reality intersect, a potential theater for the conflicts between Green and Red forces in the eastern Mediterranean.

Green

The color and principle of the restorative revolution. Associated with growth, life, Ireland, and the creative "Alpha" order. The "Green Revolution" is the movement led by Count Cyril Prasinos to defend and restore the world against the destructive "Red" principle. Green is the color of the scapular worn by members of the Company, the verdure of the garden they cultivate, the emerald of Ireland itself. It represents hope, renewal, and the persistent vitality of creation against the forces of decay and death.

Green Gamble

The risky commitment to the restorative revolution, the wager that the protagonists make with their lives. Dana is "born for the adventure, for the Green Gamble," his existence oriented toward this cosmic bet. The gamble involves staking everything—life, soul, worldly security—on the uncertain outcome of the struggle against evil. It is not reckless but a calculated risk, informed by faith and guided by mysterious authorities. The Green Gamble is the protagonist's vocation, the adventure for which they exist, accepting radical uncertainty in service of a higher cause.

Green Revolution

The cosmic and political movement to restore the world to its proper, divinely ordered state. Led by Count Cyril Prasimos and carried out by the "Company of Doers," it opposes the destructive "Red Revolution" of the Devil and his agents. Characterized by agrarianism, monarchism, localism, and traditional religion. Not a single historical event but an ongoing struggle across time and space, appearing in different forms (Carlism, Federalism, Catholic restoration) in different contexts. The Green Revolution is creative, restorative, rooted—working with the grain of Creation rather than against it.

Green Scapular of Saint Cyril

A devotional object worn by members of the "Company," marking their allegiance to the Green Revolution. Named for Count Cyril, it is a "private sign" of commitment to the restorative cause. The scapular is a traditional Catholic sacramental, here adapted for a specific, quasi-secret purpose. Wearing it signifies membership in the Company and willingness to fight in the "green battle." A badge of identity, a source of spiritual protection, and a link to the network of agents spread across the world.

Green Thorn

Elena Prado's alias or aspect, the seductive force that causes Dana's fall from his status as Holy Lion. The "Green Thorn" is a paradoxical image—green (the color of life and the revolution) combined with thorn (pain, the crown of thorns, danger). She is "forbidden ground" for the Lion, a temptation that appears aligned with his cause but is actually its corruption. The name suggests that even within the "Green" principle, there are dangers, false paths that lead to spiritual ruin. Dana's seduction by the Green Thorn is his gravest personal failure.

Greenfields

The mystical estate in England that serves as a headquarters and spiritual center for the Company. Governed by eight Residential Governors, it is the site of key events including the discovery of Joseph Jolif's body and the verification of Dana's election as Count. The name embodies the "Green" principle—fields of growth and life. Greenfields is a protected space, a remnant of the sacred order in the midst of a corrupted world, a place where the rules of ordinary reality are subtly different and the supernatural is closer to the surface.

Gruenland, Kemper

Author of a Testament that provides key theological and historical interpretations within the series. His work describes the "unchaining" of the Devil around the mid-18th century and articulates the theory of the "two revolutions." Though dead, Kemper continues to develop his Testament from beyond the grave, its contents growing over time. A source of esoteric wisdom, his writings are a key text for understanding the metaphysical framework of the narrative. His name, meaning "green land," aligns him with the restorative principle.

Guadeloupe

French Caribbean island, administratively linked to Basse-Terre (which is one of its constituent islands in reality, though the series treats it as a distinct place). Part of the colonial French West Indies, a region of plantation agriculture, slavery (abolished in 1848), and complex racial and cultural mixing. The broader Caribbean context for Basse-Terre's unique character, a world of Creole culture, colonial administration, and tropical environment that shapes the island's distinctive society and supernaturalism.

Guardian

A protective role, both human and supernatural. Dana serves as a guardian for various characters and causes. The Brotherhood of the Holy Lions are guardians of Gate Castle. Guardian angels and spirits are implied in the providential protection some characters receive. The guardian's role is to defend against threats, both physical and spiritual, to maintain the integrity of what is guarded. It is a sacred trust, and failure in guardianship—as when Dana falls from his role as Castle Lion—has profound consequences.

Guayaquil

Principal port city of Ecuador, located on the Pacific coast at the mouth of the Guayas River. In the mid-19th century, the commercial and liberal heart of Ecuador, in constant tension with the conservative, highland capital of Quito. Described as "light-minded," a place of intellectuals, poets, and cosmopolitan influences. The rivalry between Guayaquil and Quito embodies the broader conflict between coastal liberalism and highland conservatism, a microcosm of the struggle between Red and Green principles in Latin America.

Guerchin

French colonial governor of Basse-Terre. A "friendly atheist," he represents the secular, administrative face of French rule on the island. Despite his atheism, he is not hostile to the protagonists and may even be a quiet ally. His position requires navigating the island's unique spiritual ecology—its folk religion, its supernatural manifestations, its complex social structure. A pragmatic figure, more concerned with maintaining order than with metaphysical struggles, yet inescapably drawn into them by virtue of his office.

Guilt

A burden carried by many characters, a consequence of their participation in violence, deception, and moral compromise. Dana's guilt over his killings, his affairs, his failures, is a recurring theme. Guilt can be a path to repentance and grace, but it can also be paralyzing or self-destructive. The series distinguishes between healthy guilt (leading to contrition and amendment) and morbid guilt (leading to despair or rationalization). Forgiveness is available, but it requires genuine acknowledgment of wrongdoing—hence Dana's inability to receive absolution when he lacks true remorse.

Gunpowder

The technology of modern warfare, enabling the violence that pervades the narrative. Battles are fought with muskets, cannons, and pistols. The "gunpowder fog" of combat obscures vision and judgment. Gunpowder represents the mechanization of killing, the industrial capacity for destruction that the series views with ambivalence. It is a tool that can serve either revolution, used by both the protagonists and their enemies. The smell and smoke of gunpowder pervade the battle scenes, a sensory marker of the violent world the characters inhabit.

H

Haiku

Japanese poetic form invoked in the context of Basse-Terre's multicultural intellectual life. The Professor at Greenfields mentions it as one of the forms practiced by the island's literati. An exotic cultural import, part of the "floating world of intellectuals" that circulates through the series's cosmopolitan settings. Its inclusion signals the global reach of the narrative's cultural references, incorporating Asian as well as European and American traditions.

Halo

A sign of sanctity or supernatural status. Catherine Dembinska is described as possessing a kind of spiritual radiance. More ironically, the "halo" of gunpowder smoke surrounds combatants in battle. The series plays with the concept, suggesting that true holiness may not be visible to ordinary eyes, while false or parodic halos (the glamour of violence, the charisma of evil) may deceive. The halo is a visual marker of the sacred, its presence or absence a sign of spiritual alignment.

Hand

An instrument of action, both human and divine. The "Hidden Hand" is the Devil's agency in the world, manipulating events through human puppets. The "hand of God" or providence intervenes at crucial moments. Dana's hands are instruments of violence and creation, killing and building. The hand that plants a flag claims territory; the hand that signs a document creates legal reality. Hands are synecdoches for agency itself, the point where intention becomes action in the material world.

Happiness

A state often associated with "battle happiness" or the "High Hilarity" of combat and adventure. Dana experiences a fierce joy in fighting that borders on the ecstatic. This happiness is distinguished from the peace and fulfillment promised by the "Green" order—a deeper, more stable state aligned with divine purpose. The happiness of the protagonists is often fleeting, interrupted by tragedy and loss. True happiness, in the series's vision, requires alignment with the creative order, not merely the excitement of conflict.

Harbor

A place of arrival, departure, and transition. The harbors of Bantry Bay, Basse-Terre, Montevideo, and other ports are key settings where characters meet, part, and embark on new phases of their journeys. Harbors are liminal spaces, thresholds between land and sea, the known and the unknown. They are also sites of commerce, intrigue, and cosmopolitan mixing. The harbor is where the "Back Doors of the World" open onto the broader seas, a point of access to the global network of the series's action.

Hatred

A powerful but spiritually dangerous emotion. The hatred between Dana and Ifreann is ontological, rooted in their fundamental opposition. Hatred can motivate heroic resistance but can also corrupt and consume. The series distinguishes between righteous opposition to evil and the corrosive personal hatred that damages the soul. Ifreann's hatred is pure and destructive; Dana's must be tempered by his alignment with the creative principle. Hatred is a weapon that can wound its wielder as much as its target.

Haussa

West African ethnic group from which Damisa the Leopard comes. The Haussa are depicted as possessing deep spiritual knowledge and practical wisdom, a tradition of engagement with "unnatural animals" and supernatural phenomena. Damisa's Haussa heritage is the source of his magical abilities and his unique perspective on the conflicts the protagonists face. A repository of non-European wisdom, the Haussa represent an alternative tradition of spiritual power that complements and enriches the predominantly Christian framework of the Green Revolution.

Healing

Both physical and spiritual restoration, a key goal of the "Green" principle. Richard Vif, the "witch doctor" of Basse-Terre, combines Western medicine with supernatural healing. Spiritual healing—the restoration of fallen characters like Dana, Elena Prado, or Carolina Tirana—is a central narrative concern. Healing is not automatic; it requires the right conditions, often including divine intervention, and is always contingent and partial. The world itself is wounded and in need of healing, a task the Green Revolution undertakes on a cosmic scale.

Heart

The seat of the will, emotions, and spiritual orientation. A character's heart determines their fundamental allegiance to the Alpha or Omega principle. The "stake through the heart" is one of Ifreann's devil wounds, a physical mark of his demonic nature. Dana's heart is tested through temptation and trial; his falls are failures of the heart, his recoveries its restoration. The "Sacred Heart" devotion (to which Garcia Moreno dedicates Ecuador) is a Catholic practice honoring Christ's love, the ultimate model for the properly ordered heart.

Heaven

The realm of God and the blessed, the ultimate destination of the saved. Heaven is invoked as the "Kingdom" that earthly kingdoms should mirror, the source of the hierarchical, monarchical order that the Green Revolution seeks to restore. The "Democracy of Heaven" is dismissed as absurd, reinforcing the series's anti-democratic political theology. Heaven is also the origin of divine communications, the source of the "final answers" and the authority delegating power to figures like Count Cyril. A real place and a governing principle, the standard against which all earthly arrangements are judged.

Heel

Invoked through the myth of Achilles, whose heel was his fatal vulnerability. The "Achilles' heel" motif is transformed into the "Achilles' thumb," a magical malediction transferred from Dana to his enemy. The heel (or thumb) represents the point of weakness in even the greatest hero, the vulnerability that enemies exploit. The series plays with this classical motif, showing how mythic attributes are actively manipulated within its magical economy, curses and blessings transferred between characters in the ongoing struggle.

Helen

The archetypal woman—"there has been only one woman in the world, except Catherine"—whose name appears across history in various forms: Helena, Ellen, Elaine, Aileen, Eveline. Angelene suggests that even Eve was really "Eve-Helen" (Eveline). This single feminine archetype manifests in different individuals: Aileen Dinneen, Elaine Kingsberry, Elena Prado, and others. Helen is the face that "launched a thousand ships," the embodiment of feminine beauty, power, and danger. The series's female characters are understood as local incarnations of this universal principle, variations on an eternal theme.

Hell

The realm of the Devil and the damned, actively present and intervening in the world. Hell is not merely a post-mortem destination but a current reality with its own geography, inhabitants, and politics. The "Third Babylon, The City In The Sea" is a location in Hell with a "weak connection" to Aasel Castle. Ifreann's ship is named Gates of Hell. Devils, demons, and damned souls operate from Hell as agents in the cosmic war. Hell is the source of the "Omega" principle, the anti-creative force seeking to unmake the world and drag souls into its domain.

Hendaye

Town on the French side of the Franco-Spanish border, at the mouth of the Bidasoa River. In the mid-19th century, a critical point of passage for those entering or leaving Carlist territory. Described as a "nests within nests of spies," a place of intense intrigue where the agents of multiple powers converge. Dana's journey into the Carlist Hills begins here. A liminal space, a threshold between France and Spain, liberalism and traditionalism, where the crossing itself is fraught with danger and significance.

Henry IV [Historical]

Biographical: Henry IV of France (1553–1610), first Bourbon king, who ended the French Wars of Religion by converting to Catholicism. Famous for the Edict of Nantes granting religious tolerance and for his desire to see "a chicken in every pot."

Connection: Cited by Dana as the model of good kingship: practical, just, and concerned with the welfare of ordinary subjects. The "First Bourbon" establishes the standard against which later rulers are measured. His reign represents the achievable ideal of the Green Revolution—legitimate authority exercising power for the common good, law that is just, and prosperity within a traditional, hierarchical order.

Heresy

Deviation from orthodox belief, a category treated with characteristic complexity. The series's own theology—with its dualism, its emphasis on hidden knowledge, its syncretic incorporation of folk religion—has heretical overtones. Yet the protagonists are firmly Catholic, and the "Omega" worldview is the ultimate heresy: the denial of God's existence. Heresy in the conventional sense (Protestant, liberal Catholic, etc.) is less concerning than this fundamental apostasy. The series navigates between official orthodoxy and a more expansive, esoteric spirituality, ultimately affirming core Christian truths while incorporating heterodox elements.

Hero

A central role, embodied most fully by Dana Coscuin. The hero is "fated to be a fabulous and many-named" figure, his identity multiple and his deeds legendary. Heroism in the series is not simple or idealized; the hero is a "double person," capable of cowardice and folly as well as courage and wisdom. The hero's journey involves falls and restorations, moral failures and redemptions. True heroism requires alignment with the "Green" principle, commitment to the creative order despite personal cost. The hero is an agent of providence, a "deputy elected by the Lord" to act in a world of radical uncertainty.

Hidden Hand

The Devil's agency in the world, operating through human instruments and institutions. The "Hidden Hand" manipulates governments, revolutionary movements, and individuals, advancing the "Red" revolution while remaining obscure. It is the organizing intelligence behind the seemingly disparate forces of destruction, coordinating attacks on the traditional order from multiple directions. The protagonists must contend not just with visible enemies but with this occult power directing events from the shadows. Discerning and opposing the Hidden Hand is a key task of the Green Revolution.

Hierarchy

The principle of ordered ranks, both celestial and terrestrial. The "anierocracy" of Hell parodies the divine hierarchy. The Church has its hierarchy of pope, bishops, priests. The Green Revolution is organized hierarchically under Count Cyril. Political legitimacy derives from hierarchical succession (kings, emperors). The series strongly affirms hierarchy against egalitarian and democratic challenges, seeing proper order as the reflection of divine design. Disruption of hierarchy—whether through revolution, usurpation, or moral fall—is a primary source of disorder and evil.

High Hilarity

The ecstatic, often violent joy associated with battle, adventure, and the embrace of danger. Elena Prado embodies the "High Hilarity of Blood and Death," an intoxicating but ultimately destructive state. Dana also experiences high hilarity in combat, a "battle happiness" that must be distinguished from mere bloodlust. The phrase captures the paradoxical pleasure of risking everything, the rush of engagement with life's most extreme stakes. It is not inherently good or evil but depends on its orientation—whether serving the creative or destructive principle.

Highlands

Mountain regions associated with traditional, conservative values. The Carlist "high hills" of Navarre are the heartland of Spanish traditionalism. The highlands of Ecuador (Quito) oppose the liberal coast (Guayaquil). Highland peoples are depicted as closer to the earth, more rooted, preserving ancient ways against the cosmopolitan innovations of the lowlands and coasts. The highlands are a bastion of the "Green" principle, a geographical and cultural refuge for traditional order against the encroaching "Red" revolution.

Hiodra (Sea Monster)

An ocean monster who lives on the dark and rocky shore three hundred feet beneath Dana's Seven Heads Castle. His name derives from the Hydra of classical languages. Hiodra speaks in a series of whistles and grunts that only Barbary Joe understands completely; Joe translates his sea-lore for human listeners. The monster's ethics are simple—"If it's on the shore, eat it"—which startles Barbary Joe, who teaches him land-ethics in exchange for under-sea-lore. When informers are caught, Hiodra is called upon to dispose of them; bones that pass through his maw become "instant ancient bones." In his longer days he sleeps away much of his time. He tells creature-tales to Barbary Joe and small groups of humans gathered on the shore.

History

Not a fixed sequence of events but a contested, malleable narrative. The protagonists and their enemies work to "alter history before it happens, while it is happening, and even after it has happened." History is the product of the struggle between Green and Red revolutions, its meaning determined by which side prevails. The "Lord of History" is a title contested between God and the Devil. Arpad Arutinov's Back Door of History provides theoretical framework for this view. History is essentially spiritual warfare, its apparent contingency the surface of deeper metaphysical conflict.

Holland

The Netherlands, a maritime and commercial power with a complex religious history. Amsterdam is a key setting, particularly Scheherazade Jokkebrok's establishment on Coffin Street. The "secret church" Dood-Christus-Kerk represents the persistence of Catholicism in a Protestant nation. Holland gin fuels the "crooked revel" at Ifreann's party. The Dutch are part of the cosmopolitan, Protestant-commercial order that contrasts with the Catholic, traditional world the Green Revolution defends, yet individual Dutch locations can serve as nodes in the sacred geography.

Holy Ghost

The third person of the Trinity, invoked in prayers and as the source of spiritual gifts. The Holy Ghost enables the miraculous and guides the faithful, working through and beyond human understanding. References to the Holy Ghost ground the series's supernatural events in Christian theology, distinguishing divine action from demonic or merely magical phenomena. The Holy Ghost is the indwelling presence of God, the source of the grace that enables protagonists to resist evil and fulfill their vocations.

Holy Lions

The Brotherhood of the Holy Lions, a mystical order charged with guarding Gate Castle and maintaining the sacred order on Basse-Terre. Dana becomes a Holy Lion, achieving an "eminence" from which he falls through seduction by the "Green Thorn." The Lions are a chivalric order, combining martial prowess with spiritual discipline. Their role as guardians places them at the front line of the conflict between Green and Red principles. The fall of a Holy Lion is a cosmic event, announced by the booming of castle bells and resulting in spiritual "death" in role.

Holy Roman Empire [Historical]

The medieval political entity, dissolved in 1806, that claimed succession to the Roman Empire under Christian auspices. Invoked as an ideal of unified Christendom under a sacred emperor. The "Black Pope" teaches that the "Empire is in abeyance," the world living in a kind of interregnum awaiting its restoration. The Green Revolution implicitly seeks this restoration—not necessarily the literal Holy Roman Empire but the principle of sacred, hierarchical, unified Christian order that it represented. Its absence defines the modern condition of exile and fragmentation.

Holy Week

The week preceding Easter, commemorating Christ's passion, death, and resurrection. Key events in the narrative are timed to the liturgical calendar, including Holy Week. The sacred time of Holy Week intensifies the spiritual stakes of actions taken within it, linking the protagonists' struggles to the foundational drama of Christian salvation. The pattern of death and resurrection that Holy Week commemorates is the template for the protagonists' own experiences of fall and restoration.

Home

A deeply desired but often elusive state. Dana's home is Bantry Bay, but he is driven into exile and wanders the world. Basse-Terre becomes a new home, but it too is threatened. The "Black Pope" teaches that true home is the "Kingdom" now in abeyance, making earthly homes provisional and impermanent. Home represents belonging, rootedness, and the security that the "Green" principle seeks to restore. The loss of home—through famine, exile, or destruction—is a primary wound suffered by the protagonists, a condition they work to remedy for themselves and the world.

Honor

A code of conduct demanding integrity, courage, and fidelity to one's word and station. Dana is concerned with dying "honorably" versus "dishonorably." The Holy Lions operate by a code of honor that their fall violates. Honor distinguishes the protagonists from their enemies, who (like Ifreann) reject "fair play" and any constraint on their actions. Yet honor is not absolute; it must be balanced against other demands, and rigid adherence to honor codes can sometimes hinder necessary action. True honor aligns with the divine order, not merely with social convention.

Hope

One of the theological virtues, the expectation of divine fulfillment. Hope sustains the protagonists through their trials, enabling action despite uncertainty and suffering. Distinguished from mere optimism, hope is grounded in faith—trust that the "Alpha" principle will ultimately prevail, that God's purposes will be accomplished. The "Green" revolution is a movement of hope, working toward restoration without guarantee of success in historical time. Hope is what allows the Company to continue after defeats and losses, to "fail well enough" and persist.

Horn, Cape

The southernmost point of South America, legendary for its dangerous waters. Rounding the Horn is presented as a trial comparable to the "death adventure," with only two outcomes: "salvation for the salty elect, or destruction forever." A rite of passage, the ultimate test of seamanship and courage. Dana and his companions round the Horn, demonstrating their worthiness for the challenges ahead. The Cape is a geographical and symbolic threshold, a point where ordinary voyaging becomes mythic quest.

Horror

Not merely fear but the encounter with radical evil and the violation of natural and spiritual order. The "Damnation/Horror" experienced in Ifreann's realm is visceral and metaphysical, an assault on the soul as much as the senses. Horror arises from witnessing or participating in acts that transgress fundamental boundaries: necrophilia, child murder, the corruption of sacred things. The series does not flinch from depicting such horror, presenting it as the true face of the "Omega" principle. Confronting horror is necessary for the protagonists; surviving it with their integrity intact is a measure of their spiritual strength.

Horse

A creature of power, nobility, and mythic significance. The "Enchanted Horses" of Ireland are invoked alongside the Puca. Irish horsebreeding is legendarily traced to Alaric's stolen Roman stallions. Dana battles a "devil-beast" stallion on Basse-Terre. The Gauchos of Argentina are defined by their horsemanship. Horses are symbols of martial prowess, aristocratic status, and natural nobility—aligned with the "Green" principle of organic, rooted power. The horse is companion and instrument for the hero, its strength and spirit extensions of his own.

Horseman

A figure of power and mobility, embodied by the Gauchos, cavalry officers, and Dana himself. The horseman commands an animal partner, forming a centaur-like unity of human will and equine strength. Cavalry charges (like the Light Brigade at Balaclava) represent the heroic ideal of direct, personal combat increasingly threatened by modern, mechanized warfare. The horseman is a figure from an older, more heroic age, his skills and ethos challenged by the "dehumanization of war" but still valued in the Green Revolution's vision of restored order.

Host

The consecrated bread of the Eucharist, the Body of Christ in Catholic teaching. The miracle at the Dood-Christus-Kerk involves the Word made Flesh, invoking the Eucharistic mystery. The Host is the central sacrament, the point where divine and human meet in material form. Its profanation would be the ultimate sacrilege; its proper reception is a source of grace. The Host embodies the "Alpha" principle—God present in Creation, the divine order incarnate in ordinary matter, the foundation of the sacramental worldview that the Green Revolution defends.

Hostility

The default state of relations in a world at war. Hostility exists between individuals (Dana and Ifreann), nations (England and Ireland), factions (Federalists and Unitarians), and cosmic principles (Alpha and Omega). The protagonists navigate a world where enemies are everywhere, alliances are fragile, and trust is hard-won. Hostility is not always evil—righteous opposition to wickedness is necessary—but it must be carefully directed. Indiscriminate hostility, like Ifreann's, becomes mere destructiveness; targeted hostility against genuine enemies is a duty.

House

A physical dwelling and a lineage. The House of Cosquin is Dana's line and dwelling on Basse-Terre—the name borne first by a flat rock ("the House of Dana Cosquin"), then by the built house. The "Domdaniel" is the "House of Daniel," Angelene's home and its mystical associations. Houses are destroyed (O'Boyle's inn) and built (Dana's establishment on Basse-Terre), reflecting the larger patterns of destruction and creation in the cosmic struggle. The house is a microcosm of the order the Green Revolution defends—a rooted, stable structure providing shelter and continuity, threatened by the chaos of the Red revolution.

Human

The condition of the protagonists, distinct from angelic, demonic, or other supernatural states. Humans are "mottled between God and the Devil," capable of both good and evil, the primary battleground of the cosmic conflict. Human limitations—mortality, ignorance, moral weakness—are constant challenges, but also the context for heroism and grace. The series affirms human dignity and agency against both the demonic forces that would corrupt and the mechanistic ideologies that would reduce humans to matter. To be human is to be called to participate in the divine order, however imperfectly.

Humboldt Current

The cold ocean current flowing northward along the west coast of South America. One of the great elemental forces shaping climate and ecology, it originates (in Dana's imagination) in a "shop under Antarctica itself." A natural power of immense scale, indifferent to human concerns yet intimately affecting them. The Humboldt Current represents the primal, planetary forces that form the backdrop of human action, the deep structure of the natural world within which the cosmic conflict unfolds.

Humor

A pervasive element, from the "merry and clownish" aspect of divine action to the dark comedy of demonic parody. The series's humor is often ironic, juxtaposing sacred and profane, heroic and absurd. Dana himself is both hero and "craven clown." The Cardinal-Archbishop jokes about cigars in Heaven. Even the grimmest scenes contain moments of dark humor. Humor serves multiple functions: relieving tension, revealing truth through incongruity, and expressing the paradoxical nature of a world where the cosmic and the comic are intertwined.

Hunter, Crimsi

Counter-historical figure who becomes President of the United States through the machinations of the revolutionary conspirators. His name combines "crimson" (red) with "hunter," suggesting both his alignment with the Red Revolution and his predatory nature. His ascent to power is engineered by the same forces promoting the "Red Napoleon" Charles Cosmo and represents the extension of the demonic conspiracy into American politics. A puppet president, his rule advances the mechanistic, atheistic agenda threatening the world order.

Husband

A role Dana assumes multiple times: with Catherine Dembinska (a complex, unconsummated union), with Angelene Domdaniel (his "Bride" on Basse-Terre), and potentially with others. The husband's role involves protection, provision, and partnership, but also vulnerability and entanglement. Dana's marriages are complicated by his wandering nature, his metaphysical commitments, and the dangers surrounding him. The husband-wife bond is a microcosm of larger relationships: between the soul and God, the king and his kingdom, the guardian and what he guards.

Hymn

Sacred song, a form of prayer and praise. Hymns are sung at key moments: at the Dood-Christus-Kerk, at the minstrelsy on Basse-Terre, in churches and chapels throughout the narrative. The hymn is an auditory expression of the divine order, its music and words aligning the singer with sacred reality. Contrasted with the "dissonant peals" of Gate Castle and the "towering unmelody" of Ifreann's revels, the hymn represents harmony, order, and the human voice raised in service of the Alpha principle.

Hysterical ghost

One of the degraded states Malandrino Brume speculates modern man might become: "a stolid animal, or . . . an hysterical ghost, or . . . an effete avatar." The hysterical ghost suggests a being of intense but unfocused emotion, disconnected from material reality and coherent purpose. A state of spiritual dissolution, retaining the passions but losing the substance of personhood. One of the alternatives to authentic human existence that the modern, mechanistic world threatens to impose, a ghostly half-life replacing the robust, incarnate humanity the Green Revolution seeks to preserve.

I

Identity

A fluid, contested, and often multiple condition. Dana is a "many-named hero" with numerous aliases and identities. Characters assume false identities, are mistaken for others, or possess paradoxical dual natures. The "False Dana" (O'Boyle) and the "True Dana" exist simultaneously. Identity is not fixed but performed, constructed, and subject to manipulation by powerful forces. The struggle to maintain authentic identity against demonic illusion and worldly pressure is a central challenge. True identity is ultimately spiritual—alignment with the Alpha or Omega principle—rather than a matter of name or social role.

Ignorance

A pervasive condition of the protagonists, who must act without full knowledge of the forces they confront or the consequences of their actions. Dana often does not understand his orders or the larger purposes they serve. The "elect" are those who can perceive beyond the "deceitful urbanity" of the Devil's agents, but even they see only partially. Ignorance is not merely intellectual limitation but a spiritual condition—the fallen state that obscures divine truth. Overcoming ignorance requires faith, intuition, and the guidance of wiser figures, not merely the accumulation of information.

Illuminated mind

The prelapsarian state of intellectual and spiritual clarity, such as "Adam had before the Fall." Lord Joseph Peter Barbary is said to possess this quality—direct, unmediated understanding of truth. The illuminated mind is a rare gift in the fallen world, a remnant or restoration of original human capacity. It enables perception of realities hidden to ordinary consciousness and right judgment in complex situations. The Green Revolution's leaders possess varying degrees of illumination; its goal is partly the restoration of this capacity to humanity at large.

Illusion

A primary weapon of the demonic forces. Ifreann is a master of illusion, capable of creating entire false realities that trap and deceive his enemies. The Untimely Encounter is an illusory ship; the sea battle off St. Malo may be largely illusion. Elena Prado's seductions involve illusion. The world itself, under the influence of the "Omega" principle, tends toward illusion—false appearances that mask spiritual truth. Discerning illusion from reality is a constant challenge for the protagonists, requiring spiritual perception and often external assistance. The struggle against illusion is a struggle for truth itself.

Image of God

The theological concept that humanity is created in God's likeness (imago Dei). The "Black Pope" teaches that man, "made in the Image," is "given Dominion." This image is the basis of human dignity, authority, and responsibility. It is also what the demonic forces seek to corrupt or destroy—to deface the image of God in humanity is to strike at God Himself. The protagonists, despite their flaws, bear this image and are called to realize it more fully. The struggle is ultimately over whether humanity will fulfill or betray its nature as image-bearers of the divine.

Immaculate Conception

Catholic dogma that the Virgin Mary was conceived without original sin, defined by Pope Pius IX in 1854. Referenced in connection with Pius IX's pontificate and the mid-19th-century Church. The definition of this dogma is part of the larger context of Catholic restoration and assertion against liberal, secular forces. It represents the Church's claim to define truth authoritatively, a claim contested by the forces of the Red Revolution. The Immaculate Conception is a theological affirmation of purity and grace, the reversal of the Fall's corruption in at least one human person.

Immortality

A quality possessed by certain supernatural beings and, in a different sense, by the human soul. Ifreann, though repeatedly killed, persists in some form. The dead continue to act and communicate. The soul's immortality means that the stakes of the conflict extend beyond physical death to eternal destiny. The series affirms traditional Christian teaching on the soul's survival, grounding its drama in consequences that transcend mortal life. Immortality is not mere endless existence but a quality of being that participates in or opposes the eternal divine order.

Incarnation

The central Christian mystery: God becoming human in Christ. The miracle at the Dood-Christus-Kerk, where "the Word was made Flesh," is an experience of this mystery. Ifreann claims to be "the other Incarnation," a demonic parody possessing "two natures together" as Christ does. The Incarnation affirms the goodness of material creation and the possibility of divine presence within it. It is the foundation of the sacramental worldview, the "Alpha" principle made flesh. The Green Revolution defends an incarnational understanding of reality against the disembodied, mechanistic worldview of the "Omega" forces.

India

British colonial possession, source of exotic goods, animals, and cultural references. Aunt Hattie, the elephant on Basse-Terre, is an "Indian Elephant." The mongoose (linked to Prince Dorn) originates from India. The Sepoy Mutiny of 1857 is referenced as a major event of the era. India represents the vastness of the British Empire and the diversity of the world the protagonists traverse. Its religions, creatures, and cultures are part of the syncretic mix that characterizes the series's global scope.

Indian

Indigenous peoples of the Americas, appearing in both South American and (implicitly) North American contexts. An Indian woman in Bolivia identifies Dana and his companions as "angels" sent to "alter history." Indians possess traditional knowledge and spiritual perception often superior to that of Europeans. They represent the indigenous, pre-colonial dimension of American reality, a source of alternative wisdom and power. The series treats them with respect, incorporating their perspectives into its syncretic spiritual vision.

Infallibility

The doctrine of papal infallibility, defined at the First Vatican Council in 1870. Lord Acton, mentioned in the series, was a prominent opponent of this definition. The doctrine asserts that the Pope, under specific conditions, cannot err in defining matters of faith and morals. It represents the Church's claim to authoritative teaching, contested by liberals within and without the Church. The series's portrayal of Pius IX includes his difficulties in "receiving communication from Christ," suggesting the human limitations even of an infallible office.

Infernal

Pertaining to Hell and its inhabitants. Ifreann's forces are infernal; his ship burns with infernal fire; his parties are sites of infernal degradation. The infernal is the demonic made manifest, the direct intrusion of Hell into the earthly realm. Infernal powers are not merely evil but organized, intelligent, and purposeful, working toward the destruction of the divine order. Confronting the infernal requires spiritual as well as physical courage, for it threatens the soul as much as the body.

Inheritance

The transmission of property, title, and spiritual legacy from one generation to the next. Dana bears the Coscuin name and eventually the style of Count Dana—the deed to Seven Heads Castle stands made out to "Count Dana Patrick Cyril Coscuin." The Carlist cause is a dispute over royal inheritance. Inheritance establishes continuity, linking past, present, and future in chains of obligation and identity. It is a conservative principle, valuing what is received over what is invented, and forms part of the traditional order the Green Revolution defends.

Inkerman

Battle of Inkerman (November 5, 1854), a major engagement of the Crimean War. British and French forces repelled a Russian attack in confused, brutal fighting characterized by fog and close combat. Described as more savage and elemental than the earlier Battle of the Alma, a "soldiers' battle" where command broke down and individual courage determined the outcome. A key event in the Crimean theater, its chaos and horror exemplify the changing nature of warfare that the series analyzes.

Inn

A place of hospitality, refuge, and danger. Joseph O'Boyle's inn in Skibbereen is the central Irish setting—a sanctuary for the "Apostles," a site of community and resistance, eventually destroyed by hostile forces. Inns are points of convergence where travelers meet, information is exchanged, and plots are hatched. They are also vulnerable, subject to attack by enemies who recognize their importance. The inn represents the fragile, local institutions that the Green Revolution seeks to protect against the destructive forces of the Red.

Innocence

A state of prelapsarian purity, associated with Adam before the Fall and with certain exceptional individuals like Lord Joseph Peter Barbary. Innocence enables direct perception of truth and moral clarity. Most characters have lost innocence through experience, sin, or the mere condition of living in a fallen world. The recovery of innocence—not naïveté but restored purity—is part of the Green Revolution's restorative vision. Innocence is also vulnerability; the innocent are targets for the corrupting forces of evil, who seek to destroy what they cannot possess.

Inquisition

The ecclesiastical tribunal for identifying and punishing heresy. The series notes that the 19th-century Spanish regimes of Espartero and Narváez killed "twelve times as many persons as the Inquisition," defending the Church's historical institution against its liberal critics. The Inquisition represents the Church's claim to define and enforce orthodoxy, a claim the secular, liberal order rejects. The comparison challenges the conventional narrative of religious intolerance versus secular enlightenment, suggesting the modern state can be far more violent than the institutions it replaced.

Intelligence

Both the faculty of understanding and the organized gathering of information. Characters like Ifreann and Charles Cosmo possess formidable intelligence, directed toward evil ends. The various powers maintain intelligence networks, with places like Hendaye serving as "nests of spies." Intelligence in the sense of information is crucial for navigating the complex political landscape. Intelligence as faculty is valued but not sufficient; without wisdom and moral orientation, it becomes a tool of destruction. The "Devil's Disciples" are intellectually brilliant but spiritually blind.

Intervention

Action by external forces—divine, human, or demonic—that alters the course of events. Divine intervention is credited with Dana's "victory" over Elena Prado. Human intervention by the Company shapes political outcomes across continents. Demonic intervention by Ifreann and his forces disrupts the natural and moral order. The series presents a world of constant intervention, where no event is purely the result of natural or human causes alone. History is the product of competing interventions, a battlefield where supernatural powers actively engage.

Intrigue

The complex web of secret plots, alliances, and deceptions that drives political action. Hendaye is a "nests within nests" of intrigue. The courts of Europe, the revolutionary movements, the Church itself—all are sites of intrigue. The protagonists must navigate this world, employing intrigue while trying not to be consumed by it. Intrigue is the mode of politics in a world where overt power is often less important than hidden manipulation. Skill in intrigue is necessary for survival, but it poses constant moral dangers.

Intuition

Direct, non-rational knowledge, a faculty highly valued in the series. Catherine Dembinska operates largely through intuition, her spiritual perceptions surpassing logical analysis. Damisa the Leopard possesses intuitive understanding of supernatural phenomena. Intuition is distinguished from the merely rational intelligence that characterizes the "mechanistic" worldview of the Red Revolution. True knowledge requires both faculties, but intuition provides access to realities that reason alone cannot grasp. The protagonists rely on intuition in moments of crisis, trusting perceptions that defy rational explanation.

Ireland

Dana's homeland, a nation under English colonial rule, devastated by the Great Famine, yet possessing deep spiritual and cultural resources. The "Back Doors of the World" (Bantry, Kenmare, and Dingle Bays) are its mythic geography. Ireland is characterized by its "crooked tongues" (capacity for both lying and legend-making), its Catholic faith persisting despite persecution, its connection to continental Europe bypassing England, and its ancient, half-hidden traditions of magic and prophecy. Ireland is both a real place of suffering and oppression and a symbol of the resilient, creative spirit that the Green Revolution embodies.

Irish

The people, language, and culture of Ireland. The Irish are depicted as survivors, resisters, and myth-makers, their character forged by centuries of oppression. Irish Gaelic is one of the ancient languages of wisdom; "Dooms-Day Irish" is the terrifying tongue in which the bells of Thorn Castle announce a Lion's fall. The Irish diaspora spreads across the world, carrying their distinctive qualities with them. To be Irish in this series is to inherit a particular relationship to suffering, faith, and the hidden currents of history—a preparation for the cosmic struggles the protagonists face.

Irish Giant

One of the Irish "indecencies"—peculiarities found in Irish families that explain their modesty and the distance between cottages. In the Connemara district of Galway County, one family in every three has an Irish Giant. These giants are usually more than seven feet tall, described as oafish, stupid, and awkward. John Andrew MacCobble (False Jack Galopade) organizes the "All-Ireland Irish Giant Wrestle and Rough-House Contest" at Lynchy Farm, building a thirty-six foot square ring because "Irish Giants are cramped in the smaller eighteen foot and twenty-four foot rings." The contest draws twenty-four entries: sixteen genuine Irish Giants (who must meet certain size requirements) and eight "Giants-at-large" who, in an Irish bull, "did not have to be as large as the genuine Irish Giants."

Isabella II [Historical]

Biographical: Isabella II (1830–1904), Queen of Spain from 1833 to 1868. Her disputed succession (against her uncle Don Carlos) triggered the Carlist Wars. Her reign was marked by political instability, military coups, and eventual exile after the Revolution of 1868.

Connection: The "new Queen" whose government the series criticizes, whose "Abbess" (Abadesa) is a worldly political appointee, and whose regime represents the triumph of liberalism over Carlist traditionalism. Her rule, dependent on strongmen like Espartero and Narváez, is portrayed as illegitimate and corrupt, a usurpation of the rightful Carlist succession and a betrayal of traditional Spanish values.

Island

A recurrent setting and symbol. Basse-Terre (St. Peter's Island) is the central island, an "Earthly Paradise" and site of intense spiritual conflict. Ireland itself is an island, its maritime nature shaping its character. Islands are places of isolation, concentration, and intensity—microcosms where larger forces clash in contained space. The island is also a refuge, a "bottle in the sea" carrying its message through time, a place where alternative realities can flourish beyond the reach of dominant powers.

Italian

Pertaining to Italy and its people, language, and culture. Italy in the mid-19th century is a peninsula of fragmented states undergoing the violent process of unification (Risorgimento). "Salt-water Italian" is the language of Purgatory. Italian revolutionaries (Garibaldi, Mazzini), statesmen (Cavour), and churchmen (Pius IX, Leo XIII) populate the narrative. Italy is a battleground between the Green and Red revolutions, its unification a contested process with profound implications for the Church, the traditional order, and the balance of European power.

Italy

A geographical expression (in Metternich's phrase) becoming a nation. The series covers key events of Italian unification: the revolutions of 1848, the wars of 1859–1860, Garibaldi's expedition, the annexation of the Papal States. Italy is the "Eden" of Pope Pius IX, whose temporal domain is threatened by nationalist forces. Naples, Rome, Piedmont, and other Italian locations are key settings. The unification of Italy is not a simple triumph of nationalism but a complex, morally ambiguous process, manipulated by hidden forces and producing a state far from the traditional order the Green Revolution values.

Iveragh Mountains

Mountain range on the Iveragh Peninsula in County Kerry, Ireland, including Carrauntoohil, Ireland's highest peak. The mountains form the backdrop to Kenmare Bay, one of the "Back Doors of the World." A landscape of rugged beauty and ancient associations, the Iveragh Mountains represent the wild, untamed dimension of Ireland, the physical terrain that shapes the character of its people and provides refuge from external domination.

J

Jack

A common name appearing in multiple characters: "True Jack" Galopade (the original), "False Jack" Galopade (John Andrew MacCobble, who assumed the name), Jack O'Donoghue (figure of Irish legend). The multiplicity of Jacks reflects the series's interest in doubling, repetition, and the persistence of types across individuals. "Jack" is an everyman name, grounding legendary and heroic figures in ordinary Irish identity while allowing for multiple incarnations of similar qualities.

Jackal

An animal metaphor applied to Ercule Tirana, who is "jackal" to his wife Lady Valiente's "lion." The jackal suggests cunning, scavenging, and a secondary role—important support functions rather than direct, heroic confrontation. Ercule's jackal nature makes him invaluable for logistics, intelligence, and behind-the-scenes maneuvering, complementing the more direct martial qualities of the lions. Not a pejorative but a recognition of different gifts within the Company.

Jesuit

Members of the Society of Jesus, the Catholic religious order founded by St. Ignatius Loyola. Jesuits are among the clergy specifically targeted by the Irish Penal Laws. The order is associated with education, missionary work, and sophisticated intellectual engagement with the modern world. Within the series's ecclesiastical landscape, Jesuits represent a particular form of Catholic presence—learned, disciplined, sometimes controversial—operating at the intersection of Church and world.

Jesus Christ

The central figure of Christianity, whose Incarnation is the model for the "Alpha" principle and whose Passion is parodied by Ifreann's "devil wounds." Pope Pius IX laments his difficulty in "receiving communication from Christ," highlighting the challenges of divine-human relationship even for the Vicar of Christ. The series's Christology is orthodox: Christ is true God and true man, the Word made Flesh, the source of salvation. All the cosmic struggle ultimately relates to Him—the Green Revolution serves His kingdom; the Red seeks to overthrow it.

Jew

The Jewish people appear in various contexts: as part of the multi-ethnic population of Galicia and other Central European regions, as a component of the commercial and intellectual life of major cities, and in theological references to the Hebrew scriptures. The series does not focus extensively on Jewish characters but includes them as part of the diverse human landscape. Disraeli's Jewish heritage is noted. The series's treatment is matter-of-fact, neither philosemitic nor antisemitic, simply acknowledging Jewish presence in 19th-century society.

Jokkebrok, Scheherazade

The mysterious proprietress of the Floating Mortuary on Coffin Street in Amsterdam. Dana's "favorite seeress." Her name combines the legendary storyteller of the Arabian Nights with a Dutch surname. She "makes up" fates and histories, spinning alternative realities into existence. Her establishment is a threshold between ordinary reality and stranger dimensions; from it, Dana and Jack depart into the "dislocated ocean." A weaver of destinies aligned with the Green Revolution, she embodies feminine, non-rational wisdom crucial to the Company's operations, possessing methods and knowledge beyond ordinary human capacity.

Jolif, Joseph

A figure whose dead body (or wax effigy) is discovered at Greenfields, prompting questions about the nature of time and reality. The circumstances of his death are mysterious, with different accounts offering conflicting timelines. His case exemplifies the series's treatment of death as a fluid, ambiguous state and time as malleable rather than fixed. Joseph Jolif's fate is bound up with the larger metaphysical questions the narrative explores.

Journey

The protagonists are perpetual travelers, their lives defined by movement across geographical and spiritual landscapes. Dana's journeys take him from Ireland to Spain, France, the Netherlands, the Caribbean, South America, the Crimea, and beyond. Each journey is both physical displacement and spiritual odyssey, with the challenges of travel serving as trials that test and transform the traveler. The journey is the hero's mode of existence, a perpetual pilgrimage toward destinations that are as much spiritual as geographical.

Joy

An experience associated with the "High Hilarity" of adventure and the deeper satisfaction of alignment with divine purpose. Dana experiences joy in battle, in love, in the company of friends. Joy is distinguished from mere pleasure; it has a spiritual dimension, a participation in the creative energy of the Alpha principle. The "blue bird" in Carolina's head (before the evil mind worm replaces it) suggests the presence of joy. True joy is a sign of right relationship with God and creation, a fruit of the Green Revolution's restorative work.

Juarez, Benito [Historical]

Biographical: Benito Pablo Juárez García (1806–1872), Mexican lawyer and statesman of Zapotec origin. President of Mexico during turbulent periods including the Reform War, the French Intervention, and the Second Mexican Empire. A liberal reformer who implemented laws separating church and state.

Connection: In the series's counter-history, Juarez is defeated by Porfirio Diaz in a "special election" of only seven voters and subsequently dies in a "shooting accident." This grim revision places Diaz's rise within the pattern of conspiratorial manipulation by the Red Revolution's agents, turning a liberal hero into a victim of the same forces he might otherwise have opposed.

Judgment

Both divine evaluation and human discernment. "Daniel come to judgement" is the title of the minstrelsy epic, framing Dana as one whose dual nature must be weighed. Divine judgment is ongoing, not merely eschatological; characters face judgment in their choices and their fates. The "Dooms-Day" language of the Thorn Castle bells suggests immediate, personal judgment at moments of moral fall. Human judgment is also required: the protagonists must constantly evaluate situations, characters, and courses of action in conditions of uncertainty. Right judgment requires wisdom, faith, and often supernatural assistance.

Justice

A central value of the Green Revolution, distinguished from mere legality or power. The "Black Pope" teaches that man must "rule over himself in justice, and... rule over the world in justice." True kingship provides "a just law" (Henry IV's standard). Justice is the proper ordering of relationships—between persons, between rulers and ruled, between humanity and God. The Red Revolution produces injustice: oppression, exploitation, the corruption of law into an instrument of power. The restoration of justice is a primary goal of the Green cause.

K

Kenmare Bay

Large bay in County Kerry, southwestern Ireland, between the Iveragh and Beara Peninsulas. One of the three "Back Doors of the World," extending from Dursey Head on the south to Lamb's Head on the north. Fed by the Kenmare River, surrounded by the Iveragh Mountains and Caha Mountains, its waters "green and gold." Towns along its shores include Kenmare, Sneem, and Waterville. A landscape of mythic associations and practical importance, a gateway for the "illicit commerce" that connected Ireland to the wider world, bypassing English control.

Kerry

County in southwestern Ireland, encompassing Dingle Bay and Kenmare Bay—two of the three "Back Doors of the World." A region of dramatic mountain and coastal scenery, Gaelic-speaking communities, and historical independence from English control. Kerry represents the deep, traditional Ireland, a landscape of ancient associations and resilient culture. Dana's origins in this region shape his character and provide the geographical and spiritual foundation for his adventures.

Kill

An action the protagonists must undertake, though not without moral weight. Dana kills repeatedly—in battle, in self-defense, in service of the Green Revolution. The Brumes acknowledge that they must sometimes "kill" in their work. Killing is distinguished from murder; it can be justified but is never casual. Dana's inability to feel remorse for killing a man prevents his receiving absolution, highlighting the spiritual stakes. The series does not glamorize killing but presents it as a sometimes necessary, always consequential, element of the cosmic struggle.

King

The legitimate head of state, whose authority derives from divine sanction and traditional succession. The Green Revolution is monarchist, seeking to restore true kings against usurpers and republican revolutionaries. Henry IV of France is the model of good kingship. The Carlist cause supports the legitimate claimant against Isabella II. Kings are "deputies elected by the Lord," their authority delegated from above. The absence of true kingship—"the Empire is in abeyance"—defines the modern condition of exile and disorder. The restoration of legitimate monarchy is a key goal of the Green Revolution.

King of Darkness Mongoose

The demonic familiar of Prince Dorn, a mongoose with supernatural qualities. The mongoose is traditionally an enemy of snakes, but this one serves the forces aligned with the serpent (the Devil). The "King of Darkness" title marks its infernal nature and high rank among demonic animals. It participates in the conflicts of the "Congress of the Animals" and serves as Prince Dorn's companion and instrument. A creature of intelligence and malice, embodying the demonic penetration of the natural world.

Kingdom

Both earthly political realm and heavenly reality. The "Kingdom of Heaven" is the model for earthly kingdoms, making democracy absurd ("Who ever heard of the Democracy of Heaven?"). The "Kingdom" is currently "in abeyance," the world living in exile from its true political and spiritual order. Earthly kingdoms (Spain, France, the Two Sicilies) are contested spaces, their legitimacy depending on alignment with divine order. The restoration of the Kingdom—on earth as it is in heaven—is the ultimate goal of the Green Revolution.

Kingsberry, Elaine

Daughter of Lord Kingsberry ("possibly the finest mind in England"), wife of Lord Jack Galopade, and member of the Company. Attacked by a "diabolical assassin" with an axe, she survives as a testament to the dangers faced by Green agents even in England. The best friend of Dana's dead wife, Catherine Dembinska, she holds her own place in the Company; her Kingsberry birth places her within the English aristocracy. She embodies the international, cross-class nature of the Green Revolution, a bridge between the Company's continental adventures and English influence.

Kingsberry, Lord

English nobleman, suggested as a potential replacement for the Earl of Aberdeen as Prime Minister. A man of "superior intellect" aligned with the Green cause. His family, including his daughter Elaine Kingsberry (later Lady Elaine Galopade), are members of the Company. The Kingsberrys represent the English aristocratic element within the Green Revolution, demonstrating that the cause transcends national boundaries and includes sympathizers within the British establishment itself.

Knife

A weapon and tool appearing frequently in close combat and threatening situations. Mariella Cima holds a "relentless knife" to Dana's throat, forcing him to choose his words carefully. The knife represents immediate, personal danger, the intimacy of violence that characterizes much of the series's conflict. Unlike the impersonal destruction of artillery, the knife requires proximity and precision. It is the weapon of assassins, of desperate moments, of the visceral reality underlying the larger strategic struggles.

Knowledge

A contested and complex category. The "Devil's know" is vast but fundamentally flawed, lacking true wisdom. The illuminated mind possesses direct knowledge of truth. Hidden knowledge—esoteric, mystical, traditional—is valued over the surface information available to ordinary consciousness. Knowledge is power, but its nature determines whether that power serves good or evil. The mechanistic, materialist knowledge promoted by the "Devil's Disciples" is a counterfeit, obscuring deeper truths. True knowledge is ultimately spiritual, a participation in divine understanding.

Krakow

Historic city in southern Poland, in the mid-19th century part of Austrian-controlled Galicia. The ancient capital of Poland, a center of Polish culture, learning, and nationalist sentiment. Part of the "Eastern Marches" where Green and Red forces clash. Krakow represents the suppressed Polish nation, its aspirations for independence intertwined with the larger European revolutionary movements. A site of intrigue and conflict, it anchors the Eastern European dimension of the series's geopolitical drama.

L

La Catalina

A three-masted ship with a funnel that captures Dana's attention and desire. Milagroso dismisses her as a "whore," but Dana falls deeply in love with the vessel. Lady Valiente Tirana deposits a significant sum into Dana's account, enabling him to purchase the ship. La Catalina becomes a significant plot element, representing Dana's romantic attachment to the sea and his continued maritime adventures after the loss of the Catherine Dembinska.

La Plata

The Río de la Plata region, encompassing Argentina and Uruguay. In the mid-19th century, a theater of intense political conflict between Federalists and Unitarians, dominated by the figure of Juan Manuel de Rosas. Described as the "loins and the entrails" of South America—a place of raw, visceral power.
