Attachment to patria is a profound human reality, source of both strength and suffering, even for those whose ultimate citizenship is in Heaven.

Patron

A being providing protection, guidance, and support, often in supernatural or quasi-feudal context. Count Cyril Prasimos is the most significant patron—unseen instigator and employer of Green Revolution agents. He is Dana's patron, directing missions and providing resources through mysterious methods. The relationship is not simple employment but a deep, almost fated bond transcending ordinary communication and even death.

Paul VI, Pope [Historical]

Biographical: Giovanni Battista Montini (1897–1978), reigned as Pope Paul VI from 1963 to 1978. Presided over the conclusion of the Second Vatican Council.

Connection: A quote attributed to him ("Let my faith be joyful and give peace and gladness to my unhushed spirit") appears as an epigraph. This anachronistic reference connects the 19th-century spiritual struggles to modern theological concerns, suggesting continuity of faith and doubt across time.

Paulskirche

St. Paul's Church in Frankfurt, seat of the Frankfurt Parliament of 1848–49, the first freely-elected German legislative body. Its attempt to create a unified, liberal Germany ultimately failed. Ashley lists it among the "segmented spiders"—societies, clubs, and generative happenings of the age. This frames it as one of many competing organizational forms (or "webs") attempting to shape Europe's future.

Paysandu

City in western Uruguay on the Uruguay River. In the mid-19th century, an important provincial center and river port. Across the river from the Argentine town of Concepción del Uruguay, where Governor Urquiza masses his forces. Specific geographical positioning crucial to military maneuvers leading to the fall of Rosas, grounding epic events in precise strategic locations.

Peace

Not absence of conflict but a dynamic, hard-won state of being. The narrative contrasts the "serene" peace of pre-revolutionary Chile and the "violent and dynamic peace" of Ecuador under Flores and Rocafuerte with the false peace of stagnation or the chaotic violence of the Red Revolution. True peace is compatible with, even dependent on, struggle and tension.

Penal Laws

The body of legislation in Ireland and Britain imposing disabilities and restrictions on Catholics. These laws forbade Catholics from owning land, holding office, receiving education, practicing their religion, and more. The series provides extensive excerpts from these laws, demonstrating the systematic nature of the persecution. The Penal Laws are the legal framework of the "strangulations" that the Irish endured for centuries, the institutional expression of genocidal intent.

Penance

The sacrament of confession and reconciliation. Dana seeks penance after his falls, recognizing it as necessary for spiritual restoration. Penance requires genuine contrition—Dana cannot receive absolution when he lacks true remorse. The "Strada del Pentimento" (Repentance Road) leads to a Pardoner who facilitates this sacrament. Penance is a difficult, transformative process, not merely a formality but a genuine encounter with one's sins and God's mercy.

People Militant

The Church on Earth (Ecclesia militans), engaged in ongoing spiritual warfare against sin and evil. Catherine Dembinska uses the phrase to describe the followers of the Green Revolution: "We are the People Militant on Earth until we finally join the People Triumphant in the Kingdom." This recasts political and revolutionary struggle in theological terms, defining earthly existence as active, divinely sanctioned combat.

People Triumphant

The Church in Heaven (Ecclesia triumphans), composed of those who have successfully completed their earthly struggle and entered glory. Catherine Dembinska refers to this as the ultimate goal of the People Militant. The eschatological hope animating the characters' often brutal and seemingly hopeless worldly endeavors—the promise of final victory and reunion justifying present sacrifices.

Perception

The limits and possibilities of perception are continually explored. Characters often operate with partial knowledge, relying on intuition, "second sight," or messages from supernatural sources. Reality is multi-layered and often illusory. The Third Man and Count Cyril can only be perceived indirectly. The struggle is often one of perception: seeing through illusions, discerning the true nature of events, recognizing divine or demonic forces at play. Perception is not simple sensory act but crucial moral and spiritual faculty.

Peter, Saint

Simon Peter, one of the Twelve Apostles of Jesus Christ and considered the first Pope by the Roman Catholic Church. Archetype of papal authority and foundation of the Church. Pope Pius IX's declaration "I Am Peter" is a direct claim to this lineage. The narrative invokes Peter's human frailty (the denial of Christ) as a model for the struggles of its own fallible protagonists. When Dana says he will be "no Peter to deny" Count Cyril, he invokes this quintessential act of weakness under pressure.

Peter-in-Chains

Epithet for the Pope, referencing Saint Peter's imprisonment in Acts of the Apostles. Pius IX uses it to describe a necessary aspect of the papal role: "a Pope must expect to have to play the role of Peter-in-Chains as well as the role of Peter-Keeper-Of-The-Keys-Of-The-Kingdom." Signifies the Pope's participation in the suffering and persecution of the Church, a counterpart to his exercise of authority—imprisonment not as political failure but as spiritually significant, archetypal experience.

Piccola Spinta Alla Storia

Piccola Spinta Alla Storia—"A Little Shove for History"—the ship Dana acquires in the Channel Islands, spiritual and physical successor to the Catherine Dembinska. More than a vessel: mobile headquarters, sanctuary, and active agent of the Green Revolution. A place where key encounters happen and crucial decisions are made. Its name encapsulates the Company's mission: to apply small but decisive pressures at critical points to alter history's course.

Piedmont-Sardinia [Historical]

The Kingdom of Sardinia from 1815 to 1861, comprising the island of Sardinia and mainland territories of Piedmont, Savoy, and Liguria. The only independent Italian state to resist Austrian domination, it served as the nucleus for Italian unification. Under Count Cavour and King Victor Emmanuel II, it became the driving force behind the Risorgimento. A major political and military actor in the European conflicts, its role is complex—vehicle for nationalist aspirations and agent of conventional power struggle.

Pilate

Archetype of worldly power faced with a divine dilemma. Magdelena Brume, when her husband expresses skepticism about her premonitory dreams, retorts: "Who are you, Caesar's wife or Pilate's, that you should suffer a dream?" The reference to Pilate's wife frames her visions as divinely-sent warnings that worldly, masculine authority ignores at its peril, aligning her with a tradition of female intuition perceiving spiritual truths opaque to male reason.

Pillars of salt

Allusion to Lot's wife in Genesis 19:26, turned into a pillar of salt for looking back at Sodom's destruction. O'Boyle asks Carolina Tirana if they will suffer this fate for looking back after fleeing Ifreann. Carolina affirms: they will become "pillars of salt, and salt of sulphur, and salt of brimstone," linking biblical punishment to the alchemical and demonic nature of their enemy.

Pius IX, Pope (Pio Nono) [Historical]

Biographical: Giovanni Maria Mastai-Ferretti (1792–1878), reigned 1846–1878, the longest well-documented pontificate in Church history. Began with liberal reputation, became staunchly conservative after 1848 revolutions and loss of Papal States. Convened the First Vatican Council (1869–70), which defined papal infallibility.

Connection: A complex, central figure. One of four key players in the "Great Substitution" plot, a potential target for replacement by the Prisoner of Naples. Both a man of genuine faith and a worldly ruler caught in the Risorgimento's turmoil. Engages directly with Dana, discussing theology, politics, and the nature of his office. In Ashley's spider-room, he is a spider weaving a grand but new web. Treated with reverence for his office and critical eye for his human and political struggles.

Pointe-Noire

Town on the western (leeward) coast of Basse-Terre Island, Guadeloupe. One of the principal settlements, contributing to the human geography of Dana's adopted "Terrestrial Paradise."

Polevoi

In Slavic folklore, a male field spirit. Described as "field deities of uncertain allegiance," sometimes aligned with God, sometimes with the Devil. They appear as "Angels" at the nativity of Ifreann Chortovitch, serving as supernatural witness. Their inclusion blurs lines between Christian and pagan cosmology, suggesting a world where multiple spiritual hierarchies coexist and interact.

Polish Austria [Historical]

Galicia, the part of Poland annexed by the Austrian Empire during 18th-century partitions. A center of Polish nationalist intrigue and brutal suppression, particularly the Austrian-incited peasant massacre of Polish landlords in 1846. Birthplace of both Catherine Dembinska and Ifreann Chortovitch, making it the crucible of the series's central spiritual conflict.

Portobello Estate

Caribbean base of operations for Ifreann Chortovitch and his kinsman Count Vantour. Physical headquarters for the Red Revolution in the Americas and a literal gateway to the demonic realm. A place of palpable evil from which Ifreann's "four sinister flunkies" operate. Exists in direct opposition to the Greenfields estate of Count Cyril, representing the Manichaean conflict made manifest in geography.

Possession

Spiritual or demonic possession is central to the series's understanding of evil. The unborn Ifreann is "possessed by the devil"; Prince Dorn is possessed by the King of Darkness mongoose. Not merely metaphorical but literal indwelling of one entity by another. The theme extends to ideological possession, where individuals and groups are controlled by a "Hidden Hand" or malevolent animating principle, losing their will and becoming puppets in the cosmic conflict.

Poverty

Not just economic condition but a spiritual state with distinct character. The narrative contrasts Ireland's "grinding poverty" (result of systematic oppression) with the "happy paupers" of Bolivia (material simplicity with spiritual integrity). The poor often possess deeper wisdom and more profound connection to reality than the wealthy. Spiritual wealth and poverty are the more fundamental categories.

Power

Central thematic concern explored in political, military, psychological, and supernatural dimensions. The narrative contrasts different kinds: overt, coercive power of states and armies; subtle, manipulative power of secret societies and "Hidden Hands"; organic, life-affirming power of the Green Revolution; sterile, destructive power of the Red. True power is rooted not in force but in deep connection to cosmic realities—a power that even the "Devil Unchained" cannot ultimately overcome.

Prado, Conde

Uncle of Elena Prado, a figure of the Spanish nobility in the Carlist Hills. Appears to be a government sympathizer, collaborating with anti-Carlist forces. Attempts to use his authority to force Dana to dine with his niece, setting up a key confrontation. Aware of Elena's multiple identities (shy girl and "she-snake") but believes he can control her. Representative of the compromised, opportunistic Spanish aristocracy caught between old order and new.

Prado, Elena (Muerte de Boscaje)

Central and deeply paradoxical character. A Spanish noblewoman embodying multiple contradictory identities: "shy and sheltered girl," seductive "brown-skin snake," insane Carlist battle-leader "Muerte de Boscaje" (Death of the Grove), and demonic "death-witch." The chaotic, passionate, morally ambiguous soul of Spain. Initially an antagonist and "death-danger" to Dana, she becomes his lover and vessel for a dark, anti-Christian philosophy (the "Omega way"). Their relationship is a fall from grace for him. Even after seemingly killed, her ghost continues to haunt—an indestructible, seductive, ultimately tragic form of evil.

Prague

Capital of Bohemia (now Czech Republic). In 1848, a major city within the Austrian Empire and a center of Czech and Pan-Slavic nationalism. Site of the Prague Slavic Congress in June 1848, ending in unsuccessful insurrection. Catherine Dembinska goes to Prague for "Slavic business" and to carry the "Green Scapular of Saint Cyril" to Bohemia and Moravia—at the heart of the historical nationalist movements, identifying the Green Revolution with the aspirations of Europe's oppressed nations.

Prayer

Addressing God or a saint in worship, supplication, or thanksgiving. A constant activity in the series, a direct line of communication with supernatural forces. Characters pray for guidance, strength, souls of the dead, and success of their cause. Prayer is treated as a potent and practical act, as real and effective as military or political action—a fundamental tool for navigating spiritual warfare.

Premonition

Strong feeling that something is about to happen, especially something unpleasant. A common form of supernatural knowledge. Characters like Magdelena Brume, Tancredi Cima, and Elaine Kingsberry have premonitions that prove accurate. Treated not as psychological quirks but as genuine, if obscure, communications from a deeper level of reality—a vital tool for navigating a world where events are determined by unseen forces.

Pride

Explored in its dual aspect as virtue and vice. There is legitimate pride of characters like Dana and the Irish people in their identity and resilience. There is also the "superbious assumption" and hubris of antagonists like Ifreann. The narrative suggests pride is a necessary component of strong identity, becoming destructive only when not tempered by humility and recognition of a higher order.

Priest

Central and archetypal figure. Priests are mediators of divine grace, possessors of secret knowledge, and active participants in the Green Revolution. Often figures of paradox: the "old bandit or old priest" (the Black Pope), the juggler-priest (John Bosco), the music-master-priest (Monte Albani). They operate where sacramental acts are inseparable from espionage, military counsel, and revolutionary action. The priest is a key agent in the spiritual warfare underpinning all worldly events.

Priest-hider

Someone who gives shelter to a Catholic priest in a time of persecution. In 19th-century Ireland, a serious crime under British law. The accusation is leveled against General Sabatelli by the anti-Catholic mob, serving as pretext for his murder. Signifies a courageous act of defiance against an oppressive regime and solidarity with the persecuted Church.

Prince of the World

Satan or the ruling spirit of the fallen world. The Black Pope uses it to describe the master of a "prototype" of the pornographic preacher, a "small shrill man" who knows what "half of the world is like." Identifies the source of worldly corruption as a specific, intelligent entity commanding a host of human agents.

Principality

In Christian angelology and demonology, a rank of angels or demons. Dana declares, "I fight with principalities," echoing St. Paul (Ephesians 6:12). This identifies his struggle not as mere conflict with human opponents but as direct engagement with supernatural powers and "rulers of the darkness of this world" animating his enemies.

Promise

Sacred and binding declaration with performative power, creating obligations transcending time and death. Dana's promise to Catherine ("You will always be with me"), God's promise to the righteous, and the Devil's promises to his followers are all active forces in the world. Keeping or breaking promises is a key measure of moral integrity.

Prophecy

Foretelling future events, woven throughout the narrative. Not a single, clear revelation but a complex and often contradictory stream from multiple sources—saints, devils, seers, historical cycles, personal premonition. Characters must navigate this "mish-mash" of prophetic data to understand their destiny and make choices. The future is not fixed but in constant state of becoming, influenced by both divine will and human action.

Prophetess

Angelene Domdaniel is identified as a "Christian Prophetess" and a "sibyl." She possesses deep, intuitive knowledge of past, present, and future. Her prophetic ability is rooted in connection to the Earth and the organic, life-affirming principles of the Green Revolution. She serves as key source of guidance and revelation for Dana and other Company members.

Protestant Cemetery

Burial ground for Protestants on Basse-Terre. Mary Margaret McGinty expresses desire not to be buried there because she "don't know anybody on the other side of the hill." This seemingly simple statement reflects the deep sense of community and spiritual kinship defining the series's worldview—even in death, the primary consideration is to be among one's own people, highlighting the importance of communion over abstract doctrinal differences.

Providence

Foreseeing care and guidance of God over creatures of the earth. Dana questions providence when he reflects on the "providential" arrival of rough Brume just when he is told to find him, asking "how can it be providential when there is no providence?" This paradox lies at the heart of the narrative. The world often appears godless and chaotic, yet events continually align in meaningful, seemingly guided ways. Providence acts not through overt intervention but through subtle, often unseen orchestration of events and the free actions of its agents.

Prussia

Prominent German state that in the 19th century led the movement for German unification. The series mentions the Posen district as belonging to Prussia and notes Ferdinand Lassalle's political career beginning there. Portrayed as a rising, militaristic power whose consolidation under figures like Bismarck is a major force in European power struggles.

Puca

The Púca—a spirit or creature of Irish folklore, often associated with horses and shape-shifting. Mentioned alongside "Enchanted Horses" as part of Ireland's mythic fauna. The Púca represents the wild, unpredictable dimension of the supernatural, a native Irish spirit that persists beneath the overlay of Christianity, part of the deep, ancient traditions that surface throughout the narrative.

Pure of heart

State of spiritual and moral integrity. The series suggests the "pure of heart cannot be harmed even by the presence of the Devil." But it immediately qualifies: what about those only "comparatively speaking" pure, or who have achieved purity intermittently? This introduces complex understanding of virtue. Purity is not static but a dynamic condition that must be constantly defended and often regained after failure.

Purgatorial Bark

Dana's term for the ship Catherine Dembinska after her namesake's death. Conceptualizes the vessel as a soul in Purgatory, working out its salvation through suffering and pilgrimage. The ship becomes a living entity, a "person and a spirit surviving," whose journey is continuation of Catherine's spiritual struggle. Key example of the series's blurring of lines between animate and inanimate, spiritual and material.

Purgatory

State of temporal punishment and purification after death. In the series, Purgatory is a literal place with its own geography and social order. Jude Revanche describes the "roads to Purgatory" as "overcrowded" due to high mortality of the 1848 revolutions. Nehemias Jokkebrok spends an hour in the "steep flames of Purgatory" after a near-death experience. Portrayed as a place of active, often painful transformation—a necessary stage in the soul's journey toward the "Green Heaven of the Blessed."

Pyrenees

Mountain range forming a natural border between France and Spain. Key theater of action, particularly the region around Hendaye. The domain of the Brumes and a landscape of ambush, spiritual testing, and revolutionary intrigue. Portrayed as a wild, liminal space where boundaries between nations, and between natural and supernatural worlds, are thin.

Q

Quarry Ravine

Battlefield site during the Battle of Inkerman in the Crimean War. Like other named locations (Shell Hill, Home Ridge, Kitspur), it functions less as specific geography than as constituent of a chaotic, primordial battlescape. A locus for the "animal battle-passion" characterizing the conflict, a brutal theater where "old blood lust" rises again, stripped of strategic pretense. A topographical feature within a larger, almost mythical geography of violent, instinctual struggle.

Queen

Representation of the historical Queen Isabella II of Spain (r. 1833–1868). Portrayed not as a sovereign but as symbol of the decadent, anti-traditionalist regime—the "Queen's Spain"—against which the Carlist rebellion is waged. Characterized as precocious, depraved, ruling through male favorites, embodying a power that is innovative, anti-religious, and mocking of traditional morality. Paradoxically, her government is immensely popular with half of Spain, personifying a fundamental schism in the nation's soul.

Queen's Lap

Ritualized position of honor and intimacy within the Irish domestic sphere. The lap of the woman of the house upon which a favored male guest sits to "reign over that house." Dana's occupation of cousin Aileen Dinneen's lap establishes his preeminence over her other suitors—a non-verbal declaration of favor and locus of domestic power. Both physical space and symbolic throne, representing matriarchal authority that confers status and provokes jealousy, grounding broader political and metaphysical conflicts in primal household dynamics.

Queen's Spain

The socio-political and spiritual landscape of Spain under Isabella II. The ideological antithesis to the Carlist world, characterized by innovation, anti-clericalism, and shrill mockery of tradition. While possessing remnants of "old elegances," it is fundamentally a realm of moral and political decay, a parasitic entity feeding on the nation's heritage. The series's primary antagonist environment, a state defined not by geography but by adherence to a modern, destructive, and anti-religious spirit.

Quito

Capital of Ecuador, situated in the Andes mountains. During the mid-19th century, seat of Ecuador's conservative, clerical, and land-owning interests, in profound opposition to liberal, mercantile Guayaquil. Founded on the site of an Incan capital, historically a major administrative and religious center of the Spanish empire. The "immeasurably conservative fortress in the high mountains," the "high home of the restorative vision," symbolic head of one half of Ecuador's schizophrenic national soul. Its inhabitants possess a serenity and deep-chested strength born of living in a sacred, perpetually quaking landscape.

R

Radicalism

Primary ideological force embodied by the "Red Revolution" and its adherents. Not progressive but nihilistic, seeking violent destruction of traditional order, religion, and social structures for its own sake. Personified by figures like the charcoal-burner Jude Revanche, who desires to "burn down the Carlist Hills." Theologically framed by the Black Pope as a "red parasite" bringing "death of the individual and of the corporate soul." Destruction not as means to an end but as end in itself—a metaphysical principle of entropy and negation (the "Omega way") locked in cosmic conflict with the creative "Green Revolution."

Raglan, Lord [Historical]

Biographical: FitzRoy James Henry Somerset, 1st Baron Raglan (1788–1855), British Army officer and commander of British forces in the Crimean War. His leadership was criticized for logistical failures. Most remembered for issuing the ambiguous order leading to the Charge of the Light Brigade at Balaclava. Died of dysentery during the Siege of Sebastopol.

Connection: Key actor in the Battle of Balaclava, whose decision to dispatch Captain Nolan with the cavalry order precipitates the heroic but futile Charge. His action, born of tactical misunderstanding, serves as catalyst for anachronistic heroism signaling the end of an old mode of warfare and beginning of an era of incomprehension and impersonal slaughter.

Ram

Archetypal symbol of primal, masculine, and generative power. An elemental authority whose presence must be acknowledged before certain acts, such as Tancredi Cima's love-making, can proceed. The "ram-headed snake," a figure in ancient Irish art, links this virile, pastoral symbol to a more chthonic and serpentine wisdom. A pre-Christian, telluric force integral to the deep life of the land, embodying natural order that persists within and alongside the Christian framework.

Ranker, Otis

Gold prospector from the United States who joins Dana and Charley Oceaan in Cayenne. Dour, taciturn, possessed of deep-seated fear of women. Comes to Basse-Terre from Mexico, bringing a Mexican wife named Marie Teresa; they have three children. Hears and recognizes Ifreann's ghostly warning when he begins to build his house but defies it. A quintessential American adventurer driven by simple code of honor and tangible quest for gold panned with his "own hands." Swept into the Green Revolution's metaphysical conflict, traveling to Basse-Terre and later Ecuador, where his innate loyalty leads him to become a guardian of Gabriel Moreno.

Reality

Not a stable, objective field but a malleable and contested construct. Its nature is contingent upon metaphysical allegiance, defined by one of two mutually exclusive premises: the "Alpha" statement ("there is a God") or the "Omega" statement ("there is not a God"). Scheherazade Jokkebrok possesses power to fabricate reality; Ifreann can destroy it through illusion. Reality is a participatory drama, constantly shaped, defended, and assaulted by warring cosmic principles and their human agents. The ultimate moral imperative: align actions with and defend the true, "Green," God-centered reality against its "Red," nihilistic counterfeit.

Reality vs. Illusion

Central dialectic structuring the series's epistemology and metaphysics. The boundary between real and illusory is porous and frequently violated. Ifreann is a master illusionist, generating false sensory experiences and crumpling the perceived world like paper. Events are often dreamlike, their objective occurrence subject to doubt and metaphysical manipulation. History itself is a contested narrative, subject to retroactive alteration ("to alter history before it happens, while it is happening, and even after it has happened"). Reality is a porous, multi-layered construct shaped by belief, will, and supernatural intervention.

Rebellion

The mode of resistance for characters aligned with the Green Revolution. Irish rebellion against English rule, Carlist rebellion against the Isabelline government, Federalist rebellion against centralizing Unitarians—all are manifestations of the restorative revolution. Rebellion is not mere political opposition but a sacred duty, a response to illegitimate authority and cosmic disorder. The protagonists are rebels, but their rebellion aims not at innovation but at restoration of a prior, divinely sanctioned order.

Red

The color and principle of the destructive revolution. Associated with blood, fire, and the nihilistic forces opposing the Green. The "Red Revolution" is the movement of the Devil and his agents to destroy traditional order, faith, and creation itself. Red Shirts (Garibaldi's followers), red flags, the "Red Napoleon" Charles Cosmo—all embody this principle. Red represents the "Omega" worldview: denial of God, embrace of materialism and mechanism, ultimately the triumph of death over life. The antithesis of Green in the cosmic struggle.

Red Napoleon (Charles Cosmo)

A revolutionary figure who styles himself "the Red Napoleon," the genius behind the global conspiracy of the Red Revolution. Charles Cosmo brokers and backs revolutions all over the world, manipulating governments and revolutionary movements to advance the mechanistic, atheistic agenda. His presence at gatherings "seems to indicate that he intends to achieve" dominance in that part of the world. A cousin of another Cosmo figure, "though they are not close." If certain political outcomes occur in England ("Disraeli instead of Galbritain"), "the Red Napoleon will have England in his pocket." A figure of immense intelligence and malevolence, he is the human face of the organized evil opposing the Green Revolution, the Devil's most capable human instrument.

Red Revolution

The cosmic and political movement to destroy the traditional, divinely ordered world. Led by the "Hidden Hand" of the Devil and carried out through secret societies, revolutionary movements, and intellectual subversion. Characterized by materialism, atheism, violence, and the reduction of humans to mechanisms. Appearing in different forms (Carbonari, Red Shirts, liberal revolutionaries) in different contexts. The Red Revolution is destructive, uprooting, opposed to the grain of Creation. It is the "Omega" principle made political, the organized assault on everything the Green Revolution defends.

Redemption

The possibility of spiritual restoration after fall. Dana's arc is one of repeated falls and redemptions. The Good Thief (Saint Dismas) is the patron of such restoration, demonstrating that even at the last moment, even for those defined by transgression, redemption is possible. Redemption requires genuine contrition, not merely formal confession. It is the goal of the Green Revolution on a cosmic scale—the redemption of the world from the "red blight" that corrupts it.

Religion

Not merely personal belief or institutional affiliation but the foundational orientation toward reality. True religion aligns with the "Alpha" principle, recognizing God as the ground of being and creation as good. False religion, or its negation (atheism), aligns with the "Omega" principle. The series presents religion as the most important thing, the issue upon which all others depend. The Green Revolution is fundamentally a religious movement, defending the sacred order against the desacralizing forces of modernity.

Religious order

Formal institution of religious commitment, exemplified by the "Brotherhood of the Holy Lions" which Dana joins. A higher, more structured level of dedication to Green Revolution principles than common partisanship. An ancient, elite, and exceptionally strict body, suggesting the cosmic war requires not only irregular militias but also consecrated, knightly cadres.

Renan, Ernest [Historical]

Biographical: Ernest Renan (1823–1892), French expert on Middle Eastern languages and civilizations. His Vie de Jésus (Life of Jesus, 1863) depicted Jesus as a remarkable human being but denied his divinity, causing scandal throughout Europe.

Connection: Named as one of the twelve "Devil's Disciples," modern intellectuals whose work serves the destructive "Red" principle. His inclusion identifies the historicist, rationalist critique of religion as a key ideological weapon of the enemy, undermining faith and assisting in the "shriveling" of the sacred order.

Renunciation

Faith consistently requires renunciation. This ranges from small renunciations of personal comfort to profound renunciations of safety, worldly ambition, and life itself. Dana must renounce forbidden passions and desire for peaceful life to follow his path. Faith is defined not by what one believes but by what one is willing to sacrifice for that belief. The act of renunciation is a recurring ritual purifying the agent and affirming commitment to the Green cause.

Repentance

Crucial, transformative act of re-aligning the soul with divine order after a fall from grace. Dana's moral and spiritual crisis after his affair with Elena Prado culminates in seeking confession and returning to a state of grace before facing a death-ambush. Elena herself undergoes dramatic, though possibly feigned, performance of repentance. Essential for spiritual warfare, restoring integrity necessary to continue the fight against evil.

Responsibility

The series has a radical theology of personal responsibility. The Black Pope teaches that every individual has divine mandate to "rule the entire world in the fullness of his own aspect." This collapses distinction between sacred and secular, framing all human action as cosmically significant. Protagonists feel directly "responsible to God" for the state of the world, elevating political struggles into divine service. The theme rejects passive piety in favor of active, creative, dangerous co-participation with the divine in ordering reality.

Resurrection

The boundary between life and death is consistently portrayed as permeable. The narrative is populated with figures who defy death: Ifreann Chortovitch, killed by Dana but returning; Scheherazade, claiming power to resurrect the dead. This theme blurs lines between orthodox resurrection, demonic persistence, and folkloric magic. The historical upheaval of 1848 is itself termed the "Resurrection of the Fools," suggesting resurrection can be chaotic and ambivalent—an unchaining of long-buried forces rather than simple restoration of life.

Resurrection Morning

Orthodox Christian hope for the final resurrection of the dead, invoked by Joseph O'Boyle, who wishes to be buried in Irish ground so he can rise from it on that day. Grounds the series's more bizarre explorations of life-after-death in traditional eschatological framework. Affirms an ultimate, divinely ordained conclusion to history, a final victory for which the protagonists' temporal struggles are a prelude.

Revanche, Jude (Judas)

Primary antagonist, first appearing as a squarish, black-bearded "world-man" and charcoal-burner embodying the nihilistic spirit of the Red Revolution. Motivated by abstract love of "freedom," he seeks total destruction of the Carlist world. A member of the Carbonari, aligned with Ifreann Chortovitch. After being blinded by Dana in a duel, he persists as a vengeful ghost. A revolutionary whose idealism has decayed into pure, resentful malice, embodying the sterile, self-defeating nature of the destructive principle. His name evokes both the apostle Jude and the traitor Judas, signifying his role as perverse and treacherous figure of faith.

Reverence

Proper disposition toward the sacred order of creation. Manifested by protagonists and their allies, often in unexpected contexts, as with Conde Prado's powerful recitation of grace at table. An innate recognition of "Alpha" reality, a posture of awe and respect before the divine. Contrasted sharply with the mockery, blasphemy, and cynicism of Red Revolution adherents, for whom nothing is sacred.

Revolution

Central dynamic, fundamentally redefined and bifurcated into two warring metaphysical principles. The "Green Revolution," espoused by protagonists, is a restorative and conservative uprising defending a traditional, divinely-grounded order. The "Red Revolution," driven by antagonists, is a destructive, nihilistic, demonic force seeking to uproot all tradition and impose chaos. Revolution is not mere political event but essential conflict of a cosmic civil war, a permanent struggle between forces of creation and anti-creation for control of history.

Rex Randolf

An embezzler originally of the Virginia Randolfs who flees New York for Basse-Terre after stealing thirty-two million dollars in a financial coup. He brings his family including nine daughters (the eldest eleven, youngest nine—including one named Roxana). His most frequent saying is "I owe no man an apology." He acquires an African Lion which grows "pretty big" and objects when its blood is drawn for Tom Cosquin. Randolf takes "deep thought" when he hears the clattering voice of the thunder-voiced ghost-giant. He questions Dana with "heavy derision" and doubts the existence of certain figures. Charles Cosmo eventually hangs Rex Randolf, though Dana later examines a body and determines "this is not Rex Randolf—too small." Randolf's wife eventually reports that her "husband Rex Randolf has died in a private mental hospital in upper New York State."

Richard I (the Lionheart) [Historical]

Biographical: Richard I of England (1157–1199), known as Cœur de Lion, central figure in the Third Crusade. Renowned for military prowess and chivalric reputation. Spent little time in England, focusing on continental possessions and crusading. Died from a crossbow wound besieging a French castle.

Connection: Invoked as historical archetype of the heroic warrior who practiced "Counting Coup"—the daredevil feat of riding through an enemy force. His example serves as precedent for Cardigan's Charge of the Light Brigade, framing that anachronistic valor within long tradition of chivalric warfare and highlighting its tragic finality in the new age of industrial slaughter.

Rienzi, Cola di [Historical]

Biographical: Cola di Rienzo (c. 1313–1354), Italian populist leader who as Tribune seized control of Rome in 1347. Sought to restore the ancient Roman Republic and unify Italy but was driven from power. Returned in 1354 as Senator but was killed by a mob. His life inspired Wagner's 1842 opera Rienzi.

Connection: Dana and Catherine attend a performance of the opera in Krakow. The opera's theme of populist-led, glory-haunted restoration resonates with the series's revolutionary and restorative ambitions. Rienzi is a historical parallel—a figure whose dramatic, ultimately tragic attempt to reshape history is replayed as cultural artifact within the narrative.

Righteousness

Essential quality of the Green Revolution's champions. Not sinlessness but fundamental, active alignment with divine will and creative cosmic order. A militant virtue, demonstrated through courage, loyalty to the "Alpha" principle, and willingness to engage in violent struggle against evil. What validates the protagonists' often brutal actions, framing them not as crimes but as necessary acts of holy war.

Rio Gallegos

Port city in Patagonian southern Argentina. In the mid-19th century, a remote, undeveloped outpost. Mentioned as possible landing site for Ifreann's ironclad ship Brami Piekielne for repairs after damage by the Catherine. Its remoteness and lack of facilities underscore the desperation and isolation of the demonic vessel after initial defeat.

Risorgimento

The 19th-century movement for Italian unification. Personified as a historical actor and subject of theological dispute, with main persons including Pope Pius IX and Victor Emanuele II. Not mere political process but spiritual drama, a national "the rebirth, the revival, the resurrection of Italy" whose soul is contested by competing forces of Green and Red revolutions. A key battleground in the wider European struggle between restorative tradition and destructive modernity.

Rites

Formal, prescribed actions mediating between human and supernatural. The narrative features both orthodox Catholic rites (Mass, Confession) and folk-rituals possessing their own power. The authenticity of certain rites is questioned—a priest denies the existence of formal "rites of exorcism," classifying it as legend. Efficacious rites are not limited to official liturgy but can emerge from tradition and direct spiritual conflict, embodying the fluid, often heterodox nature of sacred power in the series's world.

Ritual

Central mode of action through which events are imbued with metaphysical significance. Combat, social customs, and personal trials are frequently framed as rituals. The breaking of the stallion is ritual initiation; the seating on the "Queen's Lap" is social ritual of power; the sinking of the Catherine becomes ritual sacrifice. Jack Galopade proclaims, "I love ritual myself." By treating action as ritual, the narrative elevates plot from contingent events into structured, meaningful liturgy in the cosmic war.

Rivalry

Primary engine of narrative action on every level. Cosmic rivalry between Green and Red principles is mirrored in political rivalry between Carlists and Queen's government, and in personal rivalries between key antagonists: Dana versus Ifreann, Dana versus Jude Revanche, Tancredi versus Kemper. These conflicts are the necessary expression of a fundamentally divided reality, where opposition is not incidental but the defining structure of existence.

River Maine

River in County Kerry, Ireland, feeding Dingle Bay. Flows through central Kerry into Castlemaine Harbour at the head of Dingle Bay. Its inclusion in the opening description of the "Back Doors of the World" grounds the series's mythic geography in the authentic landscape of southwestern Ireland, the place of origin for Dana and wellspring of his elemental connection to the land.

Rivero, Jaime

Member-at-Large for the Residential Governors of Greenfields Estate on Basse-Terre. One of seven governors present at the discovery of Joseph Jolif's apparent death. Represents the established, bureaucratic order of the island. His presence highlights the inadequacy of conventional authority and perception in the face of supernatural and illusionary events orchestrated by Ifreann Chortovitch.

Rosas, Juan Manuel de [Historical]

Biographical: Juan Manuel de Rosas (1793–1877), Governor of Buenos Aires Province and de facto ruler of Argentina from 1835 to 1852. Rose to power leading a Gaucho army and established an authoritarian regime enforcing Federalist principles. Overthrown at the Battle of Caseros and exiled to England.

Connection: Complex, paradoxical figure. A "great brute" and dictator, yet aligned with the localist, traditional values of the Green Revolution. His vow to "hound to death the infidel, the blasphemer, the thief" defines him as reactionary, quasi-theological force. Dana works for him, then against him as circumstances demand. Represents the moral ambiguity of political power even in service of supposedly good causes.

Royal Blood

Claimed by Prince Dorn, signifying not earthly lineage but connection to demonic aristocracy. His "royal blood" is of Hell, not of Earth. The title asserts status within the infernal hierarchy and perceived right to rule the human world. A parody of earthly nobility, demonstrating the systematic inversion of legitimate order that characterizes the Red Revolution.

S

Sabatelli, General Damiano

Neapolitan general, member of the Company, and uncle of Charlotta Paradiso and Rosa Chastel. A "red-bearded" figure of the traditional military aristocracy who serves the Green Revolution, holding commissions in the Italian, French, and British armies—called "The Most Decorated General in the World." Shot through the head by an unidentified person at close range during a chaotic confrontation; Ifreann Chortovitch then claimed Sabatelli "was being investigated as a priest-hider and a protector of rebels" to justify subjecting his body to the "Execution for Traitors." He accepts the risks of noble combat, embodying the traditional, heroic ideal contrasted with the Red Revolution's utilitarian violence.

Sacrament

An outward sign instituting grace, central to the Catholic framework of the series. Confession, Communion, and Marriage appear as sacramental acts with real spiritual effects. The wine provided by Count Cyril is marked as "sacramental wine," its consumption distinguishing the inner circle and binding participants to their mission. Sacraments are not mere rituals but channels of divine power, essential for navigating the spiritual warfare in which all characters are engaged.

Sacrifice

The series explores conflicting models of sacrifice. The traditional, heroic ideal is embodied by figures like General Sabatelli who accept noble combat's risks. This contrasts with the Red Revolution's utilitarian model where sacrifice is a tool for ideological ends. The ultimate sacrifice is portrayed not as dying for a cause but as embodying a truth, as seen in the "scapegoat" figures of Carolina Tirana and the nations of Ireland and Poland.

Sad Nag Valley

Location in Ireland, near Aileen Maccobble's farm, where worn-out horses are left to die. Source for contestants in the "Sorriest Nag Race," a farcical event staged by John Maccobble to deceive Anglo-Irish gentry. Symbolizes Ireland's debased state under English rule—a place of discarded, broken things. Yet it also becomes a theater for Irish tricksterism, as the farcical race successfully dupes oppressors, turning a symbol of decay into one of cunning victory.

Saint

A fluid category extending beyond formal canonization. Denotes individuals embodying a profound, often paradoxical spiritual reality. Characters like Magdelena Brume ("the holy panther"), Sainte Erma (the "dumb saint"), and even morally ambiguous Elena Prado ("advising saint") are designated as such. Sainthood is not moral perfection but direct, often dangerous engagement with fundamental forces. Saints are active agents, warriors, conduits of supernatural power—their sanctity defined by function within the cosmic struggle rather than conventional piety.

Saint Cyril

Unseen, quasi-divine leader of the Green Revolution, referred to formally as Count Cyril Prasimos. A "timeless and invisible instigator and employer" communicating through dreams, messengers, and uncanny coincidences. Though he dies during the narrative, his influence continues unabated from beyond the grave. He embodies providential, aristocratic power rooted in deep history and divine sanction—the ultimate patriarch and strategist of the Green cause, counterpoint to the Devil and his son Ifreann. His "family face" is said to be shared by his kin, making Dana a physical echo of his spiritual master.

Saint Januarius

Patron saint of Naples, whose famous relic of liquefying blood is witnessed by Ercule Tirana's party at the Cathedral. The saint and his miracle represent the established, historical form of Catholic faith and power that the conspirators must navigate. Their visit to the Cardinal-Archbishop at the saint's cathedral is a necessary step in the complex political and spiritual maneuvering required to confront the Prisoner of Naples.

Saint Mark

Referenced through the "Lion Banner of St. Mark," one of the heraldic attributes Dana manifests upon becoming a Holy Lion. Links Dana to the ancient, powerful symbolism of the Venetian Republic and its patron saint. Situates his personal mission within a broader historical and spiritual tradition of militant Christianity, reinforcing his role as warrior for the Green Revolution.

Saint Peter's Island

Small island off the coast of Sardinia, site of Thorn Castle. A primary European base for the Green Revolution, a place of trysting and intrigues where the Company gathers. The conflict between "Thorn" (representing suffering and struggle of the Green cause) and "Gate" (representing hellish forces of Ifreann) is geographically manifested here. A microcosm of the larger European spiritual battlefield.

Saint Peter's Square

Location in Rome of the prophesied, perhaps illusory, murder of Pope Pius IX. Whether a true event later erased from the timeline or a "valid vision of the future," the massacre is the Red Revolution's ultimate ambition: public and grotesque destruction of the ultimate symbol of Green Revolution divine authority. The Square becomes stage for a metaphysical battle over the nature of historical reality itself.

Saint Porcus

Mock-saintly title bestowed by Charles Cosmo upon a pig during the sacrilegious "execution" of Pope Pius IX in St. Peter's Square. The pig, refusing to leave the scene, is hanged alongside the bodies of the Pope and three women. The creation of "Saint Porcus" is a grotesque parody of martyrdom and sanctification, representing the Red Revolution's programmatic inversion of all sacred order, its elevation of the base and absurd.

Saint Teresa

Invoked by the "converted" Elena Prado as patron of her new resolution to live a life of penitence. The reference to the great Spanish mystic highlights the theatricality and potential insincerity of Elena's transformation. By placing herself under the patronage of a genuine saint known for ecstatic spiritual experiences, Elena's performance of piety is rendered either profound miracle or masterful, blasphemous deception.

Sainte Erma

Mute, "moronic," and "grotesque" daughter of Jane Blaye of Hendaye. Revered as a "dumb saint" possessing non-verbal, intuitive understanding of spiritual matters. A conduit and guide, leading Dana to her mother's tavern and suffering "grotesquely and silently" when he later falls from grace. Represents a form of pure, pre-linguistic sanctity, a direct connection to the spiritual world bypassing intellect and articulacy.

Sainte Marguerite

District in Paris where Mariella Cima and her company establish their base by violently evicting the communist revolutionary Donzelle Moeras. The district's "steep roofs of Paris" become a vertical battlefield for Tancredi Cima and theater for the Green Revolution's urban guerrilla activities. The "fulcrum" from which Catherine Dembinska believes the world can be moved, a microcosm of the revolutionary struggle for Paris's soul.

Sainte-Beuve, Charles Augustin [Historical]

Biographical: Charles Augustin Sainte-Beuve (1804–1869), influential French literary critic known for his method emphasizing biographical and psychological context of an author's work.

Connection: Mentioned as one of the "twelve Devil's Disciples" undermining the Green Revolution through intellectual and cultural means. His brand of critical, psychological realism is viewed as a solvent of faith and tool of spiritually corrosive modernism.

Saints

Broad and active category of beings participating directly in events. Not distant figures of veneration but invoked, experienced, and even critiqued as characters. Aileen Dinneen imagines them enjoying eels and whisky in Heaven; Magdelena Brume complains they should get off their "fat groppe and went to work." They represent an accessible, folk-Catholic spiritual reality deeply intertwined with the material world.

Salesian Priest

Religious order founded by St. John Bosco, who is a character in the narrative. Represented by the Priest-Music-Master Monte Albani and later by young Father Manzoni on Basse-Terre. The Salesians embody a practical, joyful, artistically inclined Catholicism contrasting with more severe clericalism. Their involvement in education, music, and the direct lives of the people aligns them with the populist spirit of the Green Revolution.

Salvation

The series posits two paths to salvation corresponding to Green and Red Revolutions. The Green path is "constant conversion, the ananeosis, the renewal," a dynamic, ongoing process of aligning with divine growth. The Red path offers Gnostic, elitist salvation through destruction of the existing world and establishment of a new order based on "blood and iron." The struggle between these paths is the central drama of history, with personal salvation contingent on cosmic allegiance.

San Sebastián

Coastal city in the Basque Country of Spain, near the French border. Historically a strategically important port and focal point during the Carlist Wars. The first major town Dana reaches after leaving Hendaye. A transition point from the localized, mythic world of the Irish bays to the broader, politically charged landscape of continental Europe.

Sanctity

State often pursued and missed by characters. Old priest Croinin laments he has "missed sanctity," which was "expected of very old priests in Ireland." The series treats sanctity not as placid piety but as a hard-won, dynamic quality forged in danger and spiritual combat. True sanctity, embodied by figures like Magdelena Brume, is active, paradoxical, and often violent—directly opposed to the passive or purely moralistic virtues of conventional religion.

Sane devil

Paradoxical concept raised by Ifreann Chortovitch. He asserts a "sane devil would be almost unpleasant" and that madness is intrinsic to demonic nature. This reflects the broader theme that sanity is not necessarily a virtue. The "insanity" of the demonic is a source of creative and destructive power. A "sane devil" would lack this dynamic, world-altering energy—a contradiction in terms.

Sanity vs. Madness

This theme interrogates conventional definitions, often inverting them. The established world suffers from a dull, rationalistic madness, while characters who are ostensibly "mad"—Scheherazade Jokkebrok, the visionary inhabitants of Basse-Terre—possess a deeper, more creative sanity. True sanity aligns with the "green-growing" principle and can appear chaotic, while the "madness" of the Red Revolution is a sterile, logical, destructive force. A certain form of inspired madness is necessary to perceive and act within a world governed by supernatural logic.

Santa Cruz River

River in southern Argentina. Its "double estuary" with the Chico River is mentioned as a potential repair site for the damaged Brami Piekielne. Grounds the fantastical naval conflict in specific, if remote, geography, marking the southern reach of the operational theater for the spiritual-political conflict in South America.

Scapegoat

A figure who bears the sins and sufferings of others. Ireland and Poland are designated as "scapegoat nations," their role "to suffer sufficiently to save the other nations from their sins and woes." Carolina Tirana becomes a personal scapegoat, embracing a quasi-demonic condition (becoming "salt of brimstone") for paradoxical redemption. The scapegoat concept is central to the series's theology of sacrifice and vicarious suffering.

Sea

A realm of adventure, danger, and spiritual testing. The protagonists are perpetually at sea, crossing between continents and confronting both natural and supernatural threats. The sea can be blessed (Océan Ouest) or cursed (Océan Affreux). Sea battles are among the most intense conflicts of the narrative. The "Gates of Hell" is a ship; the "Third Babylon" is a "City in the Sea." The sea is a liminal space where the boundaries between worlds grow thin and cosmic forces clash directly.

Sebastopol

Major port city and naval base on the Crimean Peninsula. During the Crimean War (1853–1856), the primary objective of Allied forces. The series gives detailed, if stylized, account of the Siege of Sebastopol, including battles of Alma, Balaclava, and Inkerman. Central stage for exploring 19th-century warfare, the clash of old and new military technologies and ideologies, and the "Counting Coup" of the Light Brigade.

Second Jesus Christ

Title given by Italian people to the "New Garibaldi" after his seemingly miraculous, bloodless unification of Southern Italy. Highlights the popular tendency to elevate political figures to messianic status. The narrative presents this as dangerous political idolatry, confusing a flawed human agent with a divine savior and masking the complex, often amoral machinations that produced the "Glorious Unification."

Secret Societies

Covert organizations such as the Carbonari, Freemasons, and "Synagogue of Satan," identified as primary vehicles for the "Devil Unchained." A "jungle of organizations" through which evil acts, using human agents as "puppets." They represent a conspiratorial view of history, where major events are driven by hidden, malevolent forces working through networks of secret allegiances and ideologies.

Secularism

A core component of the Red Revolution's ideology. The Red forces are motivated by a "totally atheistic and totally cruel premise," a mechanistic worldview rejecting the sacred. Governor Guerchin on Basse-Terre identifies as an atheist. Explored as a spiritual sickness, a "withering thing" leading to nihilism and lust for destruction, in stark contrast to the sacralizing, life-affirming vision of the Green Revolution.

Seduction of the Saints

According to scholar Yosef Paradosis, the "only theme of high art"—the temptation of virtuous individuals, particularly by "flesh phantoms." Dana's fall as a Holy Lion at Thorn Castle is a prime example. The seduction is not merely carnal but a profound spiritual trial, a fall from grace with cosmic repercussions. It posits that the most significant battles are fought within the souls of the elect.

Seeress

A woman with prophetic or intuitive powers. The narrative features several such figures, including Scheherazade Jokkebrok (Dana's "favorite seeress") and Angelene Domdaniel. Not passive oracles but active shapers of destiny, whose visions and "creative humor" can alter reality. The seeress embodies a form of feminine, non-rational wisdom crucial to the Green Revolution's operations.

Serpent

Recurring symbol of both good and evil. Catherine Dembinska identifies herself and Ifreann as two serpents "involved in an apocalyptical conflict for the Good World." Elena Prado embodies the seductive, chthonic "brown-skin snake." The primal serpent of Genesis is depicted in Jane Blaye's shrine being crushed by the Virgin Mary. The serpent is primal knowledge, temptation, and transformative power—a dualistic force that can poison or redeem depending on its alignment.

Serrano, Marshal [Historical]

Biographical: Francisco Serrano y Domínguez (1810–1885), Spanish marshal and politician who played a key role in overthrowing Queen Isabella II in 1868 and subsequently served as Regent of Spain.

Connection: Depicted as one of the "finest and most ambitious of the lieutenants of Charles Cosmo the Red Napoleon." His historical actions—overthrowing the monarchy and becoming Regent—are re-contextualized as part of the Red Napoleon's global plan for destabilization.

Seven Heads Castle

Castle on the Seven Heads Promontory in County Cork, Ireland, which becomes Dana's Irish home. An ancient, legendary site, a center for Gypsy smuggling, and home to the sea monster Hiodra. Primary base for the Green Revolution's Irish operations and nexus of supernatural and political activity. A place where myth and reality converge, a "haunted" space embodying the deep, complex, violent history of the "Spanish Coast" of Ireland.

Sheol

The underworld or a "wrong place that should not be, and is." Identified with Jewish Sheol, Gentile Hades, Irish Ifreann, and the psychological Subconscious/Unconscious. Not merely a state but a "place and a substance" that can overlay worldly regions and be entered through specific doors, such as the one in Ifreann Chortovitch's Paris house. The personal and collective underworld, repository of humanity's "dirtiest thoughts" and demonic potential.

Shrine

Place of concentrated spiritual power, often of personal and heterodox nature. The most prominent example is the "angry shrine" in Jane Blaye's quarters, containing a statue of the Virgin Mary exhibiting "murderous glee," a painting of Christ coming through a wall, and the skull of her murdered husband. A shrine in the series is not passive veneration but an active site of communion with powerful, often dangerous spiritual forces.

Sibyl

Prophetic woman. The term describes Angelene Domdaniel, a "Christian Prophetess, a sabla or wise woman." Situates her within a classical tradition of female seers, but her powers are rooted in Catholic faith, chthonic connection to the earth ("out of the Earth"), and intuitive wisdom. She embodies a syncretic spirituality drawing on multiple traditions.

Sin

Not simple transgression of rules but profound spiritual condition, a misalignment with the "Green" principle of divine growth. The "ultimate sin" is "the trivialization of ghastly sin," making evil seem commonplace. Dana falls through seduction (the "Green Thorn"), betraying his sacred role as Holy Lion and resulting in his "fall." Redemption is possible but requires violent re-engagement with the spiritual battle.

Sinigaglia

Town in Italy, home of the Ferretti family from which Pope Pius IX came. Also noted for a Congrega d'Inferno (Congregation of Hell), placing it on the map of the secret war between Green and Red Revolutions.

Skibbereen

Town in County Cork, Ireland. Location of O'Boyle's Land-and-Ocean Travelers Inn (formerly Skibbereen Castle). A secondary hub of Green Revolution activity in Ireland, complementing Dana's base at Seven Heads Castle. A center for both practical resistance and nurturing a new generation of Irish "apostles."

Skibbereen Castle

A castle in Ireland, part of the network of strongholds and properties connected to Dana's circle. Located in the Skibbereen area of County Cork in the south of Ireland, a region historically associated with the Great Famine and resistance to British rule.

Slavery/Oppression

Historical oppression of Ireland by England is a central theme, detailed through the litany of Penal Laws and economic strangulation leading to the Great Famine. Contrasted with other forms of bondage like the contrato system for African labor in South America. Catherine Dembinska identifies Italy, Poland, and Ireland as the "three slave nations." The Green Revolution is fundamentally a struggle against this multi-faceted oppression, aiming to restore not just political freedom but spiritual and cultural autonomy.

Soul

The immortal, individual core of a person, distinct from the more fluid "spirit." The soul's fate—salvation or damnation—is the ultimate stake of the conflict. Ifreann seeks not Dana's death but his "death and damnation." The soul can be wounded, corrupted, or restored; its condition determines eternal destiny. The Green Revolution fights for souls as much as for territories or governments. The world's soul (its fundamental spiritual orientation) is what the cosmic struggle is ultimately about.

South America

A major theater of the series's action, encompassing Argentina, Uruguay, Chile, Ecuador, and Brazil. The continent is divided between Federalist/conservative and Unitarian/liberal forces, a local expression of the Green/Red struggle. Key figures include Rosas in Argentina, Garcia Moreno in Ecuador. South America is a place of raw, elemental power—the "loins and entrails"—where the conflicts of the Old World are replayed in new forms and where the protagonists find both allies and enemies.

Spain

A nation divided by the Carlist Wars, one of the primary theaters of the Green Revolution's struggle. "Queen's Spain" is the decadent, liberal, anti-clerical regime of Isabella II; the Carlist highlands represent traditionalist, Catholic resistance. Spain is the "soul" of the conflict in a particular way—passionate, violent, its political divisions reflecting deeper spiritual schisms. Dana enters Spain as his first major mission, and it remains central throughout the narrative.

Spanish Bay

An alternate and older name for Bantry Bay in Ireland. Signifies long-standing historical and commercial ties between southwest Ireland and Spain. Reinforces the concept of the "Back Doors of the World" and establishes the region's identity as outward-looking, deeply connected to continental Europe, rather than merely an isolated periphery of the British Isles.

Spanish hills

General term for the mountainous regions of northern Spain, particularly the Basque country and Navarre—heartlands of the Carlist movement. The domain of the Green Revolution's traditionalist forces. A landscape of rugged faith, guerrilla warfare, and deep-rooted local loyalties, representing a bastion of resistance against the centralizing and secularizing forces of the modern world.

Spirit

The non-physical essence of a person or place, distinguished from the soul (the individual, eternal core). Ghosts are frequently referred to as spirits. Places also have spirits—the "happy spirit of the island" of Basse-Terre or the "uncanny spirit on the hills" in Ireland. An animistic worldview posits a world permeated by conscious, non-material forces interacting with and influencing characters.

Spirit of the fountain pool

The animating entity of a roadside spring in Italy that speaks to Dana after his fall as Holy Lion. Its words, "May the brightness of the saints return to you," represent a moment of grace and potential redemption offered by the natural world itself. Exemplifies the series's animistic theology, where sanctity resides not only in consecrated persons or places but in the living landscape.

Stallion

Dana battles a "devil-beast" stallion on Basse-Terre, a creature of supernatural malevolence that must be broken through violent confrontation. The battle with the stallion is a ritual initiation, testing Dana's capacity to dominate primal, chaotic forces. The horse in general is associated with nobility and martial power; this particular stallion is its dark inversion, a demonic beast embodying untamed, destructive energy.

Stigmata

The supernatural appearance of Christ's wounds on a person's body. Ifreann Chortovitch claims a demonic inversion: "Mine is not the stigmata of the five wounds but of the thirteen devil wounds." This blasphemous parody is part of his claim to be an anti-Christ, a caricature who "exceeds the original in every way."

Stoker, Bram [Historical]

Biographical: Bram Stoker (1847–1912), Irish author best known for his Gothic horror novel Dracula (1897).

Connection: Mentioned as a key writer of the Irish literary revival, noted for his "excellent ghost stories" and "Creature Tales." His inclusion places him within the tradition of Irish storytelling exploring the supernatural and monstrous, a tradition to which Lafferty's own narrative belongs.

Strauss, David Friedrich [Historical]

Biographical: David Friedrich Strauss (1808–1874), German liberal Protestant theologian whose Das Leben Jesu (The Life of Jesus) caused major controversy by arguing the New Testament was largely mythical.

Connection: Named as one of the "twelve Devil's Disciples." His critical, demythologizing approach to Scripture is framed as a key intellectual weapon of the Red Revolution, an attack on the foundations of faith serving the "obstructing blight's" goal of desacralizing the world.

Subconscious

One of the modern, "Doctor's" names for the personal underworld (Sheol, Ifreann). The series dismisses it as an inadequate and misunderstood term, stating those who use it understand the reality "even less" than those who used older terms. The series rejects purely psychological interpretation of this inner realm, insisting it is a real "place and a substance," not merely a state of mind.

Suffering

A fundamental, often necessary aspect of existence, particularly for nations designated as "scapegoats" like Ireland and Poland. Their role is "to suffer sufficiently to save the other nations from their sins and woes." While the Red Revolution seeks to eliminate suffering through violent reordering, the Green Revolution accepts it as part of the spiritual struggle, a path to redemption and mark of sanctity.

Sun

Dana is identified as a "sun child" who is "burned lighter by the sun." In battle he grinds the "false snake itself that he ground under his heels," echoing solar myths of heroes defeating chthonic monsters. The sun is a principle of clarity, divine power, and masculine, heroic energy.

Supernatural

The fundamental operating principle of the series. Ghosts, demons, saints, and mythological creatures are active agents in historical and political events. Reality is fluid, with porous boundaries between life and death, sanity and madness, physical and spiritual realms. Supernatural occurrences—prophetic dreams, bilocation, magical transformations—are treated as objective facts, demanding the reader accept a worldview where history is shaped by metaphysical forces.

Swiss Guards

The papal guard, whose historical duty is to protect the Pope and Vatican. In the series's account of Rome's fall, five Swiss Guards are killed in the seizure of the Vatican Palace by King Victor Emanuele's troops. Their deaths represent violent overthrow of the old papal order and physical defeat of the Church's temporal power.

Symbolism

The narrative acts on a principle of radical symbolism, where objects, characters, and events are simultaneously literal and allegorical. A ship is also a soul in Purgatory; a political conflict is also a theological war; a person is also a mask of divine or demonic force. Characters constantly interpret this symbolic layer, from reading the "puns" in Celeste's weaving to understanding Green Revolution "code names." The world is presented as a text to be deciphered, a web of interconnected meanings.

Syncretism

The model of heroism in the series is profoundly syncretic, blending elements from disparate traditions: Irish mythology, Catholic hagiography, classical epic, and folk magic. A hero like Dana embodies qualities of the curadh (Irish champion), a Christian knight, and a trickster figure. This suggests true heroism in the modern world requires drawing on a wide range of spiritual and cultural sources to combat multifaceted, modern evil.

T

Taboo/Forbiddenness

Primary catalyst for heroic action and spiritual trial. Dana's departure from Ireland is a flight from a "forbidden" passion for his cousin Aileen Dinneen, an illicit attraction necessitating exile. This is contextualized within heroic literary tradition where great heroes are compelled to leave home due to a "too close love." The "forbidden degree" of kinship serves not merely as societal constraint but as metaphysical trigger for the hero's journey. The ultimate taboo is alignment with the absolute evil of the Red Revolution, a transgression constituting spiritual self-annihilation.

Talbot, William [Historical]

Biographical: William Henry Fox Talbot (1800–1877), English scientist and key pioneer of photography. Inventor of salted paper and calotype processes, foundational to photography as a reproducible medium.

Connection: Mentioned as having made "smashing improvements" to photography in 1840, following Daguerre's initial work. Cited alongside F. Scott Archer (1851) to historically situate the series's exploration of perception, reality, and the "pristine ugliness" revealed by the new medium.

Talking Ravens

Supernatural entities functioning as messengers, guides, and agents of cryptic providence. A raven appears to Dana and the Cimas in Sardinia, speaking Polish and offering gnomic advice. Later, in Poland, another raven acts as Dana's guide in his final hunt for Ifreann Chortovitch. In Ireland, a raven brings warning about the peril of John Andrew Maccobble and Aileen Dinneen. Not merely intelligent animals but totemic beings operating at the intersection of natural and supernatural worlds, aligned with the Green Revolution. They represent wild, pre-modern wisdom, serving as conduits for crucial information and aid.

Tambo inn

Post-house in the high Andes of Ecuador, at Pillaro beyond Ambato. Described as having the "lean sharp flavor" of a religious foundation. A point of stability and refuge amidst the physically and metaphysically turbulent mountain landscape. A narrative nexus where travelers converge, exchanging theological and political discourse. Also a site of supernatural intrusion, where Dana is psychically assaulted by Ifreann Chortovitch's ghost. A liminal space, a worldly sanctuary on the threshold of a spiritual war zone.

Tara [Historical]

The Hill of Tara in County Meath, Ireland—ancient ceremonial and political seat of the High Kings from pagan times until the 11th century. By the mid-19th century, it existed primarily as a powerful symbol in the nationalist imagination, evoking a lost golden age of Gaelic sovereignty. Referenced through an epigraph from Thomas Moore ("No more for Chieftains and Ladies bright / The Harp of Tara wells"), framing contemporary Irish subjugation against the memory of former glory.

Tares

Allusion to the Parable of the Tares (Matthew 13:24-30), where an enemy sows tares (zizania in the Vulgate) among a farmer's wheat. The Black Pope employs this allegory to frame the central cosmic struggle. The "green thing growing forever" of the Green Revolution is the good seed; the "red blight"—the parasitic, destructive Red Revolution—is the tares sown by the Enemy. The conflict is not symmetrical opposition between equivalent forces but contamination of primordial good by parasitic, negating evil. The duty: to "extirpate by prevention," safeguarding the original good crop.

Tchernaya Valley

Valley surrounding the Tchernaya River in Crimea, southeast of Sebastopol. A key strategic area during the Crimean War. Mentioned as one of the sites of the Battle of Inkerman (November 5, 1854). Its inclusion adds historical verisimilitude to the Crimean War sections of the narrative.

Temptation

Primary mechanism through which evil acts, targeting the will and spirit rather than physical body alone. Evil is personified in Ifreann Chortovitch and Elena Prado, institutionalized in the Red Revolution. Temptation is multifaceted: Ifreann tempts with power, shared damnation, and intellectual despair, playing on victims' weaknesses and pride. Elena embodies carnal and philosophical temptation, offering a coherent "Omega" worldview that threatens to consume the soul. Treated as ongoing spiritual warfare, a series of tests where succumbing results in fundamental loss of self and alignment with destructive cosmic principle.

Teresa

Mentioned by Mariella Cima in the exclamation "By God and Mary and Teresa." Her inclusion in this trinity suggests status comparable to the Virgin Mary within regional piety. Represents an earthy, powerful, distinctly female aspect of the holy, a patroness whose authority is immediate and deeply felt. A symbol of the series's syncretic, non-doctrinal Catholicism incorporating local and personal saints.

Teresa of Ávila, Saint [Historical]

Biographical: Teresa of Ávila (1515–1582), Spanish Carmelite nun, mystic, author, and reformer. A central Counter-Reformation figure whose works on mystical prayer (The Interior Castle, The Way of Perfection) are seminal texts in Christian spirituality.

Connection: Elena Prado blasphemously invokes St. Teresa alongside St. Catherine of Siena to support her claims of carnal, physical union with Christ. She urges Dana to "Read what they really wrote about their encounters," twisting mystical ecstasy into evidence for her "Omega" worldview—a deliberate co-opting and perversion of orthodox mystical theology.

Terrestrial Paradise

The island of Basse-Terre in French Guadeloupe, physical location of the Terrestrial Paradise. The narrative gives precise geographical coordinates, claiming they match those of the Earthly Paradise in an ancient chronicle. Not a place of idyllic stasis but a microcosm of the world, containing all its flora, fauna, and human diversity. The central stage for the cosmic conflict between Green and Red Revolutions, home to both Greenfields estate and the demonic stronghold of Thieves' Town. A new Eden where consequences of a new Fall, or possibility of a renewed world, are contested.

Terrestrial Paradise Weekly

Newspaper founded on Basse-Terre by the consumptive American journalist Mary Margaret McGinty. The title embodies a hopeful, almost utopian project to articulate and chronicle Green Revolution principles as manifested in the island's "Terrestrial Paradise." Though the foundress dies before the first issue, Angelene Domdaniel and Mary Esfinga Lancel vow to continue publication, symbolizing persistence of the "green" word and necessity of creating a new narrative to counter destructive ideologies.

Test oath

The series cites the historical "test oath" of the Orange Society in Ireland, allegedly requiring members to swear to "exterminate all the Catholics." A historical anchor grounding the series's depiction of sectarian conflict in documented institutionalized hatred, providing context for deep-seated historical grievances fueling the Irish dimension.

The dislocated ocean

Metaphysical and psychological space experienced by Dana and Jack Galopade following their battle with Ifreann's ship. An "untimely ocean," a chaotic, primordial realm outside conventional space-time where they exist as "sea monsters." A profound psychic fragmentation resulting from direct encounter with cosmic evil. Scheherazade Jokkebrok claims this ocean is part of her created world from which she must rescue them. A symbol of the soul's perilous journey through the abyss of un-creation.

The Lion has Fallen

Dirge and declaration, tolled by the supernatural bells of Thorn Castle to announce Dana's spiritual fall. Signifies a critical defeat for the Green Revolution: the capitulation of its chosen champion to temptation ("the thorn in the flesh"). Not a private moral failure but a cosmic event with immediate consequences, demonstrating the precariousness of virtue and high stakes of spiritual warfare. Marks Dana's transition from "Holy Lion" to the compromised "Thorned Lion."

The Roman Thing

Used by the ghost of Count Vantour, referring to a grand historical project coming to an end. Symbolizes the decadent, imperial tradition of Western civilization rooted in Roman ideals of power and order. Its impending collapse signifies a major historical discontinuity, clearing the way for a new, demonic order. The end of "The Roman Thing" marks a shift from established, albeit corrupt, structures to pure, elemental conflict.

The Twelve Apostles

The eleven (later twelve) orphaned or abandoned boys sheltered by Joseph and Charlotta O'Boyle in secret caves beneath Skibbereen Castle. Including figures like Lion Mulville and Patrick Daniel Christopher, they become a nascent revolutionary cell, a new generation of disciples for the Green cause in Ireland. Portrayed as possessing intuitive, almost prophetic understanding of the ongoing spiritual conflict. They represent organic, grassroots formation of the "Company," embodying resilience and future hope of Irish resistance.

The Underworld

Both psychological state and tangible place. Given numerous names from different traditions: Sheol (Jewish), Hades/Kolasis (Gentile), Infernum (Vulgate), Ifreann (Irish), Océan Affreux (Charley Oceaan), Sotterraneo (Tancredi Cima), Luecke (Kemper Gruenland), and the Unconscious (modern psychology). The narrative insists on its status as "a place and a substance" that can be entered through physical portals. The source of Ifreann Chortovitch's power and home base of the Red Revolution, a realm of anti-creation and spiritual death.

Theology

The series fuses elements of folk Catholicism, Irish mythology, Gnostic dualism, and Lafferty's idiosyncratic metaphysical system. Its central axiom is cosmic conflict between two opposed principles embodied in two "revolutions": the Green, representing life-affirming, creative, divinely rooted order; and the Red, a parasitic, destructive, demonic force. This theology posits a world where the supernatural is immanent, intervening directly through angels, devils, ghosts, and totemic beings. History is not linear progression but malleable text, and reality itself a "mish-mash" of conflicting, co-existing planes.

Theophany

Direct manifestation of the divine is presented as ongoing reality, not confined to the distant past. The earth of the high Andes is speaking "as it had spoken at Sinai and Olympus, at Ararat and at Athos." Not metaphorical but literal theophany, an eruption of the sacred into the material world. This immanence of the divine is core to the Green Revolution's worldview, contrasting with the sterile materialism of its Red adversary.

Thiers, Louis [Historical]
